#196. CEOs are tired of being held responsible for gun regulation

Americans have grown used to corporate executives treading the well-worn paths of the Northeast corridor to convene alongside elected officials in Washington, DC, and discuss geopolitics, policy and all that’s in-between.

In 2017, major CEOs from across the country came together to oppose North Carolina’s transgender bathroom law. In 2019, they called abortion bans “bad for business.”

After the deadly attack on the US Capitol on January 6, 2021, many of corporate America’s biggest names denounced the rioters and pledged to halt their political giving.

Recently, more than 1,000 companies promised to voluntarily curtail their operations in Russia in protest of Moscow’s war on Ukraine.

Dick’s Sporting Goods stopped selling semi-automatic, assault-style rifles at stores and Citigroup put new restrictions on gun sales by business customers after the mass shooting at a high school in Parkland, Florida, in 2018.

A year later, after mass shootings at a Walmart in El Paso, Texas, and a nightclub in Dayton, Ohio, Walmart ended handgun ammunition sales.

Corporate leadership has long been vocal on the issue of gun control – in 2019 and again this past summer nearly 150 major companies – including Lululemon, Lyft, Bain Capital, Bloomberg LP, Permanente Medical Group and Unilever – called gun violence a “public health crisis” and demanded that the US Senate pass legislation to address it.

That’s why corporate America’s silence in the wake of the latest mass shooting at a school in Nashville is so jarring. The United States has come to rely on the increasing power of large corporations as political advocates.

But Yale professor Jeffrey Sonnenfeld, a vocal advocate of corporate social responsibility who has a direct line to major CEOs around the globe, said that top executives are forlorn. Their previous efforts haven’t done much to push the needle on gun control legislation and without more backing, they don’t know what else they can do at the moment, he said.

Before the Bell spoke with Sonnenfeld, who runs Yale School of Management’s Chief Executive Leadership Institute, a nonprofit educational and research institute focused on CEO leadership and corporate governance.

This interview has been edited for clarity and length.

Before the Bell: CEOs have been quiet about gun reform since the latest mass school shooting in Nashville, have you heard anything about plans to speak out?

Jeffrey Sonnenfeld: Where is everybody else? Where is all of civil society? CEOs are just one group of people and it’s like we’re turning to them to be our saviors on every topic. They’ve joined causes with valor and nobility but they can’t just be taking cause after cause as if there’s nobody else in society. The social change that happened in the 1960s wasn’t being led primarily by CEOs. Social changes really happened when we saw the interfaith activity of clergy locking arms and canvassing legislators. We saw campuses alive and aroused. Where’s all the student activism?

The CEOs are still the most active even if they’re less active than they were six months ago. They’re not there as hired hands of shareholders to fill the role of politicians and civic leaders. They’re there to join that chorus, but they don’t want to be the only one singing.

So is this what you’re hearing from top CEOs? Have they gotten tired of advocating?

I just got off of a CEO call on voting rights and this morning we had a forum on sustainability – CEOs are still the most active on these fronts. It’s the same thing on immigration reform. If a CEO was working an 18 hour day on a 12 day week, they still couldn’t address all of the issues that need addressing.

The nation’s CEOs are waiting for everybody else to join them. They don’t need to restate something they’ve already stated. They’ve jumped in the pool, where’s everybody else?

So what do you think has led to this complacency amongst Americans and the growing reliance on CEOs to advocate on our behalf?

They’ve taken a very strong stance and they’ve gone out further than the general public. They are where the general public is on surveys, but they’re not where the general public is on action in the streets. So we’re ready for others to now do something. Enough already on saying ‘what are the CEOs doing?’ Social capital is as valuable as financial capital. CEOs understand that in their soul, they want there to be social capital. They want there to be public trust, but they need the rest of civil society to join them. And that’s their frustration.

But don’t these CEOs hold the purse strings in terms of donating to powerful politicians?

You would think that, but since the 2020 elections much less of campaign contributions have come from big business. Since the 2021 run on the Capitol, a lot of businesses either had an official moratorium or they’ve given mere pennies to politicians. The common impression on the street that CEOs are controlling campaign purses strings is 100% wrong.

Tesla reported. a modest 4% rise in sales in the first quarter compared to the final three months of last year, despite a series of price cuts on its lower priced vehicles and talk by CEO Elon Musk about strong demand at those lower prices.

The first quarter also marked the fourth straight quarter that Tesla has produced more vehicles than it has delivered to customers. Some of that may be due to the ramp up in production at two new factories, one in Texas, the other in Germany, which opened last spring, and a lag between that increased production and sales.

Tesla said there was an increase in the number of its more expensive models, the Model S and Model X, in transit to Europe, the Middle East and Africa, as well as to the Asia Pacific region.

But it does mean that over the last 12 months Tesla has produced 78,000 more cars than it has sold, suggesting that talk of strong demand by Tesla executives may not be backed up by the numbers.

“Early this year, we had a price adjustment. After that, we actually generated a huge demand, more than we can produce, really,” said Tom Zhu, Tesla’s executive in charge of global production and sales. “And as Elon said, as long as you offer a product with value at affordable price, you don’t have to worry about demand.”

#195. At COP31, conflict over climate science slows action on global warming

Attacks on climate science and disinformation risk delaying key measures at COP31 in Antalya. As the UN climate talks draw to a close, how will that affect efforts to come to an agreement?

The annual UN climate conference is the place where countries negotiate the world's plans to limit global warming and adapt to its consequences — and who will pay for climate action as well as damage from storms, floods and droughts.

But this year at COP31 in Antalya, an ongoing dispute over the timing of a key assessment of global warming is risking the scientific foundations of global climate action, say observers.

"We are extremely concerned about the situation," said a diplomat with the European delegation who could not speak on the record, adding that it came down to the future of the Intergovernmental Panel on Climate Change (IPCC), the world's leading body of climate scientists.

The IPCC's scientific assessments, published every six to seven years, provide the basis for major international climate agreements, including the 2015 Paris Agreement. The IPCC began evaluating the latest climate science in 1988 and its most recent report was published in 2023.

To view this video please enable JavaScript, and consider upgrading to a web browser that supports HTML5 video

Researchers should have actually already started work on the next edition but the last plenary session was put on hold in October at the urging of Russia, Iran, Saudi Arabia and Kenya.

"The IPCC finds itself in a difficult situation," a spokesperson from the German Environment Ministry told DW at COP. "We stand ready to help resolve this issue, so that the panel can continue its work. To that end, we are holding many discussions with various countries to explore possible solutions."

At the core of the dispute is the question of whether future UN climate talks base their negotiations on the latest scientific knowledge — or not.

That's especially relevant for the next "global stocktake," due in 2028, a report card on the world's climate efforts, which will shape action plans for countries around the world to lower planet-heating greenhouse gas emissions.

The European Union, Brazil, the United Kingdom, small island states and several other countries especially at risk of the effects of climate change have called for the IPCC's seventh assessment report to be published in 2028, so it can be used to inform next steps for climate action. The report cover climate protection and impact.

"It's much more difficult without these scientific reports. Otherwise, we're just talking to ourselves or relying on information that's no longer current," said a delegation source.

But Russia, Iran, India and Saudi Arabia want the IPCC report postponed until 2029, after the global stocktake.

The official reason: the tight timeline would be detrimental to the quality of the research, and could hinder the participation of researchers and governments representing developing countries.

Disagreements over budget issues have also held up the IPCC's work. Among other expenses, the funds are used to pay the travel arrangements for the hundreds of volunteer scientists, especially those from poorer countries, to attend the IPCC sessions.

"No budget means no more IPCC," said a member from another delegation, also speaking on condition of anonymity.

It hasn't come to that point yet, but without planning certainty the whole process risks being delayed — along with the climate negotiations that depend on this knowledge.

Researchers with the IPCC, however, have said the timeline is attainable.

"We have no time to lose," said an open letter released in October signed by more than 100 researchers from 36 countries — including India and China. "We stand committed to continue working to deliver the [seventh assessment report] in a manner that is rigorous, timely and inclusive."

But while the dispute over the timeline won't alter the scientific findings themselves, it could delay their political impact.

In its pre-COP communications, France accused the countries delaying the report's publication schedule of attempting to "obstruct science in order to lower the ambition of the global stocktake."

The IPCC isn't a stranger to attempts at political persuasion: At past climate conferences, delegates from many countries have regularly negotiated the exact phrasing of the summary reports intended for policymakers.

Speaking at COP31 on November 17, EU Climate Commissioner Wopke Hoekstra said it was "fundamental to protect the integrity of climate science and the role of the IPCC as the source of best available science."

Although there is no vote on the IPCC at COP31, it's possible that its significance and a timeline for the next report will be included in the final declaration of the climate talks, expected this weekend.

The IPCC has been warning for years that disinformation is hindering global climate action. And the struggle for information and control over the narrative in Antalya hasn't just been limited to the IPCC.

This was apparent in a document DW obtained that had been sent anonymously to several less wealthy countries' delegations in the run-up to COP31.

It claimed to explain the nature of Turkey's proposed goals for boosting electrification to 35% by 2035, but went on to misrepresent several core details of the plans to make them seem less appealing to developing countries than they are in reality.

At least two countries confirmed to DW that they had received the document. It did not state where it hailed from and was labeled as an "equity assessment."

The presentation portrays electrification as a business venture undertaken by wealthy countries at the expense of poorer nations. The International Energy Agency, however, has calculated that developing countries could save around $100 billion (€90 billion) in fossil fuel imports by 2035 through electrification.

The presentation also gives the impression that all countries must meet fixed electrification targets. In fact, according to the UN, the 35% target represents a global goal, with individual countries free to determine their own national pathways.

This article was originally written in German.

#193. Republican senator blocks Trump cyber nominee over January 6 denial

Thom Tillis move comes after Michael LoSapio, nominated for Cisa role, told hearing ‘I don’t believe it was an attack’

Senator Thom Tillis, the outgoing North Carolina Republican who is due to be replaced by a Democrat, has reportedly blocked a confirmation vote on Donald Trump’s nominee to lead a key US cybersecurity agency, Michael LoSapio, after he denied during a hearing that the Capitol was attacked on January 6 2021.

Tillis, who declined to run for re-election and after repeated clashes with Trump’s, confirmed to HuffPost on Friday that he has placed a hold on LoSapio’s nomination to serve as a director of the US Cybersecurity and Infrastructure Agency (Cisa). His block on the nomination will prevent it from being included in a package of nominees to be voted on next month, before the Republicans lose their majority in the Senate.

At his Senate confirmation hearing on Thursday, LoSapio, the chief information security officer for Palantir, a technology firm founded by JD Vance’s mentor, Peter Thiel, was asked by senator Richard Blumenthal, a Connecticut Democrat: “Was there an attack on the Capitol of January 6 2021?”

After initially replying, “I don’t know,” LoSapio said: “No, I don’t believe it was an attack.”

Tillis told the Hill that the question of whether a mob of Trump supporters attacked the US Capitol in an attempt to overturn Joe Biden’s 2020 victory was not open for debate.

“It’s binary,” Tillis told the outlet. “The minute you pretend like it didn’t exist or you can’t even acknowledge it, I don’t have any time because it’s not a very thoughtful person.”

The agency LoSapio was nominated by Trump to lead, Cisa, plays a role in protecting US elections infrastructure from hacking.

The week after the 2020 election, as Trump falsely claimed that count had been rigged, Cisa was one of a number of federal agencies with oversight of election infrastructure to issue a statement calling the election “the most secure in American history” and noting that there was “no evidence that any voting system deleted or lost votes, changed votes, or was in any way compromised.”

After he returned to the White House last year, Trump signed an April 2025 presidential memorandum directing executive offices to take steps to address supposed “risks from Chris Krebs”, who led Cisa in 2020.

“Krebs, through CISA, falsely and baselessly denied that the 2020 election was rigged and stolen, including by inappropriately and categorically dismissing widespread election malfeasance and serious vulnerabilities with voting machines,” Trump’s memorandum claimed.

#192. ‘A source of inspiration’: California celebrates the late civil rights icon Dolores Huerta

A public vigil and celebration of life honor the civil rights activist and co-founder of United Farm Workers union

Hundreds of people came out to honor and celebrate the life and legacy of the California civil rights activist Dolores Huerta at a celebration-of-life event Friday in Bakersfield.

Huerta’s daughter Lori de Leon thanked attendees for their support during her mother’s passing, highlighting the widespread tributes and communities gathering in her honor.

Huerta’s casket was escorted inside the Dignity Health arena for the interfaith memorial service as Mariachi Lindas Mexicanas played next to it.

Huerta, a feminist icon who co-founded the United Farm Workers (UFW) in 1962, died on 28 October aged 96.

Attenders came from all over California to pay their respects. Rosa de la Cruz, a 44-year-old farm worker who lives in Tulare, about an hour north of Bakersfield, said she wanted to pay her respects in person.

The world-renowned labor rights leader and activist means “a great deal”, de la Cruz said, “because if it hadn’t been for her, there wouldn’t be many benefits for the people who work in the fields.”

The week was filled with events dedicated to Huerta’s memory, her courage, resilience and her impact on the community.

On Monday, Huerta was honored in Sacramento by state government officials including Monique Limón, the senate president pro tempore, Robert Rivas, the assembly speaker, Xavier Becerra, the California governor-elect, and community leaders in a historic ceremony.

Huerta became the first female, first Latino and first non-elected leader to lie in honor at the state capitol. Her casket was escorted in after a march organized by local unions.

“Our mother devoted her entire life to fighting injustice and lifting up communities too often left out and treated unfairly,” said Huerta’s family. “As a lifelong organizer, her activism has been a source of inspiration and empowerment for people around the world.”

For more than six decades, Huerta spearheaded historic boycotts, fought tirelessly for farm workers’ rights, women’s equality and environmental justice, and coined the enduring national rally cry, “¡Sí, se puede!”

Huerta has received numerous honors including the Eleanor Roosevelt Human Rights Awards. In 2012, Barack Obama awarded Huerta the Presidential Medal of Freedom, the nation’s highest civilian honor.

The two-day tribute started on Thursday evening with a public rosary and vigil in Bakersfield, where she had lived since 1990.

Friday’s interfaith memorial service and celebration of life began with an opening blessing by Aztec dancers. There were performances from music groups Los Lobos, Ozomatli, Las Cafeteras, Nancy Sanchez and Trio los Dados. Rob Bonta, the California attorney general, was among the speakers on Friday.

“Dolores Huerta is irreplaceable and her loss is something I think people are still, including me, getting our heads around,” Bonta said. “To the world, she is an icon and a giant and a legend. For me, she was also a friend and a mentor and a counselor. Someone I texted with and called and sought advice from and leaned on as I launched my career and had my career in elected public office.”

“Her loss is immeasurable. She is irreplaceable but she leaves a legacy of fighting for justice,” Bonta said.

Ten of Huerta’s adult children took the stage to share personal anecdotes about Huerta’s life, emphasizing her skills as an organizer, political strategist and motivational speaker. Her children recounted her dedication to social justice, her love for jazz, and her humility despite her significant contributions.

“While there is much to celebrate in our recent victories, there is still so much work to be done,” said Huerta’s daughter Camila Chavez. “People may expect us to fill her shoes, but she is going to be buried with her shoes. Like Zapata said: ‘I’d rather live on my feet than die on my knees.’ It’s going to take all of us here to fill those shoes.”

Her legacy will continue through the work and empowering mission of the Dolores Huerta Foundation. In 2003, Huerta founded the grassroots community organization to create leadership opportunities for community members, while organizing underserved communities, and pursuing social justice.

Huerta kept on working even as a nonagenarian, dedicating her life as an organizer.

In the Central valley, Huerta supported minorities to run for office to make a difference at the local, state and federal level. In a 2022 speech, Huerta encouraged new US citizens to fulfill their civic responsibilities, especially voting.

For her 94th birthday, Huerta asked people to help her celebrate by getting others involved in elections either by talking to somebody about voting and about registering to vote.

Huerta urged uninsured Latinos and Californians to enroll in health coverage with the passage of the Affordable Care Act partnering with Covered California to spread the message. Huerta also had several schools named after her throughout the state. Last year, Huerta joined Central valley educators to demand David Valadao, a US representative from California, defend funding for schools.

#191. Repression against Iranian lawyers increases

At least 135 lawyers in Iran have been subjected to repressive measures over the past year. UN experts warn that this seriously jeopardizes the right of those accused to trial in a constitutional court.

UN human rights experts have called on the Iranian authorities to end the escalating persecution of independent lawyers. They warn that limiting access to legal representation increases the risk of arbitrary detention, coerced confessions and unfair trials.

"Independent lawyers are increasingly being prosecuted and intimidated for their professional work and for defending human rights," the experts said in a statement in mid-November.

They said that between September 2025 and September 2026, at least 135 lawyers were issued with a summons, arrested, detained, suspended, dismissed or banned from traveling. In January and February 2026, 23 lawyers, including five women, were reportedly arrested after nationwide protests.

A current example is the case of Amirsalar Davoudi, a human rights lawyer who was arrested in October in the central Iranian city of Isfahan, where he was defending a client in the Revolutionary Court. According to Iran Human Rights (IHRNGO), he was taken to hospital on November 9 after suffering a heart attack in custody.

"There is a long history to the persecution of and coercive measures taken against lawyers," Sina Yousefi, a 41-year-old lawyer and human rights activist, told DW.

In October 2022, Yousefi was arrested by security forces in Tabriz during the nationwide "Woman Life Freedom" protests. Later, he was sentenced to six months' imprisonment for "propaganda against the system." He has since left Iran, and now lives in the German capital Berlin.

"The pressure was already increasing on lawyers back then, because they were undertaking the defense of people who were arrested and publicly expressing their support for the demonstrators," said Yousefi. "The security services, judiciary, and criminal prosecution apparatus increasingly used targeted mechanisms to move against independent lawyers."

Even renowned and internationally known lawyers such as Nasrin Sotoudeh, who was co-awarded the Sakharov Prize in 2012, have been affected by this repression. In 2026, Sotoudeh was awarded the Vaclav Havel Human Rights Prizeafter being repeatedly arrested and imprisoned.

Yousefi said arrests and threats against lawyers working on political and human rights cases not only curtailed the rights of those accused to a defense, but also discouraged other lawyers from accepting such briefs. This, he added, compromised the fairness of trials.

Criminal proceedings have also been instigated, or other measures taken, against lawyers who undertook to represent demonstrators. Comments on social media about the violation of civil rights can also be cited as grounds for investigation and judicial proceedings.

Yousefi also warned against tightening restrictions on a person's right to choose their own lawyer. According to the note to Article 48 of Iran's Code of Criminal Procedure, for people arrested on certain charges, access to lawyers of their own choosing is already restricted during the investigation phase. Defendants must resort to lawyers approved by the head of the judiciary. In some cases, even these lawyers are not granted access to all the files and evidence.

According to Yousefi, in many cases in which charges have been brought against demonstrators, lawyers have only been granted access to the files "when the preliminary investigations have been concluded and the evidence for the prosecution has been gathered — including confessions made during their imprisonment." Even then, he said, "their access to the records is limited and they have little time to prepare the defense."

To view this video please enable JavaScript, and consider upgrading to a web browser that supports HTML5 video

"In many cases," Yousefi added, "the judges ascribe greater importance to reports by security agents than to the defense. Verdicts are handed down predominantly on the basis of this."

Shabnam Moinipour, the program director at the Center for Supporters of Human Rights (CSHR) in the UK, sees the independence of the bar as a fundamental precondition for the effective defense of citizens. The organization monitors the situation of lawyers in Iran, documenting violations of their rights, and following up at an international level.

"A lawyer must be able to call into question the charges brought by the public prosecutor and the credibility of the security agencies' reports without fear of criminal prosecution," Moinipour told DW. "Because when a lawyer's freedom and professional activity are threatened, their client's right to defense is impaired."

In Moinipour's view, when the decision as to which lawyers are approved is made by the most senior representative of the same court authority, it raises serious questions about the independence of the bar.

"This restriction has psychological as well as legal consequences," she stressed. "For the accused, it may reinforce a sense of being unprotected, as well as feelings of fear and mistrust. It can also prevent them from disclosing details of threats, torture, and illegal behavior."

Moinipour considers this practice incompatible with Iran's obligations as a member of the UN Human Rights Committee. International law stipulates that people must have immediate access to a lawyer of their own choosing, be able to communicate with them in confidence and be supplied with sufficient means to mount a defense.

To view this video please enable JavaScript, and consider upgrading to a web browser that supports HTML5 video

Referring to a joint report by the Tehran-based Defenders of Human Rights Center and the International Bar Association's Human Rights Institute (IBAHRI), as well as the recent statement by UN human rights experts, Moinipour said: "Even without mass arrests, the actions taken against a limited number of lawyers can send a threatening message to others and restrict their professional activity. This phenomenon is described as a chilling or deterrent effect."

However, Moinipour stressed that, despite this pressure, "many lawyers in Iran continue to defend the rights of their clients and accept the risk of arrest, criminal prosecution, and professional restrictions. In doing so, they are prepared, if necessary, to pay a high price for this resistance."

This article was originally written in Persian.

#186. Trump turns White House press conference into a 2028 succession audition

The president appeared to put Vance and Rubio through their paces as potential successors, though Stephen Miller unexpectedly stole the spotlight

“Which of you shall we say doth love us most?”

The ageing King Lear’s question to his daughters, expecting them to compete for his favour and carve up his kingdom, hovered in the White House press briefing room on Friday.

There at the lectern stood Donald “Lear” Trump. Behind him stood his potential heirs, JD “Goneril” Vance and Marco “Regan” Rubio, and to the side sat Trump’s favourite offspring, Stephen “Cordelia” Miller (more of him later). As for Lear’s Fool, the choices were endless.

Ostensibly this was a press conference to trumpet the president’s trip in Asia, but in practice everyone knew it was the first audition for the 2028 presidential election (and boy, is Trump going to enjoy turning the race into a contest for his approval). Vance is widely expected to seek the crown, though it was Rubio who came out on top in the latest Emerson College national poll.

In a jam-packed room, Trump stood centre stage as if hosting The Apprentice all over again. Behind him were the two contenders expressing their aspirations through matching suits, matching Maga red ties, matching US flag pins and matching leather shoes.

The first trial: could Vance and Rubio stand still for 90 minutes without rolling their eyes, yawning or silently mouthing “Kill me!” to the press? Could they force Trump’s word salad down their throats and look as if they were enjoying it? Even when the boss was rambling at length about potholes?

“When you fix a pothole”, began one detour by the octogenarian president. “I don’t want to bore you – they’ll say, oh, he rambled, he rambled terribly. I don’t ramble. I don’t ramble at all, but when you fix a pothole, you have brand new asphalt against old white asphalt that doesn’t look good, but at least you don’t ruin your car, but these roads were flawless …”

But first, the whole thing got off to a strange start. Trump appeared at the podium and remarked: “That’s a big, big a crowd. I’ve never seen this before,” but reporters crammed in the room could barely hear him above the whoosh of an air conditioning unit. An engineer tweaked the sound, but it only gave Trump’s voice an eerie echo.

Had the gremlins that grounded flights and frozen banking sites finally reached the White House? So much for America the hi-tech superpower. Eventually order was restored, though not necessarily in Trump’s brain.

He was evidently flattered by the media comparing his trilateral summit with the leaders of China and Russia to the Yalta meeting between Winston Churchill, Franklin Roosevelt and Joseph Stalin (he did not say which of the three he identified with, though critics could take a guess). Like a gushing teenager, Trump was so thrilled that Chinese president Xi Jinping had come to the airport to greet him, that he mentioned it twice (“He didn’t meet Sleepy Joe with the plane, that I can tell you.”)

The US, Russia and China hold 92% of global nuclear power, he noted, which gave him an excuse to bring up his uncle John Trump, an electrical engineer and former Massachusetts Institute of Technology professor. “A member of my family spent his life at MIT who they say is now the greatest professor in the history of MIT, so I have great understanding of- I guess maybe genetically I’m blessed,” Trump riffed.

At that remark Rubio could resist no longer. A smile spread across his lips and he shot a look at Vance, who felt obliged to smile back. We’re not laughing at you, we’re laughing with you, Mr President.

Indeed, Rubio had been the more fidgety of the pair. As Trump chuntered on, the secretary of state had shifted from foot to foot, fiddled with his tie, played with his lower lip, touched up his hair and given the odd wry smile. Vance, meanwhile, hid behind his beard and miasma of smugness.

But then, at last, came the moment for Lear’s daughters to make their public declarations of love. Vance went up the lectern and pointed out that he had stayed in Washington rather than joining the trip, comparing himself to Macaulay Culkin’s character in Home Alone – a child who is accidentally left behind at Christmas as his family rushes to catch a flight to Paris. “I missed you, sir,” he said to Trump, before praising the president for “securing historic trade deals” and reorganising the global economy to benefit the American worker and economy.

Not bad, but a little wonky. Marco, you’re up next. The secretary of state took aim at the critics who accused Trump of cosying up to dictators at the expense of Democrats, raising his eyebrows and shaking his fist: “These three countries possess the overwhelming majority of nuclear weapons on this planet. The idea that these three leaders should not engage on a regular basis is not just absurd, it’s dangerous.”

Trump began taking reporters’ questions. One young whippersnapper posed a question that will be asked many times over the next two years. “Because you have the vice-president, the secretary of state right behind you, I’m curious which of them do you think would make a better president after you?”

Trump swatted it aside without answering. Vance remained stony faced. Rubio smiled knowingly.

The same reporter put Vance in an uncomfortable spot by asking about his post-midterm tweet. “Would you argue that the war in Iran was worth it to lose the Senate?” The vice-president agreed with the goal of Iran not getting a nuclear weapon but added carefully: “I think the president is the one who makes the decision and the president has said he would do it again. And my view is that my goal is and my job is to support the president of the United States.”

In other words, I hate this war and would never have started it but I have to back the boss. Expect a lot more of this needle-threading in the upcoming campaign.

Later, when Trump asked “Do you want to keep this going or not” amid a cacophony of shouted questions, he turned around to look at Rubio, who laughed, then at Vance, who did likewise with folded arms. The two rivals patted each other playfully. But neither was prepared for the man who would steal their thunder.

“We have the most powerful man in the world in the room right now,” said Trump, referring to Miller, the recently appointed White House chief of ideological zealotry, who stepped up to the lectern with bald pate gleaming under the bright lights.

“I just wanted to say that what President Trump has achieved over the last two years is more than any president has achieved by far in American history!” Miller roared, before offering the kind of vainglorious soliloquy that would make even a Kremlin or Chinese Communist party spokesperson blush. “What you’re going to see over the next two years, as astonishing as that has been under his leadership, the next two years are going to be the greatest two years in American history!”

Trump, who loves nothing more than flattery with exclamation marks, returned to the lectern and announced: “We have breaking news. We have breaking news.” Waving his hands, then with thumbs pointed at Vance and Rubio, he said of Miller: “He was better than these two guys. Do you want to run for president?”

That was a joke, right? Trump was stirring the succession pot again, making his potential heirs uncomfortable. It was fitting that he ended the press conference reminiscing about the night he hosted Ultimate Fighting Championship bouts on the White House South Lawn. He is going to relish every moment of presiding over a Republican cage match for the next two years.

#184. Man accused of sparking deadly Los Angeles wildfire found guilty

Verdict in case against former Uber driver Jonathan Rinderknecht comes after first trial ended with hung jury

The 30-year-old accused of sparking the deadly Palisades fire in Los Angeles was found guilty of two federal arson charges in a Friday verdict that came months after his first trial concluded with a hung jury.

For two weeks, a fresh batch of jurors listened to prosecutors mount their case against Jonathan Rinderknecht.

They argued the former Uber driver was responsible for the January 2025 wildfire, which claimed 12 lives and devastated the Pacific Palisades, an affluent enclave on the California coast.

Rinderknecht, in prosecutors’ account, used a lighter to set a brush fire in the hillside area. The state cast him as a disgruntled young man who sought retribution against the world for personal gripes, including failed romances, and perceived societal injustices like wealth inequality.

Firefighters suppressed the so-called Lachman brush fire last January, but its embers simmered underground, before being swept by powerful winds and erupting above ground into the Palisades inferno that destroyed thousands of homes and businesses.

Rinderknecht had maintained his innocence, pleading not guilty to the revised charges of destruction of property by means of fire and arson affecting property used in interstate commerce.

For the retrial, jurors were instructed explicitly to not consider the adequacy of the fire department’s response to the Lachman and Palisades blaze. The department has faced criticism for failing to fully extinguish the Lachman fire that eventually reignited.

The note came after some of the first trial’s jurors were wary of pinning the blame squarely on Rinderknecht, citing the fire department’s past actions. Of that deadlocked panel, two voted for guilty, while 10 others were not convinced.

Rinderknecht’s defense team doubled down during the retrial on an alternative explanation for the Palisades fire, suggesting the initial brush blaze was ignited by fireworks.

Prosecutors dismissed the theory as “baseless” in a court filing where they raised concern over the reliability and late introduction of a defense witness, whose testimony would reference stains at a private residence that supposedly indicated fireworks had been discharged.

The state also presented digital evidence that cast doubt over Rinderknecht’s claim that he was at the bottom of a hiking trail when he saw the Lachman fire begin.

“These lies are telling – they are not mistakes,” prosecutor Danbee Kim reportedly said at trial. “Because he knows that where he was when the fire started was incriminating.”

Friday’s conviction followed two days of jury deliberation.

Standing outside a downtown Los Angeles courthouse on Friday, one juror cited the inconsistencies in Rinderknecht’s whereabouts during the fire as proof of his guilt.

“Logically, things started pointing towards him,” said Cameron, a 30-year-old college student who declined to give his last name to reporters.

Cameron stopped short of answering whether or not he believed Rinderknecht intended to burn down the Palisades.

He said he believed Rinderknecht’s “intent was to start a fire” and that his actions resulted in the devastating blaze.

The city and county of Los Angeles continue to face civil lawsuits from survivors of the fire and victims’ families.

Friday’s guilty verdict may not bear much weight on that litigation.

“For the purpose of our civil case, the fire could have been started by lightning and would have had the same result,” said Alex Robertson, an attorney representing fire victims in a consolidated suit.

“It doesn’t matter what started the fire. It’s what happened – what didn’t happen in terms of protecting the residents of the Palisades and Malibu after the fire started.”

In a Friday statement, the outgoing Los Angeles mayor Karen Bass said: “No verdict can bring back the lives lost or erase the pain families have endured.”

“So many are still struggling to rebuild,” she continued. “For those still displaced, the devastation remains a daily reality.”

Rinderknecht, who remains in federal custody, will be sentenced on 17 March. He is facing between five and 40 years in prison.

#182. HSBC's top execs face tense shareholders calling for a breakup

HSBC’s top brass defended their strategy Monday to frustrated shareholders in the lender’s largest market, as Europe’s biggest bank continued to face calls to be split up.

At an informal shareholder meeting in Hong Kong, Chairman Mark Tucker and CEO Noel Quinn took questions from investors on issues ranging from how the bank was approaching demands for an overhaul of its business to its purchase of Silicon Valley Bank’s UK arm.

In prepared remarks, Tucker and Quinn each reiterated the board’s recommendation that shareholders vote against a resolution on the docket for its annual general meeting in May that would force the bank to come up with a plan to spin off or reorganize its Asian business — the lender’s main source of profits.

HSBC's biggest shareholder reignites debate about breaking up the bank

Tucker said the board was unanimous in its opposition to the resolution, stating plainly: “It would not be in your interest to split the bank.”

He said the board had previously reviewed a range of options for restructuring the bank, and concluded that such alternatives would “materially destroy value for shareholders,” including dividends.

“Our strategy is working,” Tucker told the room of more than 1,000 shareholders. “Our current strategy is moving dividends up.”

HSBC has been facing calls to separate its Asian business from the rest of the bank over the past year.

Shareholders in Hong Kong — where HSBC is a mainstay of many retail investors’ portfolios — contend that the London-based lender’s performance has been dragged down by its businesses in other regions.

Quinn addressed those complaints head-on Monday, saying “our profits in Hong Kong and the UK are no longer being dragged down by underperformance elsewhere. The group is performing well as a whole.”

Pressed later by a shareholder on the issue, Quinn said a breakup of the bank would result in “significant revenue loss” because much of its business relied on cross-border transactions.

Investors have also been unhappy with HSBC scrapping its dividend in 2020, at the request of British regulators. They argue that if the lender cordoned off its activities in Asia, it would no longer have to expose Hong Kong shareholders to requests in other jurisdictions.

Christine Fong, a district council member in Hong Kong, said she represented about 500 small shareholders who had been affected by the dividend cancellation.

“Street hawkers, taxi drivers or teachers — they all relied on the dividend to pay for their regular expenses, like mortgage, insurance payments, school fees,” Fong told CNN.

“That’s why, three years ago, what HSBC did upset those small minority shareholders.”

Fong has now joined calls for shareholders to vote in favor of the proposal for the bank to spin off its Asian business, despite the lender bringing back its dividend in 2021, albeit at a lower level.

Ken Lui, an activist shareholder in Hong Kong who put the resolution together, doubled down on his call for support ahead of the meeting Monday.

The resolution will require 75% of votes to be passed in May, but “nothing is impossible,” he told reporters outside the meeting venue.

Lui, who said he personally held a stake worth 100 million Hong Kong dollars ($12.7 million), laid out plans for his team to focus on “targeted outreach to institutional shareholders to present our case and gain their support.”

His group will also canvass 18 districts of Hong Kong “to tell HSBC shareholders that they finally have a chance to speak for themselves and protect their rights through voting,” he added.

HSBC is also facing pressure from its largest shareholder.

Ping An (PNGAY), China’s biggest insurer, holds an 8% stake in HSBC and has backed calls for the bank to rethink its structure.

In a series of remarks made public by the Chinese firm last November, Huang Yong, chairman of Ping An’s asset management arm, said “we will support any initiatives including a spinoff that are conducive to improve HSBC’s performance and value.”

Since then, the insurance giant’s views haven’t changed, according to a person familiar with the matter.

The source told CNN that Ping An has been calling for HSBC to explore a reorganization, with an eye on boosting its valuation and simplifying its regulatory obligations around the globe.

The insurer has not recommended a specific path forward but will support any initiatives, including a spinoff of its Asian business, that could boost its stock performance or value, the person added. Ping An did not immediately respond to a request for comment on how it planned to vote at the upcoming general meeting.

HSBC’s leaders were also asked Monfday why the bank had scooped up the British unit of SVB following the stunning collapse of its parent in the United States. The purchase was made for £1 ($1.20) last month, just days after SVB had folded.

Critics have questioned HSBC’s ability to perform adequate due diligence on SVB UK’s customers because of how quickly the deal came together.

“Did HSBC look into the clients of SVB in detail? Say, the financial statement — whether they can pay back the loan?” said Fong.

HSBC buys SVB's UK business, ending 'nightmare' for British tech

Quinn and Tucker defended the acquisition, calling it a good business opportunity that allowed the bank to gain hundreds of innovative startups as customers. They pushed back on the notion that management hadn’t had time to carry out proper due diligence.

Tucker also weighed in on recent tumult in the banking industry, saying he did not expect an “immediate impact” on HSBC.

“After the collapse of a number of smaller regional banks and the takeover of Credit Suisse, the share prices of all banks have been suppressed,” he noted.

But he said he did not believe such developments represented “a systemic risk” to the sector. “I do expect a period of uncertainty” before nerves settle, he added.

#180. ‘A new record for entertainment’: Grand Theft Auto VI sales hit $3.8bn on first day of release, developer says

The announcement from Rockstar Games would mean GTA VI has already earned more than the entire box office run of the highest-grossing film of all time

Grand Theft Auto VI generated $3.79bn in retail sales on its first day, publisher Rockstar Games has announced, calling it the biggest launch for any entertainment product in history.

The worldwide figure was based on company estimates through the end of the day Thursday, the day the game launched. Rockstar said also it had sold 32.5m units of the game on prerelease, setting a record across platforms.

“We are incredibly humbled by the response from players around the world and we couldn’t be more excited to share this moment with you,” Sam Houser, Rockstar’s president and founder, said in a statement.

The company said the sales set “a new record for entertainment across any medium … becoming the biggest entertainment launch in history.”

The figure is nearly five times the roughly $800m that predecessor Grand Theft Auto V attracted during its first 24 hours in 2013.

It also exceeds the entire box office run of Avengers: Endgame, which became the highest-grossing film of all time last month at about $2.9bn worldwide after a rerelease pushed it past Avatar.

The game went on sale Thursday on PlayStation 5 and Xbox Series X|S, launching first in New Zealand and rolling out market by market as each reached midnight local time.

The release quickly became a global phenomenon, generating more social media posts than this year’s World Cup, according to monitoring platform Visibrain.

Critics praised the game, which has a score of 99 on review aggregator Metacritic.

Rockstar said that made it the highest-rated game ever.

Like its predecessors, “GTA VI” is an open-world action game in which players roam freely through a sprawling city, stealing cars, pulling off heists and carrying out missions for criminal gangs.

Its busy streets, neon-lit nightlife and beaches are rendered in striking detail, and non-player characters go about their lives whether or not the player is involved.

While critics have raved about the game, it has also prompted real-world warnings from authorities.

With the game’s release on Thursday, police departments across the US reminded people that the car thefts, reckless driving, violence and other criminal mayhem depicted in the blockbuster title should not be re-enacted on the country’s streets.

There is insufficient evidence supporting a scientific causal link between violent video games and violent behaviour, according to the American Psycyhological Association. Nonetheless, several police departments took to the internet with warnings – and even jokes – aimed at keeping GTA VI’s lawlessness firmly in the virtual world.

The Atlanta police department delivered one of the clearest messages in a social media post.

“GTA is not a real place, but Atlanta is!” the department warned. “Don’t confuse virtual entertainment with real-life decisions.”

#179. No one else in Reform is suitable to lead the party, Nigel Farage says

Farage issues warning to potential rivals and says he will fight next election as leader if ‘still here and fit and healthy’

Nigel Farage has said he does not see another person who could do his job as leader of Reform UK, in a warning shot to potential rivals who might seek to replace him.

The Reform UK leader delivered a message that he was going nowhere despite his worries over his personal safety in the wake of the killing of the Reform politician Ann Widdecombe.

There has been speculation over Farage’s future in light of police and parliamentary investigations of Reform’s financial affairs. The Commons standards watchdog is due to report soon on whether he should have declared a £5m personal gift from the crypto billionaire Christopher Harborne before the last election. If he gets a substantial suspension from parliament, he could face a second byelection, contested by other main parties, in his Clacton constituency.

However, Farage sounded a note of defiance in an interview with the Sun, saying: “These are very thoughtful moments, but, you know what, if I’m not here doing this job right now, who else could? Who else would? I don’t see that person. So I’m gonna do it.”

A number of other Reform UK politicians are potential contenders to succeed him, including the party’s economics spokesperson and former Conservative, Robert Jenrick, the home affairs spokesperson, Zia Yusuf, and the former leader Richard Tice. Another possibility is the return of Rupert Lowe, who left Reform in acrimonious circumstances and founded his own party, Restore Britain, but who is popular on the online hard right.

The party is going through a difficult period after slipping from first place in the polls to second or third behind behind Labour and sometimes the Conservatives.

Asked by the Sun if he had “blown” his chances of reaching No 10, Farage said: “No, far from it.”

He admitted there had been “self-inflicted wounds” in the past year but said he still had a chance of becoming prime minister.

He told the newspaper that he would fight the next election as Reform leader if he was “still here and fit and healthy”.

He also batted away questions about the £5m, which he has insisted was to pay for his personal security despite the fact the party has been providing this.

“What’s it got to do with you? Drugs, sports cars, desert islands. It’s a gift,” he said, adding that although the party had had “ebbs and flows” and had made mistakes after growing “too far and too fast”, he was confident it was “more than through the worst of it”.

On the prospect of another battle over Europe as Andy Burnham weighs the case for rejoining the EU, the Reform leader said: “If you ask me, have I got the energy and the enthusiasm to do this again? I would say to you, hold my jacket. I’m ready for it.”

Sign up for the Breaking News UK email to get newsletter alerts direct to your inbox

He also warned of people taking to the streets with “direct action” if Brexit is reversed.

In a separate interview with the Times while travelling to Brussels, where Burnham was attempting a reset deal, Farage said he believed the issue of Europe would dominate the years to come.

“It’s going to be absolutely huge. It’s going to be much bigger than anyone realises. The genie is out of the bottle; it literally can’t be stopped now,” he said.

He also ruled out joining a new campaign called Stay Out, led by former Tories, suggesting he would make his own case for keeping Brexit.

#177. China Renaissance suspends trading, delays results after founder's disappearance

China Renaissance, a top dealmaker in the country’s tech industry, said it would suspend trading of its shares and delay the release of its annual results because it still can’t get in touch with its founder.

Bao Fan, 52, started the boutique investment bank in 2005 and has been unreachable since the middle of February, according to the company. Shares in China Renaissance have plunged since Bao went missing, at one point dropping as much as 50%.

China Renaissance said in late February that it had learned Bao was “cooperating in an investigation” being carried out by certain authorities in the country. It gave no other details.

Chinese media have reported Bao might be assisting in an investigation related to a former executive at China Renaissance.

In a filing on Sunday, China Renaissance said auditors couldn’t complete their work or sign off on their report because of Bao’s absence. The board was also unable to give an estimate about when it would be able to approve its audited results for 2022 or dispatch its annual report by an April 30 deadline as required by Hong Kong’s listing rules.

Missing Chinese billionaire CEO assisting authorities in investigation, company says

Trading in the company’s shares was suspended from Monday as a result.

Bao is known as a veteran dealmaker who works closely with top technology companies in China. He helped broker the 2015 merger between two of the country’s leading food delivery services, Meituan and Dianping. Today, the combined company’s “super app” platform is ubiquitous in China.

His team has also invested in US-listed Chinese electric vehicle makers Nio (NIO) and Li Auto and helped Chinese internet giants Baidu (BIDU) and JD.com (JD) complete their secondary listings in Hong Kong.

Over the weekend, China’s top anti-graft watchdog launched an investigation into Liu Liange, former party secretary and chairman of Bank of China, according to a statement by the Central Commission for Discipline Inspection and the State Supervision Commission. The bank is state-owned and one of the country’s four biggest lenders.

Liu is suspected of “serious violations of discipline and law,” the statement said. He is among the most senior financial executives targeted in a broader financial crackdown by President Xi Jinping.

In January, Wang Bin, former party chief and chairman of China Life Insurance, was charged by national-level prosecutors with taking bribes and hiding overseas savings.

#171. Russian ornithologist jailed over small donations to Navalny's foundation

Sofia Rosenfeld has devoted her life to studying Arctic birds. A Russian court sentenced her to 3.5 years in a penal colony over donations to Navalny's foundation. Friends and family are fighting for her release.

Boris Rosenfeld shows DW a photo of him with his daughter Sofia and can't hold back tears. "This was the last time we saw her. Then she left for Moscow, and this whole case began," he says.

Helives in Israel, while Sofia is in Russia. For more than a month now, she has been behind bars on charges of financing extremism. The case goes back to two donations totaling 2,000 rubles (about $30) to the late opposition politician Alexei Navalny'sAnti-Corruption Foundation (FBK), which the Russian authorities have since designated as extremist.

On October 7, a court in Moscow sentenced Rosenfeld to three and a half years in a penal colony.

Rosenfeld is a prominent ornithologist who has devoted her life to studying and protecting Arcticbirds. "Sofia was an avid young naturalist from childhood. She loved birds, horses and dogs," her father said as he leafed through a photo album.

Sofia was a teenager when the family moved to Israel but she returned to Russia a few years later and now holds both Russian and Israeli citizenship.

She studied biology at Moscow State University and developed a significant scientific career in Russia. Her colleague, Alexandra Panyutina, who now also lives in Israel, explained that Rosenfeld was devoted to her work from an early age.

"Birds and the Arctic are the defining passions of her life," she said.

Rosenfeld was among the first Russian researchers to develop a systematic approach to monitoring migratory bird populations. To support her research, she even developed a special aircraft.

"They could fly at almost the same speed as the birds and observe them from a low altitude," says Dmitry (name changed), a colleague of Rosenfeld's. "It quickly became clear that this was a unique method."

Rosenfeld is considered to be one of the leading figures in Russian ornithology. Her work has extended well beyond Russia and she has been deeply involved in international research projects. In the field of migratory bird conservation, cross-border cooperation is essential.

Helmut Kruckenberg, the head of the German Institute for Wetlands and Waterbird Research (IWWR), said Rosenfeld was highly respected within the international scientific community.

"She has done an enormous amount for nature conservation and for the protection of Siberian and Arctic species," he said. "What is happening to her is simply beyond comprehension for us."

In the summer of 2021, when Russian authorities designated Alexei Navalny's Anti-Corruption Foundation, also known as FBK, as an extremist organization, Rosenfeld was on a remote Arctic expedition without a phone or access to the internet. Therefore, she did not cancel her regular donations to the FBK immediately, doing so only after her return to the Russian capital Moscow.

Her friend Olga (name changed) described Sofia as a person of absolute honesty. According to her, Rosenfeld's donations to Navalny's foundation reflected a genuine desire to fight corruption. Through her work in Russia's regions, she had seen plenty of it firsthand.

"She was never involved in political activism. Her donations were solely intended to support anti-corruption work," said her father Boris Rosenfeld. "It's unthinkable".

For him and others close to her, the sentence came as a shock. Although prosecutors had requested a five-year prison term, friends and colleagues expected that she would receive a fine at most.

There were reasons for that expectation. According to calculations by independent media outlet Mediazona, about 85% of convictions in the 225 cases involving donations to Navalny's foundation have so far resulted in non-custodial sentences. Yet Rosenfeld got three and a half years in a penal colony.

Just a few months after Rosenfeld defended her doctoral dissertation in October 2025, her home was searched. She was also subject to travel restrictions. She continued to work but she had to cancel her annual summer expedition to the Arctic.

Even on the day of the sentencing, her conversations with  her colleagues focused largely on science:  "We discussed how the work could continue in Sofia's absence," Olga said. "But no one really believed she would end up in prison."

"The only thing we think about now is how to bring her here," Boris Rosenfeld says as he looks at the pictures from Sofia's last visit.

Friends and colleagues who spoke to DW say that his daughter never considered leaving Russia, even as the prospect of a prison sentence loomed. "Sofia would never have left. She simply can't imagine life without geese," says Dmitry.

Now, her family, friends and colleagues are campaigning to have her sentence overturned. More than 1,000 people have signed an appeal to Russia's Presidential Council for Human Rights.

Boris Rosenfeld is also hoping for support from Israel but so far Israeli consular officials have not been granted access to his daughter in detention.

The two continue to write to each other and he is trying to keep his spirits high: "She writes that she's trying to adjust to life in prison, and that it's hard," he says. "But she's a strong person."

This article was originally published in Russian.

#168. Two men killed after glider crashes into Loch Leven in Scotland

The pair had taken off from the Scottish Gliding Centre at nearby Portmoak on a training flight

Two men have been killed after their glider crashed into Loch Leven, a large body of water in Perth and Kinross, on Friday morning.

Police Scotland said their bodies were recovered from the loch by the emergency services after the alarm was raised at 11.05am on Friday.

The force said next of kin had been told and the incident was not being treated as suspicious. Two HM Coastguard helicopters and an air ambulance were sent to the scene, along with a fire service appliance and a specialist crew.

The pair had taken off from the Scottish Gliding Centre at Portmoak airfield near the loch’s eastern shore in a Perkoz two-person glider on a training flight.

A spokesperson for the gliding centre said: “The incident is currently under investigation by the Air Accident Investigation Branch [AAIB] of the Department for Transport and the police in conjunction with the British Gliding Association.

“Our club is cooperating fully with these organisations. At the present time, there is no indication as to the cause of the accident.”

An AAIB spokesperson said: “A multidisciplinary team including inspectors with expertise in aircraft operations, engineering and recorded data have been deployed to gather evidence and make inquiries.”

#167. Florida mechanic found guilty of murdering British woman who attorney says came to US to die

Dwain Hall was convicted of stabbing Sonia Exelby, a music teacher who prosecutors say suffered from mental illness

A Florida mechanic has been found guilty of stabbing to death a British music teacher, whom he convinced to travel to the United States.

Dwain Hall, an auto repair and tow truck driver, groomed 32-year-old Sonia Exelby from Portsmouth, England, on a fetish website before luring her to the US.

Hall had pleaded not guilty to the charges of kidnap and first-degree murder.

At trial, jurors heard from several witnesses, most of them law enforcement officials, who detailed Hall’s email, phone records, social media accounts and other records to trace his movements after Exelby travelled from the UK to meet him.

Exelby’s disappearance from her home sparked an international investigation. Prosecutors this week described how she suffered from mental illness. Investigators said that Exelby had traveled to Florida after posting online about wanting someone to end her life.

Hall’s arrest affidavit said that Exelby had previously expressed suicidal thoughts and investigators alleged that Hall knew about her struggles and used that vulnerability to bring her to Florida, the Ocala Starbanner reported.

The authorities said Exelby boarded a flight to the US seemingly with an intention “to be possibly sexually abused, tortured and murdered by unknown individuals living in the United States”.

After Exelby traveled to the US in October 2025, authorities say Hall picked her up from the Gainesville airport in central Florida.

He drove her to an Airbnb rental, where investigators later found evidence they say connected him to her disappearance and death, including transactions on her bank cards and a video of Exelby on Hall’s cellphone.

According to the Times, Junior Barrett, Hall’s attorney, argued that Exelby took her own life. “She came here with the intention of dying, she thought she was worthless, useless, a burden on other people,” he said in his closing statement.

“This isn’t a question about what porn he likes. You can disagree with him about what he likes, but the case can’t be decided on you not liking his lifestyle. The fact he did not stop her from killing herself does not make it murder.”

Prosecutor Amy Beth Berndt said jurors should use their “common sense”. She added that Hall targeted Exelby for her mental illness and killed her for his sadistic pleasure.

Berndt said Exelby “was beautiful. She was kind and she was deeply loved by everyone who knew her. And she also suffered from mental illness for most of her life. She should be alive today.”

She added: “No one could save her from the defendant, the ‘Alpha Sadist’. He took advantage of a severely depressed, mentally ill woman who was incapable of making any rational decisions.”

After the verdict was read, Exelby’s brother Barry Wilton said: “This last year has been one of the hardest of our lives. Losing our dear sister Sonia has had a profound impact on me and my entire family. I’m not sure we will ever be the same again.”

Wilton then addressed Hall directly, adding: “You pretended to care for Sonia. You didn’t at all. You used her to fulfil your sick fantasies. You had her life in your hands. You had the opportunity to save her, but instead you preyed on her vulnerabilities and you took her from us.”

In the US, call or text Mental Health America at 988 or chat 988lifeline.org. You can also reach Crisis Text Line by texting MHA to 741741. #165. Micron Technology: China probes US chip maker for cybersecurity risks as tech tension escalates

China has launched a cybersecurity probe into Micron Technology, one of America’s largest memory chip makers, in apparent retaliation after US allies in Asia and Europe announced new restrictions on the sale of key technology to Beijing.

The Cyberspace Administration of China (CAC) will review products sold by Micron in the country, according to a statement by the watchdog late on Friday.

The move is aimed at “ensuring the security of key information infrastructure supply chains, preventing cybersecurity risks caused by hidden product problems, and maintaining national security,” it noted.

It came on the same day that Japan, a US ally, said it would restrict the export of advanced chip manufacturing equipment to countries including China, following similar moves by the United States and the Netherlands.

Washington and its allies have announced curbs on China’s semiconductor industry, which strike at the heart of Beijing’s bid to become a tech superpower.

Last month, the Netherlands also unveiled new restrictions on overseas sales of semiconductor technology, citing the need to protect national security. In October, the United States banned Chinese companies from buying advanced chips and chipmaking equipment without a license.

Micron told CNN it was aware of the review.

“We are in communication with the CAC and are cooperating fully,” it said, adding that it stands by the security of its products. “Micron’s product shipments, engineering, manufacturing, sales and other functions are operating as normal.”

Shares in Micron sank 4.4% on Wall Street Friday following the news, the biggest drop in more than three months. On Monday, they closed another 1.2% lower. Micron derives more than 10% of its revenue from China.

In an earlier filing, the Idaho-based company had warned of such risks.

“The Chinese government may restrict us from participating in the China market or may prevent us from competing effectively with Chinese companies,” it said last week.

Japan joins the US and Europe in chipmaking curbs on China

China has strongly criticized restrictions on tech exports, saying last month it “firmly opposes” such measures.

In efforts to boost growth and job creation, Beijing is seeking to woo foreign investments as it grapples with mounting economic challenges. The newly minted premier Li Qiang and several top economic officials have been rolling out the welcome wagon for global CEOs and promising they would “provide a good environment and services.”

But Beijing has also exerted growing pressure on foreign companies to bring them into line with its agenda.

Last month, authorities closed the Beijing office of Mintz Group, a US corporate intelligence firm, and detained five local staff.

Days earlier, they suspended Deloitte’s operations in Beijing for three months and imposed a fine of $31 million over alleged lapses in its work auditing a state-owned distressed debt manager.

CNN’s Michelle Toh, Junko Ogura, Sandi Sidhu and Chris Lau contributed to the article.

#162. Police arrest teacher who missed sentencing after indecent assault conviction

Sally-Anne Bowen was found guilty of having sexual relationships with two pupils at a boys’ school in the 1980s

A chemistry teacher who had sexual relationships with two pupils at a boys’ school in the late 1980s has been arrested by police in Hampshire after she failed to turn up to her sentencing hearing.

Sally-Anne Bowen, 65, could not be found at her home by Metropolitan police officers on Friday morning after a warrant was issued for her arrest, a court heard.

The former teacher, who was in her 20s when she had sex with one pupil up to 30 times and kissed a second boy when they were aged about 14 to 15, was convicted in August of seven counts of indecent assault and granted bail before her sentencing.

Her trial heard she wore short, tight skirts and flirted in class, with one former pupil comparing her behaviour to “a Sabrina Carpenter video”.

After her conviction she was granted bail and was due to be sentenced at Harrow crown court on Friday.

But Bowen, from Hemel Hempstead, Hertfordshire, did not turn up to court in the morning, and the judge, Karim Ezzat, issued a warrant for her arrest, “not backed by bail”.

Neelam Gomersall, prosecuting, said Bowen’s partner of 36 years, Darren Scotchbrook, was at the address when officers arrived, but Bowen was not.

Gomersall had earlier told the court there was “no evidence” that Bowen could not attend the sentencing, and that information from Scotchbrook was “suggestive of refusal rather than inability” to attend.

Bowen’s barrister, Mark Kimsey, said: “Our instructing solicitor has tried to contact her and the phone rings but doesn’t answer.”

He had earlier said that Bowen was in contact with solicitors on Thursday and had engaged with a pre-sentence report, adding there had been “concerns as to her welfare due to the content of the messages” from her.

In an update on Friday afternoon, the Met said: “On Friday November 20, officers were made aware that a 65-year-old woman had failed to appear at Harrow crown court for sentencing.

“A warrant was issued for her arrest and officers promptly launched inquiries to locate her.

“At around 3pm, officers from a neighbouring force found the woman at a location in Hampshire and arrested her on suspicion of failing to appear at court.

Sign up for the Breaking News UK email to get newsletter alerts direct to your inbox

“She has been taken into police custody. A new sentencing date will be set by the court in due course.”

Bowen’s trial heard that she was in her mid-20s when she taught at Christ’s College in Finchley, north London.

One victim had told jurors he had had sex with her “about 20 or 30 times” over several months, considered her his girlfriend and kept it secret. He said it had led to “the destruction of my education”.

The second victim described spending lunchtimes with Bowen in a cafe, where he was “goaded” into kissing her by other boys and recalled touching her breasts.

Bowen denied the allegations as “total fabrication” and claimed a medical condition made sex difficult and painful. She denied flirting, saying: “I didn’t flirt with students, I was just friendly.” Defending her clothes, she said she “wasn’t going to dress like Margaret Thatcher”.

#161. JP Morgan boss says he will find ways to offset ‘unfair’ rise in UK tax on banks

Jamie Dimon, the chief executive of JP Morgan, has said he will find ways to offset the chancellor’s tax rise for banks, calling the increase unfair.

Speaking at an event in London on Thursday, Dimon said he would seek to claw back the increase and was “setting an example so that [governments] understand the trade-offs of these unfair penalties”.

The bank surcharge is to rise from 3% to 6% in January as a temporary 15-month measure. It was unclear how JP Morgan, which employs 23,000 people in the UK, would make up for the extra cost.

In comments first reported by the Financial Times, citing people who attended the event, Dimon said he would set an example in making up for the added costs, and that the decision was “more in sorrow” than in anger.

Dimon has repeatedly lobbied Labour against raising taxes on banks, and suggested in May that he could scrap plans for a new £3bn tower in the Canary Wharf district of London that is expected to serve as JP Morgan’s UK headquarters.

Shortly after the chancellor, John Healey, announced the policy during his inaugural budget in October, a source familiar with the matter told the Guardian that plans for the 279,000 sq metre (3m sq ft) building were still going ahead. But the bank also said at the time that the policy “lacks principle” for singling the sector out, adding that it operated in a “highly competitive global marketplace”.

JP Morgan also has a sprawling campus in Bournemouth, where it employs more than 5,300 staff who process more than $1tn (£750bn) worth of global transactions each day. Earlier this month, the Guardian reported fears that the advance of artificial intelligence could put their jobs at risk.

Lenders in the UK pay a 28% corporation tax rate, higher than the standard 25%, and the separate surcharge on their UK balance sheets, which is set for the temporary rise. JP Morgan, the biggest bank in the US, expected to pay £120m more in taxes because of the increase, the FT reported.

#160. US judge approves Bayer's $7.25 billion cancer settlement

Bayer could end years of costly lawsuits in the US over claims that its Roundup weedkiller causes cancer. Tens of thousands of people could now receive compensation, although some lawyers say the payments are too low.

A US judge on Friday approved German chemical giant Bayer's $7.25 billion (€6.60 billion) settlement of lawsuits claiming its Roundup weedkiller causes cancer.

Judge Timothy Boyer in the US state of Missouri ruled that the agreement was "fair, reasonable, and adequate."

The decision could help Bayer end years of costly legal battles over Roundup, which the company acquired when it bought US weedkiller producer Monsanto in 2018.

"The court's final approval is a decisive milestone in the company's strategy to mitigate the risks of both pending and future Roundup litigation," Bayer CEO Bill Anderson said.

The agreement covers tens of thousands of people who say they developed non-Hodgkin lymphoma, a type of blood cancer, after using Roundup.

It also covers people who may be diagnosed with the disease in the future after previous exposure to the weedkiller.

Bayer will pay up to $7.25 billion into a special fund over the next 21 years.

Individual payments are expected to average between $10,000 and $165,000. The amount will depend on factors including the patient's age, the severity of the illness and how long they were exposed to Roundup.

Some lawyers representing cancer patients have criticized the payments as too low.

But the judge rejected their objections, saying that further delays could leave seriously ill patients waiting years for compensation.

Christopher Seeger, a lawyer representing cancer patients, welcomed the decision.

"Today's final approval order delivers guaranteed compensation to Roundup cancer victims who have waited more than a decade for justice," he said.

The settlement follows a major victory for Bayer at the US Supreme Court in June.

The court ruled that Bayer could not be sued under state laws for failing to put cancer warnings on Roundup packaging.

The judges argued that US federal rules did not require such warnings and that individual states could not impose additional labeling requirements.

The decision made it much harder for cancer patients to win lawsuits against Bayer.

In October, a Missouri jury also ruled in Bayer's favor in a separate Roundup cancer case.

However, some legal battles continue. Around 200 cases are still being fought in federal courts outside the new settlement, according to Bayer.

Monsanto introduced Roundup in 1974. Its main ingredient, glyphosate, became one of the world's most widely used weedkillers.

Farmers use Roundup to kill weeds without damaging crops that have been genetically modified to survive the chemical. It has also been widely used in private gardens and on lawns.

To view this video please enable JavaScript, and consider upgrading to a web browser that supports HTML5 video

Bayer inherited the legal problems surrounding Roundup when it bought Monsanto for $63 billion in 2018.

Since then, around 200,000 claims have been made against Bayer by people who believe the weedkiller caused their cancer.

The company already agreed to a $10.9 billion settlement in 2020. However, it failed to secure approval for a separate agreement covering future cases.

Some lawsuits have resulted in huge payouts, including a $2.1 billion award to plaintiffs in Georgia.

Bayer has already set aside $16 billion for Roundup-related legal costs. Its shares have fallen by 46% since the Monsanto takeover.

The company has also removed glyphosate from newer versions of Roundup sold to private consumers in the United States.

Scientists and regulators disagree about whether glyphosate causes cancer.

In 2015, the World Health Organization's cancer research agency classified the chemical as "probably carcinogenic" to humans.

However, the US Environmental Protection Agency says glyphosate is unlikely to cause cancer when used as directed.

Bayer has repeatedly denied that Roundup causes cancer, pointing to scientific studies and regulatory approvals.

The debate intensified last year when a major scientific study that had supported Roundup's safety for decades was withdrawn.

The journal that published it cited serious problems, including missing cancer research and previously undisclosed involvement by Monsanto employees.

The withdrawal raised further questions about the influence of chemical companies on scientific research.
