#118. Myanmar ruling gives new relevance to genocide convention and is ‘full of messages’ for Israel

Hailed as ‘a rare moment of justice’, judges’ decision sets out a roadmap for assessing the question of intent

The international court of justice (ICJ) verdict making Myanmar the first country in history to be found guilty of committing genocide does not just deliver justice for Rohingya victims. It also gives the 1948 genocide convention new relevance in governing the actions of nation states.

In their nine votes to six majority ruling, the panel of judges in The Hague set out a detailed roadmap for how the convention should be applied to real-world situations in which a government is accused of committing genocide against a whole population group, especially when it comes to assessing the question of intent.

The lengthy judgment will have been read particularly carefully by Israel, the only other country currently before the ICJ accused of genocide.

It is also relevant to Germany, which has been brought before the court by Nicaragua on the accusation it has facilitated and failed to prevent genocide in Gaza.

“I think it’s a judgment that brings the genocide convention alive,” said Philippe Sands, a leading human rights lawyer who was part of the Gambian legal team. “It gives the convention practical, real meaning. And if the alternative view had been taken, the convention would essentially become a dead letter.”

Amnesty International’s secretary general, Agnès Callamard, called the ruling “a rare moment of justice for survivors, victims and their families”. But it was also an assertion of humanitarian law in an increasingly lawless world.

“This is a good day for international law. We needed it,” said Dr Iva Vukušić, an assistant professor in international History at Utrecht University and a war crimes expert. “It is really important, clarifying a lot of law, and that’s going to be very helpful going forward.”

There will be a spillover from this ruling to its sister tribunal in The Hague, the international criminal court (ICC), providing a benchmark for when genocide charges could be made against individual perpetrators.

The Trump administration, instinctively hostile to international legal institutions, is also watching critically. The US ambassador to the UN told the ICJ before this judgment not to “lower the legal threshold for proving genocide” lest it be “weaponised” against US and allied soldiers.

The court did not directly lower the standard for demonstrating genocide, and the Gambia – which brought the case to The Hague – did not ask it to.

The judges kept the existing benchmark for judging genocide intent as the “only reasonable inference” of Myanmar’s actions and referred extensively to prior case law. But it went a long way towards clarifying how that standard should be applied in assessing atrocity crimes committed by states.

Before this case, only Serbia had faced the charge of genocide at the ICJ, in cases brought by Croatia and Bosnia and Herzegovina. In the Bosnian ruling in 2007, Belgrade was found to have failed to prevent the 1995 genocide in Srebrenica, but in both cases it was cleared of committing the genocide directly.

The new ICJ judgment finds the Myanmar military directly responsible for genocide against the Rohingya, a Muslim minority targeted in a ferocious counterinsurgency campaign that reached its peak in 2017.

In assessing the Myanmar campaign, the ICJ looked not just at the death toll of about 10,000 in assessing the scale of the atrocities, but at the total Rohingya population targeted in northern Rakhine state, 815,000 people. Significantly, it cast its net wider than just the death toll, considering mental damage done to the survivors, particularly victims of sexual violence, and the impact on reproduction in the Rohingya community.

Most importantly, the ICJ judgment provided a guide to interpreting the standard set by Serbian cases, in which a finding of genocide could be made only if there were not other plausible motives for mass killing.

In this judgment, the court ruled that Myanmar could have had other motives, such as the desire to displace or punish the Rohingya population, but that those motives could exist alongside genocidal intent. The judges said genocidal intent was the “only reasonable inference” from the “totality” of evidence.

In justifying that conclusion the judges pointed to several factors that international law experts say are relevant to the Gaza case, which is now hearing written pleadings and is expected to take at least another three years.

The common factors include the targeting of children, which the court described as striking at the “foundation” of the Rohingya as a group – the ICJ said up to 2,109 Rohingya children under the age of five had been killed, while more than 5,000 Palestinian children under five years old have been killed in the Gaza war.

Another incriminating factor cited by the court was the “bulldozing and large-scale terrain clearance” after the destruction of villages, followed by “rapid construction” including security installations, which has been the hallmark of Israeli army occupation of over 60% of the Gaza Strip.

“It’s full of messages for Israel,” said William Schabas, an international law professor and expert on genocide at Middlesex University. “There are obvious differences, of course, the evidence will be different, but if you exchanged the word Myanmar with Israel, you wouldn’t have to change much in the judgment.”

The fact that there were six dissenting voices on the bench suggested that substantial parts of the majority’s reasoning were not beyond dispute, and lawyers involved in the case cautioned that it did not necessarily mean that the same bench would find Israel guilty of genocide in Gaza. But the same lawyers added that the opposite was almost certainly true. If this case had failed, the case brought by South Africa against Israel would have been all but over.

#117. Finland's Prime Minister Sanna Marin concedes election

Finland’s left-wing Prime Minister Sanna Marin conceded defeat on Sunday in the Nordic country’s parliamentary election as the opposition right-wing National Coalition Party (NCP) claimed victory in a tightly fought contest.

The pro-business NCP was expected to win 48 of the 200 seats in parliament, narrowly ahead of the nationalist Finns Party with 46 seats and Marin’s Social Democrats on 43 seats, justice ministry election data showed with all ballots counted.

“We got the biggest mandate,” NCP leader Petteri Orpo said in a speech to followers, vowing to “fix Finland” and its economy.

He will get the first chance at forming a coalition to obtain majority in parliament as Marin’s era as prime minister was expected to end.

Popular abroad, at home Marin faces battle to keep her job

“We have gained support, we have gained more seats (in parliament). That’s an excellent achievement, even if we did not finish first today,” the prime minister said in a speech to party members.

Marin, 37, the world’s youngest prime minister when she took office in 2019, is considered by fans around the globe as a millennial role model for progressive new leaders, but at home she has faced criticism for her partying and her government’s public spending.

Finnish Prime Minister Sanna Marin takes drug test following partying backlash

While she remains very popular among many Finns, particularly young moderates, she antagonized some conservatives with lavish spending on pensions and education they see as not frugal enough.

The NCP has led in polls for almost two years although its lead had melted away in recent months. It has promised to curb spending and stop the rise of public debt, which has reached just over 70% of GDP since Marin took office in 2019.

Orpo accused Marin of eroding Finland’s economic resilience at a time when Europe’s energy crisis, driven by Russia’s war in Ukraine, has hit the country hard and the cost of living has increased.

Orpo has said he will negotiate with all groups to obtain a majority in parliament, while Marin has said her Social Democrats may govern with the NCP but will not go into government with the Finns Party.

Marin called the Finns Party “openly racist” during a debate in January – an accusation the nationalist group rejected.

The Finns Party’s main goal is to reduce what leader Riikka Purra has called “harmful” immigration from developing countries outside the European Union. It also calls for austerity policies to curb deficit spending, a stance it shares with the NCP.

Most notable of Marin’s foreign policy actions has been her push, along with President Sauli Niinisto, for the country to make a watershed policy U-turn by seeking NATO membership in the wake of Russia’s invasion of Ukraine.

That process is now almost complete, with Helsinki expected to join within days after all the Western defense alliance’s 30 members approved the accession.

#115. Iraq university’s removal of male professors teaching women sparks weeks of protests

Al-Qadisiyah University’s move a sign of the country’s slide into a Taliban-like gender segregation, say activists

The removal of male professors teaching female students at a university in southern Iraq has sparked protests and been criticised by activists as a Taliban-like gender segregation policy.

As many as 400 women are estimated to have joined protests over the past two weeks calling for the return of their professors at Al-Qadisiyah University in a move that appears to have surprised its administrators.

The university said the move was necessary to “prevent the mixing” of men and women and had been requested by the families of female students. But students said their families had known since before they enrolled that there were men on the teaching staff and supported their daughters’ protest.

Students said university bosses had mocked their protests and refused to discuss their concerns about the sudden loss of their academic supervisors. On social media, commentators also accused the women of seeking to keep male professors to “establish immoral relationships with them”.

“My classmates and I went to demonstrate, and the university president, Rahi al-Yasiri, blocked our path with his bodyguards, who then began making fun of us. He ordered them to take photographs of our faces, in an attempt to intimidate us and punish us later,” said Huda, 22, one of the female students.

Huda said the students tried to explain how men and women still worked together in society in Iraq. “Aren’t there doctors who treat both men and women? Why then do you prevent male professors from teaching us, too?” In response, Huda said Al-Yasiri replied: “Even if you hit your heads against the wall, I will not bring back the professors.”

The students said a large number of them were tied to graduation projects and research supervised by the professors. They said they had grown accustomed to their professors’ teaching methods, who also knew their students’ strengths and weaknesses, which made replacing them at this stage more complicated.

Female students said they had been told by the university that if they were not happy with the change, they should drop out.

An Iraqi-British feminist researcher, Balsam Mustafa, from the University of Cardiff, said the female students were “treated as if they are minors without the right to object to decisions affecting their academic lives”.

Mustafa said the removal of the male professors was a part of a worrying trend in women’s rights in the country, with “growing discussion about women’s clothing and chastity reflected in the celebration of female students who wear the abaya”.

An Iraqi lawyer and human rights activist, Israa Salman, said the restrictions on women was a “societal catastrophe”.

“Attempts are being made to suppress women’s talents and turn Iraqi society – a society that has produced remarkable minds and expertise, with women playing pioneering roles in many fields – into something resembling Kabul under Taliban rule.

“This contradicts Iraq’s constitution and the international treaties and conventions the country has signed and ratified,” said Salman.

#113. Misty-eyed Burnham gambles on closer ties with EU as he throws down gauntlet to Tories and Reform

PM challenges rivals to rebuff plan he says could add 0.5% annual growth to economy and offer young people hope

Several sentences into a slightly convoluted answer about where UK-EU relations might go next, Andy Burnham abruptly changed tack. “The important point is that the Britain of today and the EU of today is not the one of 2016,” he said. “Life has moved on in so many ways.”

This is undoubtedly true. But Burnham’s political gamble on closer ties with Brussels hinges on the assumption that most British voters feel this way as well.

Seeking to push his deal on food checks, emissions trading and a new youth exchange scheme, Burnham was at first in full consumer politics mode, saying it would bring cheaper food – with a namecheck for Tesco, Asda and other supermarket chains – as well as lower electricity costs and a claimed 0.5% boost to overall growth.

But as the Brussels press conference went on, the prime minister became more misty-eyed, particularly in describing the youth exchange scheme as a gift to a generation stripped of choices by the 2016 referendum and then Covid. “Hope is a word,” he intoned. “And it starts now.”

For all that Burnham is a skilled political salesman, his oratory is backed up with some helpful political context, matched with cooperative allies.

To begin with, not only politics but polling results are now different. A new constituency-level survey released on Friday showed that of 394 constituencies estimated to have voted leave in 2016, voters in 274 of them would back rejoining the EU – including Burnham’s Makerfield seat.

Standing alongside the UK prime minister in the venue, Ursula von der Leyen, the European Commission president, and António Costa, the European Council president, at times resembled proud parents, beaming at every positive phrase.

They, of course, began work when the EU reset was the personal project of one Keir Starmer. His name was not mentioned on Friday, and it is undeniable that Brussels finds life easier dealing with a UK leader who is both bold and popular.

Speaking to British reporters later in the day, Burnham openly challenged political opponents to rebuff a plan that, he said, could add 0.5% annual growth to the economy in the long term – more, he said, than any other single intervention.

“Why would anybody turn their back on that?” he said, with particular focus on Nigel Farage, who was, soon after Burnham spoke, hosting a retaliatory press conference of his own, 10 minutes’ walk from the EU building.

Even with the tight timing – Farage had to delay his event as the summit timetable slipped – the Reform UK leader drew a decent crowd of reporters. But there will be a sense in No 10 that beyond Farage, most of the formidable Brexit forces of 2016 have melted away.

Kemi Badenoch, who has been advised by her team to talk more about the economy and less about Brexit, spent the day on a visit in Essex. The Conservatives did send out a condemnatory press release about the EU deal, but it focused more on Burnham “reopening the divisions of the past” than the specifics.

More trenchant was a statement from Stay Out, the recently formed Brexit purity group, which floated its argument that rejoining the EU would cost the UK £750m a week.

Sign up for the Breaking News UK email to get newsletter alerts direct to your inbox

For all that the dominance of vote leave alumni in Stay Out gives the campaign the slight air of a historic battle re-enactment society, Burnham cannot ignore them. A resonant sidenote of the summit was how he was happy to talk about the specifics of the deal, but not much beyond that.

While it was the PM himself who provoked a debate about a future in which the UK rejoined the EU, the brakes were firmly on in regard to any discussion of such ideas.

Pre-summit talk of possible closer future links with Brussels was replaced by much vaguer language about mutual “brainstorming” on what would come next, with UK officials saying any progress could be months in the making.

Another sign of the caution embedded within Burnham’s ambition was the lineup of UK cabinet ministers who watched as the prime minister, von der Leyen and Costa arrived on Friday morning.

Alongside those you might expect – Ed Miliband, the foreign secretary, John Healey, the chancellor, and Jonathan Reynolds, the business secretary – was Anneliese Midgley, a close ally of Burnham who has the job of chief whip, No 10’s liaison with Labour MPs.

She was there because a bill setting into UK law the new EU deal will be presented to parliament next week. Most Labour MPs agree with Burnham that the world has changed since 2016. But, as with the voters, he needs to bring them along with him.

#112. Britain's pound is beating every other major currency this year

The British pound crashed to a record low last fall as investors rebelled against budget plans by former Prime Minister Liz Truss. Now, it’s enjoying a comeback.

Sterling hit its highest level against the US dollar in 10 months on Tuesday, topping $1.25 for the first time since June 2022. The pound, which has advanced about 3.3% versus the greenback since the start of 2023, is the best-performing currency among developed economies this year.

The UK currency has been boosted by indications the country’s economy is holding up better than expected. Activity is now thought to have expanded 0.1% in the final three months of last year, up from a previous estimate of no growth at all. Gross domestic product growth in January has been estimated at 0.3% after dropping 0.5% in December.

This resilience is bolstering expectations the Bank of England will maintain aggressive interest rate hikes despite concerns about the health of the global banking sector. Rising rates can boost the domestic currency because they help attract foreign investors searching for higher returns.

Inflation in the United Kingdom also jumped to an annual rate of 10.4% in February, underscoring the need for the Bank of England to maintain its tough approach.

The pound plunged close to $1.03 in September 2022 after the Truss government unveiled plans to boost borrowing while slashing taxes, unleashing panic in financial markets that fueled fears of a recession in the United Kingdom.

The International Monetary Fund predicted in January that the UK economy would contract by 0.6% this year, while all other advanced economies would grow, if only slightly.

“There was a lot of pessimism being priced into the pound,” said Francesco Pesole, a currency strategist at ING.

But the sharp pullback in energy prices and China’s reopening have provided some relief about the economic outlook since the start of the year.

“There was a big re-rating of growth expectations around Europe, and that impacted the UK,” Pesole said.

The euro has also been lifted by these dynamics, rising 2.3% against the US dollar in 2023. The pound’s rally has been sharper in large part because its 2022 declines were more severe, according to Pesole.

Both currencies have been aided by the greenback’s sharp drop from highs reached last September as recession fears have percolated in the United States.

A lack of clarity around the Federal Reserve’s next steps has also restrained the dollar in recent weeks. Investor speculation has increased that the Fed could pause or stop rate hikes due to concerns about the economy following the failure of Silicon Valley Bank last month.

Jordan Rochester, a currency strategist at Nomura, said he thinks the pound could rise to $1.30 this year and “potentially higher.” But he still sees risks given the uncertainty surrounding the Bank of England’s plans and how rate rises will feed back through the country’s economy. And Pesole cautioned that currency fluctuations are often overdone when markets are choppy, as they are now.

“In a volatile market environment, moves are exacerbated,” he said.

#110. 'I tell my son it's just fireworks': Life inside Yemeni city besieged by Houthis

Houthi attacks on Taiz have killed more than 140 civilians since August, local government officials say

In the Yemeni city of Taiz, ringed by jagged mountains and Houthi rebel fighters, Ashwaq has only one way to try to soothe her frightened child - lie.

"When my 11-year-old son hears a loud sound, I tell him, 'Don't be afraid, it's just fireworks'," she says. "I don't want him to panic, but deep down I know he is old enough to understand what is happening around him. He has lived through war for most of his life."

Taiz has long been a prize - and a front line - in Yemen's civil war.

Houthi forces tightened their grip last month, fully encircling the government-controlled city, and the population - estimated to be between 600,000 and 1 million.

Since then, Yemen's third-largest city has been cut off from the world, and its residents have been largely unheard. We reached seven people inside the city who mapped out the anatomy of the siege.

They gave detailed accounts of the Houthis' death grip on Taiz. Only a minimal amount of food can get in. Prices for staples like rice, sugar and cooking oil have doubled or even trebled. Essential supplies, like medicine, water and fuel, are dwindling.

Burning piles of rubbish are producing toxic smoke that hangs over the city

On streets that used to hum with traffic and background chatter, a silence has fallen. Schools are closed and restaurants are shuttered. Suffering is being heaped on suffering. Piles of rubbish are trapped inside the city, alongside the people. When residents try to get rid of it by burning it, there is a toxic smoke. Even the air is now a Houthi weapon.

From positions on the hills, rebel fighters - and snipers - can wage war on the city below.

Estimates for the death toll vary widely. At the Office for Siege Affairs, a local government department, they say 148 civilians have been killed by Houthi attacks since August.

Some of those we contacted in Taiz do not want to be identified because they fear for their safety. Many in the city are reluctant to even speak on the phone because the Houthis control key mobile carriers.

"Sometimes it feels like the city has become a huge prison," says Ashwaq, a teacher and mother of three, who only wanted her first name to be used. She yearns for the old Taiz.

"It used to be a lively city," she says. "People loved life, worked hard, visited one another, and had hopes for the future, despite all the difficulties. Now, we hardly go out anymore. The [Houthi] shelling can happen any time, and sniper fire near the front lines makes even a short trip dangerous."

She keeps her son and two elder daughters indoors.

"I worry about them every single day, every hour, and honestly every minute," she says.

Taiz has been a major battleground in Yemen's civil war for more than a decade

War is not new to Taiz. The strategic city is the gateway to Aden - the temporary seat of the internationally recognised government - and to southern Yemen.

Houthi rebels - backed by Iran - reached the outskirts of Taiz in 2015, cutting off roads, and laying siege from three sides. A year earlier, they had seized Yemen's capital, Sanaa.

More than a decade on, the government, which is weak and fractured, has been unable to reclaim the capital and much of north-western Yemen. That's despite the backing of Saudi Arabia, and its air strikes.

In recent months, the conflict has flared up and expanded after a four-year lull. The Houthis made a lightning advance, taking territory from the government, and striking Saudi Arabia with missiles and drones.

A long-running civil war has become a threat to regional stability.

An emergency room doctor, whom we are calling Yaser, bears witness to that.

Among the war wounded recently brought to his hospital were four children, shot by Houthi snipers. They usually aim for the head and chest, he tells us. Two of the four children died.

The hardest thing he faces, as a father himself, is treating wounded children. "I have to rise to the responsibility, compose myself and support the families," he says. "It's a difficult situation that I cannot even put into words, when I see a father looking at his badly wounded child right in front of him."

Most of the injured are victims of shelling. Reaching the hospital can be an ordeal. Some families have to walk for hours from outlying areas.

They arrive exhausted, like the doctors who will treat them, and who are struggling with "everything", says Yaser. Even sterilising fluid for surgical instruments is in short supply.

He tells us the hospital is running on empty, and can carry on for two more weeks, "maybe".

Taiz's hospitals have lost 70% of their capacity and 120 health centres have closed, its governor says

If the emergency rooms are busy, so are the graveyards.

"Funerals heading to the cemeteries have become the defining scene in Taiz, around the clock," says Taiz's Governor, Nabil Shamsan.

It's not just bombs and bullets that kill, he tells us.

"The people of Taiz are losing their lives due to supply shortages, lack of food, and loss of income," he says. "Every single day, we bury dozens of Taiz residents."

We have not been able to independently verify these figures.

He remains inside the city, and inside the siege. "I live among my people," he says.

On a video call, he looks weary but delivers a lengthy chronicle of a city being starved of oxygen - literally.

He tells us that hospitals have lost 70% of their capacity, and 120 health centres have closed. Diseases are spreading, neonatal deaths have increased, and malnutrition is surging. Hundreds of kidney patients are not getting dialysis because they can't get to hospitals or because fuel is short when they do.

The Houthi leader, Abdul Malik al-Houthi, has denied Taiz is besieged. Late last month, he claimed that routes remained open for food, medicine and humanitarian aid.

That is denied by the government, residents of Taiz, aid agencies and the United Nations.

What's at stake in the battle for the city is more than territory, says Shamsan.

He says the Houthis - who mostly follow a branch of Shia Islam - want to change the character of this predominantly Sunni city, long known as a seat of learning and a bastion of resistance.

"Everyone knows that Taiz is a hub of culture, a city characterised by freedom, political pluralism and all forms of civic life," he says. In sharp contrast, he says, the Houthis aim to impose their strict religious doctrine and drag the city "back to the dark ages".

Whatever the pressure on the city and its people, the governor insists the Houthis will never win.

"Until the population perishes," he replies.

Essential supplies, like medicine, water and fuel, are dwindling

They are defending their city, alongside relatives, friends, neighbours, and five brigades of pro-government troops, who can't do the job on their own.

They don't have heavy weapons or enough numbers for the expanding front line.

Training camps have sprung up for volunteers, but the lessons can be as brief as a day or two before they are sent to join the fight.

Akram (not his real name) is already a veteran. He's 31, with a child on the way.

When Taiz was fully encircled before, in 2015, he broke away from his studies and took up arms. In that round of fighting, the Houthis gained control of his district on the outskirts, and he was uprooted. He hasn't seen his old home since then.

Last month, when Taiz was completely cut off once again, he bought a rifle, paying about $1,200 (Â£910) for a Chinese version of a Kalashnikov. It used to cost less than half that sum.

He tells us this fight is different to 2015.

"This time the Houthis have new technology and new missiles," he says. "They can wage a war in the air with drones. We fear the death of our families and our civilians, and ourselves."

He has already lost four friends in the past month, who left behind wives and children.

But Akram, and many others, see defending Taiz as their duty. He says that "all the sons of Taiz" will fight the Houthis "and their extremism".

The armed group is currently designated as a foreign terrorist organisation by the US (which has gone back and forth on the issue) but not by the UK or the European Union.

"They say God gave them the right to rule everyone in Yemen. We don't accept that," says Akram. "We live in freedom. We cannot surrender; we cannot leave. We cannot breathe without our city."

Almost 350,000 people have been displaced by the fighting in recent months

But it can be suffocating inside Taiz, where nightfall brings no rest.

"Every night we have bombing and shelling," says Ahmad Al-Basha, a photographer. "I think the Houthis prefer to fight at night. Maybe they don't want us and our children to sleep inside our homes."

The people of Taiz feel let down, he tells us, by their government and by the international community. "We feel frustrated and sad," he says, "because we don't see any move to help us."

Many believe the Houthis will keep a chokehold on Taiz from the outside, and exert significant control over the city without trying to enter it.

Ahmad believes it won't come to that but fears the worst if it does.

"Maybe they will clean Taiz of any resistance, kill people and settle scores with anyone who is against them," he says.

His greatest concern is for his children, who cannot escape the sounds of war.

Like Ashwaq, the teacher, he tries to explain it away.

"I tell them it's a party," he says, "and if it's really loud, I say it's a big party."

As Taiz endures bombing, shelling, and a sense of abandonment laced with growing hunger, Ashwaq has a parent's plea for the international community.

"Please don't forget Taiz," she says. "We are ordinary families. We want our children to study, play and dream like other children around the world. We are not just numbers in news reports."

Weakened by the midterm elections, President Trump offered hugs, arm pats and smiles to autocrats from Russia and China in contrast to the threats he is making to ascendant Democrats in Washington.

#107. Spain frees wealthy US-sought activist on bail as Irish police raid Dublin home

Pro-Palestinian donor James ‘Fergie’ Chambers released as court considers extradition request

In a blur of events surrounding the Trump administration’s effort to extradite James “Fergie” Chambers, Spain released the wealthy US activist and donor on bail even as Irish police raided a Dublin house owned by Chambers and his partner, Stella Schnabel, acting on a request from US authorities.

Chambers’s case is the first known instance of the US seeking to extradite a citizen over alleged support for Hamas, an accusation apparently tied to an international money-laundering charge in the criminal complaint underpinning the extradition request and donations to organizations helping Palestinians in Gaza.

Speaking outside Madrid’s Aranjuez prison upon his release on Friday, Chambers called the charges against him “baseless”, adding that his release was “hopefully the first step in recognizing that internationally”.

Chambers also said: “Spain is a country that has stood on the right side of this most important issue of our time, that has stood on the right side of the genocide, and where this is some reason and some justice.”

An appeals court ordered the conditional release on Friday due to issues surrounding the legally required US translation of the extradition request’s core documents into Spanish, first reported on by the Guardian. He had been detained since 10 July, when Spanish authorities arrested him in Ibiza.

The order asserts that the translation issues cause “a diminishment, at least for the time being, in the solidity or foundation of the extradition request”.

Chambers’s release follows a separate decision on Thursday by the three-judge panel at the high court tasked with deciding on the extradition to pause the proceedings in order to evaluate the impact of the alterations the US made to the original, machine-made translation of the extradition request into Spanish.

The Guardian detailed four examples of the alterations on 7 November and the US admitted to tampering with the translation, calling them “substantial [and] inadvertent” on 17 November – and citing the same four examples. Chambers’s defense team has asserted for weeks that there are as many as 30 such alterations.

The changes introduced prejudicial notions with specific legal implications that in turn wound up in the Spanish prosecutor’s report on the case, Aitor Martínez, a member of Chambers’s defense team, told the Guardian earlier this week. The judges must consider the prosecutor’s report in deciding on the extradition request.

The high court was expected to rule on the extradition this week, following a 13 November hearing. But as of Thursday’s decision, the deadline has been extended indefinitely as the judges evaluate the translation, including a 345-page report submitted by the defense team that identifies 30 “incriminating changes” Trump administration officials made to a translation prepared by DeepL, “an AI-first translation platform”, according to its website.

The decision also gives the prosecutor an opportunity to address the issue in writing, after dismissing it in the 13 November hearing by referring to “supposed translation errors”.

Chambers’s views and donations to progressive and humanitarian projects have been extensively profiled in US media, including his controversial public comments on Israel and Gaza. A week after the 7 October 2023 Hamas attacks on Israel, he posted on X about Israel’s response: “Stop calling it ‘collective punishment’/There’s nothing to be ‘punished’ for/No faction of the Palestinian resistance, Hamas or other, has done *anything* wrong/It’s simply genocide/Like it has been.”

The heir to $250m from his family’s business, Cox Enterprises, is being sought in extradition because of such views, his defense team has asserted throughout the case.

Meanwhile, the Irish Garda Síochána, acting on a request from US authorities, raided a Dublin house owned by Chambers and Schnabel on Wednesday, destroying the front door and removing electronic items and other possessions, according to their attorney in Ireland, Ciarán Mulholland.

Schnabel, who is five months pregnant, said: “It is difficult to see this raid as anything other than a desperate fishing expedition by the US authorities to find evidence to bolster their case against Fergie.”

The attorney told the Guardian that the raid was the result of a “mutual legal assistance” request from the US – and that such a request “wouldn’t give [the Garda Síochána] permission to kick in the doors of someone’s dwelling in Ireland”.

“It’s unusual for the Irish state to be acting in such a clandestine manner,” Mulholland said. He has asked authorities to produce a search warrant and an itemized list of what was taken. “My fear is that whatever was taken has been put on a plane and taken to the US,” he added.

“This [extradition case] shows the complete disregard for due process … that the Trump administration seems to have,” Mulholland said.

#105. A new Nepal, an old neighbour - Balendra Shah's India test on first visit

The 36-year-old rapper-turned-politician arrives in Delhi on Sunday for a two-day visit

Nepal Prime Minister Balendra Shah's visit to India has raised hopes of a reset, marking the first high-level engagement between leaders of Nepal's new political generation and its giant neighbour.

It is Shah's first bilateral visit since his Rastriya Swatantra Party (RSP) swept to power in March, six months after a Gen Z uprising against corruption and nepotism toppled K P Sharma Oli's government.

The 36-year-old rapper-turned-politician arrives in Delhi on Sunday for a two-day visit, during which he will meet his Indian counterpart Narendra Modi and other senior officials.

The talks are expected to cover a broad range of issues, including economic and energy cooperation, the opening of new air corridors, and a reconstruction assistance package following the devastating flash floods that struck Nepal in August.

The disaster killed more than 1,450 in the Himalayan nation, while thousands remain missing and unaccounted for.

India and Nepal share close ethnic, cultural, religious and economic ties, with a largely open border stretching more than 1,750km (1,080 miles).

Wedged between India and China, Nepal is a strategic buffer and, analysts say, its stability has direct implications for India's security.

Nepal suffered devastating floods in August that killed more than 1,450 people and left thousands missing

But over the decades the relationship has been strained by territorial disputes, allegations of political interference and resentment over what many in Nepal see as the dominant 'Big Brother' attitude of Delhi.

Nevertheless, analysts see Shah's visit as an opportunity for a reset.

"The new Nepali leadership says they do not have the political baggage of the past and they want to focus on development, diplomacy and economic partnership," Ranjit Rae, a former Indian ambassador to Nepal tells BBC.

"They seem to be more pragmatic, less ideological. I personally feel it's an optimistic moment," he adds.

Since Nepal abolished monarchy and became a democratic republic in 2008 three political parties dominated the governance.

Delhi has maintained close contacts with all of them â the Communist Party of Nepal (CPN â UML), the Nepali Congress and the Communist Party of Nepal (Maoist Centre).

Nepal's communist parties were seen as closer to India's rival China, owing to ideological ties, and often criticised Delhi over what they viewed as unequal bilateral agreements.

Due to Nepal's strategic location, both Delhi and Beijing compete for influence in Kathmandu, leading to accusations the two Asian giants are meddling in the internal affairs of Nepal.

But Nepal's ties with India extend beyond diplomacy and strategic interests.

The Hindu-majority nations share deep social ties, with families straddling the open border and communities interacting daily.

Bilateral trade exceeds $9bn, heavily skewed in India's favour - a longstanding source of resentment in Kathmandu. India is also Nepal's main supplier of petroleum products and several food items, and a key transit route for its trade with the rest of the world.

India and Nepal share an open border and longstanding economic ties

One key area of co-operation is hydropower. India has approved projects to import up to 1,200MW of electricity from Nepal and is looking to expand imports further.

"India has a huge demand for electricity and Nepal has the potential to generate due to its water resources and geography. It's not just bilateral, Nepal also exports electricity to Bangladesh via India," says Rae.

India has often been among the first countries to assist Nepal during major natural disasters, including the 2015 earthquake and the flash floods in August this year.

Despite the close links, Nepal and India have their differences.

In 2019, Nepal was furious after India published a map that included areas claimed by Kathmandu, in a western region close to the border with China, as part of its territory. That raised heightened awareness in Kathmandu about the region.

The diplomatic wrangle escalated after Nepal published its own map which included those disputed areas.

"I hope Nepal's prime minister raises the border issue with Delhi this time. There may not be a resolution immediately, but they should start negotiations to settle the issue," Kanak Mani Dixit, a veteran Nepali political analyst, told the BBC.

Nepal has also long urged India to open additional air entry routes, allowing large aircraft to fly in from the west to its new airports at Bhairahawa, near the Buddhist holy site of Lumbini, and Pokhara, a major tourist destination.

Access to Indian airspace would mean shorter flights at lower cost and, with more flights, the airports would become commercially viable.

Some in Nepal also believe that Delhi is wary of the fact that the Gautam Buddha international airport was constructed by China's Northwest Civil Aviation Airport and the airport in Pokhara was built with Chinese financial assistance.

Shah has yet to make any overtures to Beijing, but sent a 20-member delegation of party MPs to China for an 11-day visit from 15 November, ahead of his trip to India - seen as an effort to balance ties with Nepal's two powerful neighbours.

Shah had pledged not to travel abroad during his first year in office, but devastating flash floods in August have changed the calculus.

His government estimates reconstruction will cost nearly $5bn, while international aid pledged so far has fallen short of expectations.

But some observers in Nepal believe that Shah's preparations for the high-stake India visit have been less than thorough.

"Prime Minister Shah and his advisors are young and fresh, but they do lack experience. Repeatedly they have exhibited a lack of gravitas in governance, which would be essential for diplomacy with an overwhelmingly powerful neighbour," says Dixit.

Shah is already facing criticism over some of his government's recent decisions, including to remove police chief Dan Bahadur from his post after he allegedly refused to comply with directives from the Prime Minister's office.

Police were sent to some news organisations to detain journalists for their news coverage triggering accusations of curtailing press freedom.

While Kathmandu has ambitions in advancing its ties with India, the real test for Shah would be to return home with tangible outcomes. Securing concrete commitments from Delhi on financial aid would be seen as a diplomatic success and could help blunt growing domestic criticism.

#101. French police officers found guilty of racist assault on black music producer

Beating of Michel Zecler in 2020 sparked outrage over police brutality after it was caught on security cameras

Three French police officers who beat a black music producer they said was not wearing a face mask during the Covid pandemic have been found guilty of intentional, racist violence.

The brutal assault on Michel Zecler, now 47, was caught on security cameras in his building in 2020 and triggered outrage in France over police brutality. The trial this month was considered a turning point for the recognition of racist violence.

The officers were sentenced to between one and two years in prison, with additional years suspended. They were banned for life from serving in the police.

The officers had stopped Zecler outside his Paris music studio on 21 November 2020, accusing him of failing to wear a mandatory face mask. They pushed him into the entrance of his office and violently beat him in an assault recorded by his studio’s security cameras.

Zecler sustained about 30 punches and truncheon blows to the face and body over a five-minute period, according to the investigation. He was left with craniofacial trauma, a wound to the lip and scalp, and a complete rupture of his biceps tendon, leaving him unable to work for 45 days.

Zecler told the court the officers used a racist slur against him on several occasions during the beating. He said the attack had “completely devastated him” and had left a lasting physical and psychological impact.

The three officers, Aurelien Lapersonne, 29, Pierre Plantier, 37, and Philippe Tondeux, 50, were found guilty of breaking into Zecler’s studio and committing intentional violence in a group. The judges decided racism was an aggravating factor in the attack.

The officers were also found guilty of forgery after they tried to cover up their actions by falsifying an official police report saying Zecler had been violent towards them. The police used that report to accuse Zecler of attacking them and trying to grab one of their weapons, but the video footage did not back up the claims, showing he was not violent.

The state prosecutor said that without the CCTV footage, the false police report on Zecler could have resulted in him being convicted of attacking a police officer in what would have been a serious miscarriage of justice.

The state prosecutor, who asked not to be named in the media, told the court the case had caused “immense harm to Michel Zecler but also to society as a whole”.

She said the officers had “betrayed” their uniform. She told the judges: “I ask you to believe Michel Zecler, to recognise him as a victim of these racist insults.”

The three police officers, who had not realised their attack was being recorded on security cameras, had apologised in court for beating Zecler, but denied racism.

Zecler’s lawyers and supporters said the trial had shone a spotlight on police violence in France, which victims and researchers say is systemic but authorities and law enforcement unions say is limited to isolated cases. Many members of the public who had come to support Zecler and watch the trial said it was an important moment in confronting institutionalised racism.

One of Zecler’s lawyers, Serge Money, said while summing up the case: “A verdict can force a society to really look at an injustice that up until now had been tolerated, minimised, and I dare to use the word: institutionalised.”

He said: “The Zecler trial is the trial of impunity, for all those who before him and after him will experience the same thing.”

Money told the court: “I want you to give voice to all those who didn’t have security cameras, and to all those who were not believed.”

Nine young performers who had been rehearsing in the basement at Zecler’s studio had been violently arrested when they came to assist Zecler.

Their lawyer, Seydi Ba, told the court that France needed to know how “police violence, lies and racism” would be considered by a court.

#100. These dead pets were meant to be cremated - then their bodies appeared on a livestream

Revecca Calling is devastated after hearing about the bodies that were found and wants to know if her dog, Olaf, really was cremated

A man â bald, goateed, wearing red-tinted sunglasses â walks down a cracked back road, guiding the camera towards a cluster of plastic-wrapped objects that spill out of the tall grass.

Names are written on the packages â Brandon; Ira; Lucky; Sweet â and numbers. One says 29kg (4.5 stone). Another says 2.5kg. Through the plastic you can make out what appears to be fur, claws and a flattened ear.

It's a mass dumping, the man explains. "We have about 25 animals here that have been dumped in bags. A couple of cats, some dogs."

Warning: This story contains images some might find upsetting

In the weeks since that video was streamed on Facebook, Chris and Geraldine Taylor, who run animal rescue NGO Luna and Furry Siblings (LAFS), say they have uncovered 15 dumping sites in the suburbs of Bulacan, near the Philippines' capital of Manila.

In total they've unearthed the remains of some 170 dead pets. Belgian Malinois, golden retrievers, pugs, puppies and cats have been found dumped off service roads, sealed in septic tanks, floating in rivers and buried in vacant lots.

Many of these animals are thought to have been sent to cremation businesses in the expectation they would be turned to ash.

Now, many grieving owners are having to confront a distressing reality: their beloved pets may not have received the send-off they paid for. The urn in their home may not contain their pet at all. And they may in fact see their so-called fur child again, jettisoned like common waste on the side of a highway.

One saw the livestream and wrote on Facebook: "I think one of those pets is mine." Below, they attached a photo of a beaming Alsatian.

A string of grisly discoveries in the suburbs near Manila has put thousands of grieving pet owners on edge

When Chris arrived on that back road three weeks ago, he couldn't have known he was uncorking a scandal that would see at least one business reportedly shut, four people placed under investigation, and a personal invite to sit down with a senator to discuss animal-cruelty laws.

Chris, a former police officer, and his wife are retired, but only formally. For the past three years, they've been running an animal rescue shelter out of their home in San Jose del Monte, 30km (18.6 miles) north-east of central Manila.

On 30 October, he'd driven into town to report a case of animal neglect at the local district office. When he arrived, he was told the official in charge had just left to respond to another complaint: a report of dumped animals on a nearby roadside.

Chris had seen this once before: 14 dogs that were found and soon reburied. This time, he followed the official to the scene and started documenting â taking photos, scanning the bodies for microchips and uploading footage to his Facebook page.

"I figured there's got to be more to this," he explains. "So we put out word: if anybody knows anything, any other dump locations... just report it. Let us know."

In the days that followed, tip-offs started trickling in. More dumpsites emerged.

"The long vacant lot on the left side... that's where the smell comes from," read one message.

"When we ran to this place it smelled of rotting animals," read another, accompanied by a photo of a map with a location circled in red.

Chris Taylor, pictured here with a dog unrelated to the scandal, has uncovered the remains of some 170 dead pets in recent weeks

By crowdsourcing intel in this way, and collaborating with authorities, LAFS has so far uncovered a total of 15 dumpsites, containing an estimated 170 animals. "Some of them were just bags of bones," Chris says. "Others were liquefied."

LAFS has meticulously documented the crime scenes. Often wearing little more for protection than an N95 mask and rubber gloves, Chris crouches in the underbrush and slices open discarded plastic bags, picking through the contents while Geraldine holds the camera.

Their Facebook videos have racked up more than 30 million views. Commenters ask Chris if he has seen their pet among the bodies, or if he can turn a carcass over so they can check for identifying marks.

Some are successful. On that first night in October, all 25 bodies were returned to their owners.

There's a question which hangs uncomfortably over every dumpsite: who would do something like this?

As more owners came forward, a common denominator emerged. Many said they'd taken the animals to be incinerated at the same place: a crematorium called Ahjay Paws in the city of Bacoor, not far from Manila.

"So I posted: anybody that's had Ahjay's services... these might be your animals," says Chris. "And man, it just flooded in."

Ahjay Paws, the company that was supposed to cremate Olaf, is under investigation

The Philippines has long been a dog-loving nation, but since the Covid-19 pandemic the "fur parenting" community has exploded.

A 2021 study found the country had the highest rate of dog ownership in the Asia-Pacific. Since then, the pet dog population has increased 4.7% year on year, according to another study, exceeding 21 million in 2025.

As the numbers soar, businesses have stepped up to address an inevitable implication: more deaths.

"Cremation here in the Philippines has become quite a big thing with the boom of the pet industry," says Anna Cabrera, director at the Philippine Animal Welfare Society (Paws). "There's even a ceremony where they hold wakes for the pets."

These events offer grieving owners a last chance to see their beloved pets before they're taken to a crematorium and transmuted into an urnful of ashes.

Pet care - including for those that have died - has become a booming industry in the Philippines

Revecca Calling, who lives south of Bulacan in the city of Cavite, says she paid Ahjay Paws 8,500 Philippine pesos (Â£100; $133) to cremate her dog Olaf in January. The price was calculated according to weight, and included a 30-minute viewing where she could say her final goodbye.

Calling says Ahjay Paws told her they couldn't record the cremation, as it might endanger the staff. They took away Olaf's body, and a few days later handed back a small urn that purportedly contained her cremated remains.

When nine months later Calling heard that dozens of animals' bodies had been found dumped on an empty plot of land, potentially at the behest of a pet cremation business, she was devastated.

"Pet owners entrust these services with something very important: the remains of a beloved family member," she says. "We need to know what happened."

Pets have been found dumped in bags like this - with labels giving their names and weights

Earlier this month, the Philippines' National Bureau of Investigation (NBI) filed criminal complaints against four individuals in connection with dumped pets. Among them is the owner of Ahjay Paws and two people who ran Bulacan's Pet HPHT Crematory.

According to the NBI, Ahjay Paws had initially arranged to acquire a cremation machine from Pet HPHT â but when it didn't arrive, they opted to send the pet remains to Pet HPHT instead. When the volume of those remains became more than Pet HPHT could handle, that firm paid a third party to bury them.

One interpretation is that the dumping incidents are a result of logistical failures: two businesses that bit off more than they could chew and took desperate measures to make the problem go away.

But there's another theory: that the dead pets could be evidence of a lucrative racket, with people's money taken to incinerate their pets but the animals then being dumped instead.

There is a word for this kind of deception in Philippine law â estafa; where a perpetrator knowingly misleads a victim to financially defraud them â and the NBI say they are investigating it as a possibility in this case.

Two weeks ago, Ahjay Paws' owner denied responsibility for the dumping in San Jose del Monte, according to local media. The company is apparently no longer operating, and the BBC has been unable to contact it.

The BBC has also reached out to Pet HPHT and invited it to respond. On Facebook, the firm said the dumping allegations were "completely false and have no basis".

The pet-dumping scandal, in which Olaf may have been a victim, has blown up into a national controversy

Another grieving pet owner, Emelia Legada, says she paid Ahjay Paws 10,000 Philippine pesos (Â£120; $160) to cremate her dog Harvey six months ago.

"I just can't accept the pain of not knowing what might have happened to him," she says. "I really want to confirm whether Harvey was properly cremated, and whether the ashes I received were truly his."

Many are wrestling with the same uncertainty. As Cabrera puts it: "God knows what that was inside those urns."

In response to those anxieties, a veterinarian in nearby Quezon City has started offering complimentary forensic ash testing. For those whose pets have been found among the bodies, other crematoriums have offered to do the job properly, and for free.

The animal welfare society, Paws, is volunteering legal services to help people file complaints with the authorities, and has received more than 150 requests so far. It is also calling for a nationwide audit of all pet cremation businesses.

On 2 November, Percival Vilar CendaÃ±a of the Philippines' progressive Akbayan Party appeared on national television. He was there to speak about the pet-dumping scandal, which, in the span of a few days, had flared into a national disgrace.

"Our worst fears have been confirmed," he told the Bilyonaryo news channel. "Now we see that these are not isolated incidents. It is true that this is a syndicate that is conducting widespread operations."

Neither the police nor the NBI have confirmed that the dumpings were carried out by a "syndicate", nor that they were "widespread". Many details surrounding the case remain murky. But the hysteria is real â and CendaÃ±a was giving voice to a common sentiment.

"Those who deceived these poor fur parents should be jailed," he said.

At least three individuals have surrendered to the NBI and been charged with violating the city's Code of Sanitation, the Ecological Solid Waste Management Act, and the Sanitation Code of the Philippines.

But many feel this doesn't go far enough, and say it fails to hold anyone liable for the way the animals' remains were treated.

Cabrera says the country's animal-cruelty law doesn't cover the treatment of animals after death. And "there's no crime if there's no law".

The scandal has prompted a groundswell to change that. Lawmakers like CendaÃ±a are pushing for a national commission on animal welfare to ensure their dignity in life and death.

Meanwhile, Cabrera says Paws will seek "moral and exemplary" damages on behalf of aggrieved pet owners.

Chris Taylor dealing with items resembling clothing found at a dumpsite (blurred at source)

One woman whose dog was suspected to have been found among the dumped bodies told the BBC she couldn't speak about it because she was "feeling weak".

"I haven't slept for three days from all the crying," she said.

Chris has also been losing sleep, taking calls from grieving pet owners who have watched his videos. "Hundreds of people call me in tears, just sobbing," he says.

"They're in so much pain. And half the time you're telling them: 'you're not going to find your pet. It's been too long; we're not going to be able to identify it.' And it just crushes them."

As well as new laws, Chris wants stricter enforcement of existing rules. He wants to know how he managed to unearth so much in the space of a fortnight, and why people keep coming to him saying they don't trust the police to take their concerns seriously, he says.

The BBC contacted Philippine National Police (PNP) for comment, but didn't hear back. Earlier this month, PNP chief Jose Melencio Nartatez Jr said authorities were determined to hold accountable those who allegedly took advantage of grieving pet owners, saying such exploitation "must be addressed with the full force of the law", according to ABS-CBN.

"Fraud is not measured solely by the amount of money taken, but also by the damage inflicted on the people whose trust has been betrayed," Nartatez said.

"To the affected pet owners, we recognise the pain and distress this incident has caused your families."

Yet the police themselves have come under official scrutiny.

On Monday, the National Police Commission announced an investigation into officers in Cavite and Bulacan after they suggested LAFS might be involved in the pet dumping because they were often first on the scene. The officers did not present evidence for their claims. The investigation also covers reports that officers visited the Taylors' home and took photographs.

Meanwhile, countless grieving pet owners are suspended in purgatory: desperate for closure on what happened to their dogs and cats, and calling on authorities to do more.

"We want the case to be properly investigated and those responsible to be held accountable," says Calling.â¯ "I believe that no secret remains hidden forever."

Legada, for her part, says: "I just want to find out what really happened to Harvey and where his remains are. I want to know whether he was properly cremated."

She also wants people to know: "You can't trust anyone these days."

#97. Tourists are throwing fewer coins into Rome’s Trevi fountain – is €2 entry fee to blame?

Charity that collects the coins says donations are down by £200,000 after a barrier was put in place to restrict access

When Jeff Clinton, an American visiting Rome with his wife for the first time, found a US one-cent coin on a street close to Termini train station, he knew precisely what he was going to do with it. “I’ll throw it into the Trevi fountain,” said Clinton, holding the copper coin in the palm of his hand as the couple admired the landmark.

He was well aware of the tradition among tourists of tossing a coin over their shoulder and into the waters of the baroque masterpiece, in a ritual fabled to ensure a return visit to the city or make other dreams come true.

But the only thing holding him back from partaking in the gesture was the distance: the steps towards the fountain’s sunken basin are separated by a low barrier and accessible during the day only by Rome’s residents or visitors who pay €2 (£1.64).

The fee, in place since February, has been partly blamed for taking some of the allure out of the coin-throwing tradition. The Rome unit of the church-run charity Caritas, which sweeps up the Trevi fountain’s coins, usually brings in more than €1.4m a year. But it noted a shortfall of about €200,000 between January and September compared with the same period in 2025.

“The closure of the direct access to the Trevi fountain might be a factor,” Giustino Trincia, the director of Caritas Roma, told Il Messaggero. The charity uses the donations to fund food banks and other welfare projects.

Clinton did contemplate asking someone in the basin area to throw the coin into the fountain on his behalf, but in the end slipped it back into his pocket.

The shift towards contactless payments and mobile phone wallets is also believed to have eroded Caritas’s collections.

Eric and Patty, a young couple from New York, said they might have thrown a coin, but did not have any change. Neither did they want to pay the €2 fee to get close to the basin. “It’s such a breathtaking monument, we’re happy just to take it in from here,” said Patty as they posed for selfies.

Alan and Ying, visiting Rome from Australia, did pay the fee. While they had heard that throwing coins “is what people do”, they decided not to indulge because “nobody else was really doing it”.

“I always carry cash with me because you never know when you might need it,” said Ying. “But we didn’t see anyone else doing it, so we didn’t either.”

A spokesperson for Caritas Rome told the Guardian the charity was not making any further public statements about the dwindling coin collection. The spokesperson said the collection so far this year did not take into account foreign currency coins, so the hit might not be so bad.

A digest of the morning's main headlines from the Europe edition emailed direct to you every week day

Still, the matter is understood to have caused friction with Rome’s council, which generated €435,194 from the access fee within the first month of its inception.

The initiative is aimed at curbing overcrowding at the monument while raising funds for the upkeep of the fountain and other Rome monuments. No further figures have been published, but with 4.4 million tourists flocking to the Trevi in 2025, the fee had been forecast to generate a tidy income for the council each year.

A team of stewards staff the ticketing system and monitor the flow of the crowd in the basin area. Nicolas Romero does shifts of seven or eight hours. He said people quite often asked him for change in order to throw a coin. “But many don’t even seem to know about the tradition,” he said. “This might also explain the decline.”

The gesture is believed to have its origins in an ancient pagan ritual offering to the water gods, although the custom of throwing coins only really took off globally after the 1954 romantic comedy film, Three Coins in the Fountain.

Fiona, the hippo whose premature birth and fight for survival at the Cincinnati Zoo turned her into a global sensation nearly a decade ago, died Thursday from complications after swallowing a knit hat that was accidentally flung into her enclosure. #95. ‘So what if there’s a hole? It hasn’t collapsed’: Kyiv residents unbothered by Russian attacks on city’s bridges

Despite dodging tears in the tarmac, people are confident in city’s infrastructure – and will not worry about what has not happened yet

Mykola expects to drive his battered beige passenger minibus 20 to 24 times a day past the holes in the road on Kyiv’s damaged Northern Bridge that were caused by a Russian ballistic missile attack on Tuesday.

Buses are now the only motor transport allowed across the normally busy multi-lane crossing, though on their way into the city centre they have to skirt the tears in the tarmac, using the only functioning lane to get by.

“So what if there’s a hole? The bridge hasn’t collapsed. You need to know a bit about bridge construction,” says Mykola, 65, at the wheel of a half -full afternoon bus as it clatters on its way west.

Mykola operates a marshrutka, a single deck private bus, whose drivers are famed in Kyiv mythology for being able to multi-task: chatting, smoking and collecting fares, while driving as fast as possible to take passengers from rivals.

Passengers pay 25 hryvnia (42p) in cash and typically one passenger gets the unofficial job of taking money and handing back change on behalf of the driver. It turns out this has become the Guardian’s task, in order to conduct an interview.

“It would be very difficult to destroy the bridge. It would take a huge number of missiles, and if one or two hit it, it wouldn’t be the end of the world,” Mykola continues, with behind-the-wheel speculation that is so far accurate. By lunchtime the holes have been covered up, perhaps to avoid unsettling passengers eyeing the Dnipro River below.

Alongside its attacks on the capital’s electricity, heating and water systems, Russia has been attacking Kyiv’s bridges since early October, trying to cut off the city centre in the west from the newer, largely residential east, home to about a third of its population.

Moscow struck the bridges twice again this week, first on Tuesday – overshadowed by Ukraine’s partial destruction of the Kerch road bridge to Crimea. Then again during a noisy Thursday night, hours after Vladimir Putin promised there would be a “far more painful” response to the demolition of his pet project.

As of Friday, in an already traffic-snarled city, Kyiv’s other main road bridge, the Southern Bridge, was allowing motor traffic only from east to west. The new Podilskyi Bridge, damaged early on Friday, was similarly restricted, and the city’s mayor, Vitali Klitschko, told people to avoid car travel after a night in which six were killed.

The Northern Bridge remains open for public transport – plus any cyclists, scooter riders and walkers willing to brave the damp late autumn weather and ignore missile and drone alerts. But below, earthworks have begun to prepare for a floating pontoon bridge, one of eight planned, in case of the worst.

Dmytro Bespalov, a transport planning expert with Kyiv’s National University of Construction and Architecture, said it was difficult to destroy a suspension bridge like the Northern. They have “a strong construction, with a lot of repairability”, meaning damage can usually be fixed. Though there are holes in the roadway, its supporting structures were so far unscathed, he added.

Russia is thought to have been aiming ballistic missiles at the supporting pylon, requiring a precise and difficult to execute attack, though that is what Ukraine did at the culmination of its complex attack on the Kerch road bridge, which was brought down after a Flamingo cruise missile struck its supporting arch.

Yet, Bespalov cautioned that Kyiv was strategically vulnerable if Russia targets the bridges with “dozens or hundreds” of missiles, because the city did not have enough bridges from before the war. “In 2021, Kyiv was in third place in Europe for congestion on the TomTom traffic index,” he said, behind Istanbul and Moscow.

Particular concern is reserved for the pontoon bridge alternative. They can bear less traffic: “It would take 20 pontoon bridges to have half the current capacity,” Bespalov said – and they are far more vulnerable to missile and drone attack. “I don’t think it would be possible to use pontoons during a red [missile] or yellow [drone] alarm” – not least because vehicles could end up in the water.

At the first busy bus stop on the eastern left bank, Nataliya, 40, and Iryna, 39, are happy to make the crossing over the Northern Bridge. They say they have long got used to the difficulties of war: “The main thing is to get through the first 24 hours at home after the initial discomfort, and then we’ll adapt,” said Nataliya, a customer service specialist.

Iryna added: “If we start to despair, that’s exactly what they want. A bridge can be built. The main thing is that when the attack comes, there are no civilians or military personnel around. It’s just concrete, that’s all. They’ll either repair it or come up with something new.”

It is a core attitude of Kyiv’s remaining residents, who are already contending with hours of electricity shortages and a lack of heating, with the full chill of winter yet to arrive. People want to deal with problems as they arise rather than worrying excessively about what may come – though it is not always easy amid the daily attacks, including a daytime raid on Thursday in which a drone exploded near the Dutch embassy.

Oleksandr, 42, a construction worker, was waiting to get across the bridge. “If the bridges are destroyed we’ll have to leave,” he conceded. If that happened, he and his wife would move to her parents’ house across the river. “I bought them a generator there; they have their own water well. We’d already dug the well during the war. They used to have gas heating, but we’ve converted it to wood-burning. We’re preparing,” he said.

→ Baca artikel asli

#94. ANC fights to keep control of South Africa's key cities after election setback

The ANC got the most votes overall but lost control in more than half of the councils it won in 2021

The party that has dominated South Africa for more than 30 years is in a race against time to secure power-sharing deals in most of the country's cities after a massive drop in its support in this month's local elections.

The African National Congress (ANC) suffered its worstâ¯result since it came to power in 1994 when South Africa transitioned from the racist apartheid system to democracy.

It won a majority in just one of the country's nine provinces, Limpopo, down from six in the last elections. It won the most votes in just two of the country's eight cities, compared with six in 2021.

At the national level, the ANC is sharing power with its long-time rival, the Democratic Alliance (DA), but while there is still no official announcement, it appears that ANC party officials do not want to replicate this at the local level.

Parties have until 27 November to negotiate and form local governments.

While the ANC saw a massive decline in its support, it still got more votes than any other party, 31%, down from the 46% in the last local election.

But in the country's economic powerhouse, Gauteng province, which includes Johannesburg and Pretoria, the picture is different. The pro-business DA got the most votes in both cities, as well as the province's other main region, Ekurhuleni.

Nationally, the party got 25% of the vote, up from 22% five years ago.

It also retained its majority in its power base of Cape Town, and got the most votes in Nelson Mandela Bay, where Gqeberha (Port Elizabeth) is located.

The left-leaning uMkhonto weSizwe (MK) Party, led by former President Jacob Zuma, came third, with 10% of the vote. This is a repeat of its performance in the 2024 national election, when it came in third and became the official opposition after the DA joined the coalition government.

It emerged as the biggest party in KwaZulu-Natal province, which includes the port city of Durban, securing 34% of the vote. However, this leaves it reliant on agreeing a coalition with other parties.

The only party to secure an overall majority in a city was the DA in Cape Town.

The DA and ANC have had an uneasy alliance since they entered a coalition, along with eight other political parties, in 2024.

The two, long-standing rivals due to their opposing policies, were forced into an agreement after the ANC lost its parliamentary majority for the first time since the end of apartheid. This became known as the Government of National Unity (GNU).

Despite this, the two parties have continued to disagree on key issues, and both sides have threatened to rip up their power-sharing deal.

Ahead of the local elections, analysts predicted that the two parties could team up at municipal level, mirroring the national arrangement.

ActionSA leader Herman Mashaba (left) and ANC chairperson Gwede Mantashe (right) look set to reach on agreement on co-governing some of South Africa's biggest cities

The ANC seems to have rejected this, with state broadcaster SABC reporting that most of the party's top leaders see an agreement with the DA as a last resort.

Instead, the party has been courting smaller parties to try and form councils in South Africa's cities.

In Gauteng, numerous reports point to an arrangement between the party and the anti-immigrant ActionSA, which emerged as the third largest party in the province with 17%, leaving it as a potential kingmaker.

The DA has also been trying to reach arrangements with smaller parties, and the ANC, but looks set to assume the opposition benches at least in Gauteng.

ActionSA is unlikely to work with the DA as they cannot agree on the mayoral candidates in Johannesburg and Pretoria.

It's too early to tell. Neither the ANC nor DA have spoken publicly about the future of their arrangement on a national level amid the ongoing coalition talks but the ANC's reluctance to partner with the DA in key cities points to continued strain between the two.

While the GNU has remained intact since it was formed in 2024, there have been those in the ANC who have been angered by what they view as the DA's dual role as opposition and partner in the coalition government and have pushed for the arrangement to end.

A party insider told the Daily Maverick on Sunday that a nationwide agreement to work with the DA at municipal level "was overwhelmingly rejected" at a meeting held by ANC members.

While some experts predict that the DA may have to consider its position in the coalition government given the ANC's stance, political analyst Andre Duvenhage told the BBC there was a possibility that "a lot of asymmetrical coalitions" could emerge - parties working together in one area and being in opposition elsewhere.

Another political analyst, Sandile Swana, said that any party wanting to leave the GNU over the failure to secure deals locally would be "[shooting] yourself in the foot".

The decline in the ANC's support was seen as being mainly due to high levels of corruption and a loss of confidence in its ability to provide basic services, such as electricity, water and rubbish collection.

While some attribute the increase backing for the DA to its record of competent management in municipalities under its control, some have accused it of neglecting poorer areas and townships.

But the experience of coalition governments over the past 10 years does not inspire much confidence that things will improve.

Half of the country's cities have been governed through power-sharing agreements since 2016 and these types of arrangements have often been viewed as unstable and chaotic.

As agreements collapse and new ones are forged, the delivery of basic services has often been affected.

This has been particularly evident in Gauteng, where governance in all three cities has come under scrutiny in recent years partly because of unstable governments.

Experts agree that even after new arrangements take effect, it will take longer than a five-year term to resolve the issues facing many municipalities, particularly South Africa's biggest city Johannesburg.

"I think if we are really turning the ship around, we are talking about a 10-year period," Duvenhage said.

Swana echoed this, saying it would take a decade to "cleanse and purify" local councils and "reinstate them as "proper municipalities".

ANC under pressure to reverse South Africa decline

#92. French minister under fire for Playboy magazine cover

French government minister Marlene Schiappa has come under fire from members of her own party after appearing on the front cover of Playboy magazine.

Schiappa, who has been a government minister since 2017, appeared on the cover of the magazine to accompany a 12-page interview she did on women’s and LGBT rights. Schiappa, who is the current Minister for the Social Economy and French Associations, was photographed for the cover wearing a white dress.

Schiappa has been a long-time advocate for women’s rights and was appointed as the country’s first ever Gender Equality Minister in 2017. In this role, she successfully spearheaded a new sexual harassment law which allows for on the spot fines to be issued to men who catcall, harass or follow women on the street.

Her appearance has drawn criticism from political colleagues including French Prime Minister Elisabeth Borne.

Borne pulled up Schiappa over the cover, telling her it “wasn’t appropriate, especially during this period,” CNN affiliate BFMTV reported Saturday, citing a source close to the prime minister.

France is currently in the throes of a political and social crisis triggered by French President Emmanuel Macron’s move to push ahead with controversial pension reforms despite widespread public opposition.

“We are in the middle of a social crisis, there is the issue of policing, there are people between life and death, and I have the impression of being behind a smoke screen,” Sandrine Rousseau, Green Party politician and fellow women’s rights activist, told BFMTV Friday.

France wracked by more pension protests amid rising violence on the streets

French politician, Jean Luc Mélenchon who came third in the 2022 presidential elections criticized both Schiappa’s appearance and the decision by French President Emmanuel Macron to give an interview to children’s magazine, Pif Gadget, this week.

“In a country where the President expresses himself in Pif and his minister in Playboy, the problem would be the opposition. France is going off the rails,” Mélenchon tweeted Saturday.

Schiappa responded to her critics in a tweet on Saturday, saying: “Defending the right of women to have control of their bodies, that’s everywhere and all the time. In France, women are free. With all due respect to the detractors and hypocrites.”

French Interior Minister, Gérald Darmanin came to Schiappa’s defense during an interview with French news channel CNews on Sunday calling her a “woman of character.”

“I wanted to say that Marlene Schiappa is a courageous female politician who has her character and who has her style which is not mine, but I respect,” he remarked.
