#89. They were fined $811m for conning detained migrants, but the scams didn't stop

Mildred Juarez was stuck in detention for a month after her friends sent her bail money to Libre Immigration Services

Every morning for a month, Mildred Juarez woke up at five to be the first person in the showers at the immigration centre in Texas where she was being detained. Her friends had paid her bail, and she wanted to look presentable when she was finally released.

But every day, she was told her bond had not been paid.

Juarez was confused. Her friends had wired $2,600 (Â£1,970) to a bond company, Libre Immigration Services (LIS), that had been recommended by her lawyer. LIS said it would begin "the process of paying her immigration bond". But the next day came, and the day after that, with no release.

What she didn't know was that LIS was owned by a convicted fraudster, who had acquired the assets of another bail company co-founded by two men who also had fraud convictions. These two men were fined $811m in 2024 for scamming their clients.

Their firm, Libre By Nexus, which was at one point the largest immigration bail company in America, had appeared to shut down after federal regulators caught up with them.

But public records and BBC interviews with clients and former employees reveal a tangle of interconnected operations continued - along with the flow of cash - while leaving immigrants stuck in detention centres.

Since President Donald Trump has returned to office after promising an immigration crackdown, Immigrations and Customs Enforcement (ICE) has made over 1.2m arrests, and fast-tracked deportations. The administration has tried unsuccessfully to make it illegal to give migrants bail but the sharp increase in detentions has increased demand for bond companies to secure bail and their release.

Conditions in these centres can be poor. Held in custody, away from family, often scared to speak out, many have faced health problems and endured inadequate food or treatment, according to immigrant advocates and reports by the US government.

"He did everything right" - The impact of Trump's immigration crackdown in Ohio

In the detention centre where Juarez was locked up, she and about 100 other detainees slept in bunks laid out in rows in a cacophonous room. The lights were left on all night and guards routinely woke everyone up for roll call. According to Castro and advocacy groups, detainees who caused trouble were put in "the hold" - solitary confinement.

The White House and officials from Homeland Security did not respond to the BBC's request for comment.

For Juarez, the wait to be bailed out of detention seemed interminable.

In an email to the BBC, a spokesperson for LIS said Juarez "misunderstands" their services - that they are not bondsmen themselves, but they connect detained individuals to those who are licensed bondsmen. They said another company ultimately decided not to work with her.

An email sent by LIS, and reviewed by the BBC, said that the company had paid a cash bond to ICE on behalf of Juarez. The message was forwarded to Juarez as proof of payment a few days before her release.

But it was another bail agent, who was not affiliated with LIS, that got her out of detention - after her friends raised even more money to hire another company.

"I was losing it inside there," Juarez recalled, using an expletive.

"I lost everything. When I was released I had nightmares I was back inside."

Libre Immigration Services never paid back her deposit, despite sending messages promising a refund. A spokesperson told the BBC that they would refund Juarez's bail amount once her friend verified their details.

It is notoriously difficult for migrants like Juarez to make bail, which can be upwards of $7,000.

Once charged with illegal entry, a judge may grant bail, which allows the migrant to live freely while their case is heard, a process which can take years. Once a person's court case is completed, the bond amount is returned.

Bail agents, who are licensed to provide a down payment on bail and vouch that if the accused does not show up for court they will pay the rest, can help people get out of detention sooner. But many won't help migrants because they view them as too much of a flight risk. A 2024 government report found that a third of migrants not already in custody did not show up for court.

Libre by Nexus, also known as Nexus, was willing to take that risk.

Founded in 2013, its co-founders Michael Donovan and Richard Moore had served prison time in the 1990s and early 2000s for a string of fraud and forgery convictions.

Through his attorney, Donovan declined to comment. Moore did not respond to the BBC's request for comment.

Although people with felony convictions are barred from holding a bond licence in many states, Nexus operated as a kind of middleman between immigrant clients and licensed bondsmen.

Holed up in detention in Louisiana after crossing the border, Rosa Murillo heard about Libre by Nexus from another inmate, and the 18-year-old Honduran's mother called them to arrange her $7,000 bail.

A Nexus employee picked her up and took her to a hotel room where she was told she would have to pay $420 a month and wear a GPS ankle monitor until her case was completed.

Rosa Murillo says she was ashamed to wear the ankle bracelet that Nexus required

Federal regulators accused Nexus of implying, in their contracts, that the GPS bracelets were court-mandated, when they were actually the private company's requirement.

Murillo got a job as a waitress and tried to hide the ankle bracelet for fear that people might think she was a violent criminal.

Over the course of a decade, Libre by Nexus enabled more than 20,000 migrants like Murillo to be released on bail - and took in more than $230m in revenue, court documents show.

But the ankle bracelets were a constant source of annoyance for Nexus clients released from detention, according to two former employees who spoke to the BBC.

A former Nexus co-owner, Evan Ajin, told the BBC that the company stopped requiring released detainees to wear ankle bracelets in 2018.

When Mark Supetran worked for Libre by Nexus, he says he would tell people about how they had to wear an ankle bracelet

But Mark Supetran, who was based in the Philippines and worked for the company for a few months in 2022, says the bracelets were still in use when he worked for the company.

Supetran said he would field 80 calls a day from people who were either going through the process themselves or were the loved one of someone who was. Telling them they would have to wear a tag on release was part of the conversation.

"Part of the script was to say something like: 'The client will be wearing an ankle monitor.' So that got me thinking, like, why?" he recalled. "It doesn't make sense, we're not the government."

Not getting refunds of bail deposits was another complaint. Another former employee told the BBC he was instructed to tell callers that they would get their money back once their case was completed.

"But that that was never the case," he told the BBC. "I mean, you had people calling me in almost every day like 'Hey, am I gonna get my money back? Am I gonna get my money back?'"

Ajin, the former Nexus co-owner, told the BBC that the contract clearly stated that monthly fees did not go towards their bond, but were programme fees, and so they could issue no refund.

The US Consumer Financial Protection Bureau (CFPB), an agency created to stop predatory lending and other abusive financial practices, successfully sued Libre by Nexus and its parent company.

The lawsuit accused the firm of running a "predatory immigrant-services scam" that trapped victims into paying high, long-term fees. Nexus also misrepresented and concealed the terms, the lawsuit alleged, such as the non-refundable, monthly payments.

The judgment sided with the CFPB and a federal judge found the company liable for using predatory tactics against its clients in 2024, fining Nexus, its parent company, Ajin, Donovan and Moore $811m.

In addition to the hefty fine, the judge forbade the company from collecting on past debts.

Days after the 2024 order, Donovan sold a number of the company's assets, including customer lists and records - for $3.50.

As part of the agreement the buyer would also take on its debt.

Ajin says he ceased contact with everyone involved following the sale.

Moore is now in prison, after being convicted of filing fraudulent Nexus taxes. He and Donovan, who is also behind bars, are awaiting sentencing for insurance fraud and conspiracy. Donovan has also been charged with sexually abusing a boy under 12, but has not entered a plea in that case.

The purchaser of Nexus's assets was a Pennsylvania businessman named Vincent Smith, who had been sentenced to 10 months' probation in 2015 for conspiring with employees to write over $2m in fake bail bonds.

Smith called the new business Libre Immigration Services.

This March, the judge who issued the fine also ruled the sale of the company had violated her order by transferring the debts.

Judge Elizabeth Dillon voided the sale, but did not shut Libre Immigration Services down. She is due to review claims that LIS also violated the order by trying to build an app to collect debts from Nexus's old clients.

A former paralegal at Nexus, who went on to work for LIS, told the BBC about the app. She said after the sale, the lines between the two companies were blurred.

Another employee confirmed her account independently to the BBC, noting that the app was never finished because they could not obtain the client data.

A hearing date has not been set, and LIS has denied any wrongdoing, noting the company was never in possession of the client lists.

A screenshot of Libre Immigration Service's website

Libre Immigration Services continues to solicit immigrant clients and advertises its services online and on social media.

Its website features glossy photos of beaming families alongside the promise to "get your loved one released from a detention centre today!".

That wasn't Janie Aviles's experience. She had wired Libre Immigration Services $10,000 to help get her friend out of detention in Texas. But LIS did not pay his bail, Aviles said, until a month later, leaving him, like Juarez, in limbo.

When she called they offered various explanations and then stopped answering the telephone, she said.

Eventually she drove to the office listed on the website, which had shown a picture of LIS's logo hanging above the door. But on arrival, she found there was no storefront with the business's name and logo anywhere.

She said the company finally paid the bail a month after getting the funds, after she had reported them to the district attorney. No charges have been laid.

An LIS spokesperson said that "verification of significant information had to occur to prevent or minimise loss".

"The application was ultimately approved, and the cash bond was pledged to ICE. Presumably any delay related to the substantial bond amount and the heightened requirements of review."

They also said the office at that location was for business operations, and not client services, and that a sign had been ordered and would be installed, pending permits.

But the ordeal was enough to anger Aviles, who feared for her friend while he was stuck in detention. "I'm like 'This ain't right.'"

#88. Vape users hospitalised after losing consciousness, prompting public health warning in New South Wales

Testing confirms presence of etomidate, a strong and fast-acting sedative used as a general anaesthetic overseas but not registered for medical use in Australia

Get our breaking news email, free app or daily news podcast

Three people who lost consciousness and were hospitalised in New South Wales after using certain vapes have tested positive for the unregulated anaesthetic agent etomidate, health officials said.

NSW health on Friday issued a public warning about vape liquids containing etomidate, a strong and fast-acting sedative used as a general anaesthetic overseas but not registered for medical use in Australia.

The three people who were hospitalised had used non-nicotine refillable vapes, the state health department said. Testing of their blood and urine confirmed the presence of etomidate.

The department would not confirm how old the three patients were, where they were hospitalised, or their condition on Friday.

The discovery of etomidate in vapes in NSW comes about four months after Victorian health officials announced they had found the powerful anaesthetic agent in an illicit vape sold in Melbourne.

On Friday, NSW health warned vapes sold as space pods, space oil, kpods, piu piu vapes, or zombie vapes may contain etomidate, or other substances, including ketamine, THC and potent opioids.

The medical director of the NSW poisons information centre, Dr Darren Roberts, said etomidate’s effects were unpredictable when used in a vape.

Roberts said etomidate could cause confusion, drowsiness, slow breathing, vomiting, muscle spasms or movements that were hard to control, and people who took it could become unresponsive.

These effects could be life threatening, especially if used with other drugs that could cause sedation, such as alcohol, opioids, benzodiazepines, and GHB, Roberts said.

He said etomidate could also cause long-term harm when used regularly, including the disruption of hormones produced by the adrenal gland.

These hormones are important in regulating stress responses and need to be replaced if they aren’t produced naturally, he said.

“The contents found in non-nicotine vapes vary,” Roberts said. “You can’t assume what’s inside.”

He urged anyone considering using a vape to ask themselves what could be in it.

“You won’t get into trouble for seeking medical care,” he said. If you feel unwell, or if your friend feels unwell, after using a vape, do something about it.”

NSW health officials said it recommended people administer Naloxone to anyone who became responsive after using any drug, in case they had ingested opioids.

Naloxone is an opioid overdose treatment, which is free at pharmacies and does not require a prescription.

Naloxone won’t reverse the effects of etomidate, as the sedative does not belong to the opioid class of drugs, but it is still safe to give, health officials said.

The officials said anyone who witnessed someone experiencing unexpected symptoms after using drugs should call triple zero immediately or seek urgent medical attention.

#87. The four astronauts NASA picked for the first crewed moon mission in 50 years

Sign up for CNN’s Wonder Theory science newsletter. Explore the universe with news on fascinating discoveries, scientific advancements and more.

Astronauts who will helm the first crewed moon mission in five decades were revealed on Monday, queuing up the quartet to begin training for the historic Artemis II lunar flyby that is set to take off in November 2024.

The astronauts are NASA’s Reid Wiseman, Victor Glover and Christina Koch, and Jeremy Hansen of the Canadian Space Agency.

Wiseman is a 47-year-old decorated naval aviator and test pilot who was first selected to be a NASA astronaut in 2009. A native of Baltimore, Maryland, he’s completed one prior spaceflight, a 165-day trip to the International Space Station that had launched aboard a Russian Soyuz rocket in 2014. Most recently, Wiseman served as chief of the astronaut office before stepping down in November 2022, making him eligible for a flight assignment.

Wiseman will serve as commander of the Artemis II mission.

Hansen, 47, is a fighter pilot who was selected by the Canadian Space Agency for astronaut training in 2009. From London, Ontario, Hansen is one of only four active Canadian astronauts, and he recently became the first Canadian to be put in charge of training for a new class of NASA astronauts.

He will be the first Canadian ever to travel to deep space.

Glover is a 46-year-old naval aviator who returned to Earth from his first spaceflight in 2021 after piloting the second crewed flight of SpaceX’s Crew Dragon spacecraft and spending nearly six months aboard the International Space Station.

“It’s so much more than the four names that have been announced,” Glover said during the Monday announcement at NASA Johnson Space Center in Houston. “We need to celebrate this moment in human history. … It is the next step in the journey that will get humanity to Mars.”

Glover, born in Pomona, California, served in several military squadrons in the United States and Japan in the 2000s, and he completed test pilot training with the US Air Force. When he was selected for the NASA astronaut corps in 2013, he was working in the US Senate as a legislative fellow. All told, Glover logged 3,000 flight hours in more than 40 aircraft, over 400 carrier arrested landings and 24 combat missions.

Glover’s first mission to space was as part of the SpaceX Crew-1 team, which launched to the International Space Station in November 2020 for a six-month stay on the orbiting laboratory.

Koch, 44, is a veteran of six spacewalks — including the first all-female spacewalk in 2019. She holds the record for the longest single spaceflight by a woman, with a total of 328 days in space. Koch is also an an electrical engineer who helped develop scientific instruments for multiple NASA mission. Koch, a native of Grand Rapids, Michigan, also spent a year at the South Pole, an arduous stay that could well prepare her for the intensity of a moon mission.

The Artemis II mission will build on Artemis I, an uncrewed test mission that sent NASA’s Orion capsule on a 1.4 million-mile voyage to lap the moon that concluded in December. The space agency deemed that mission a success and is still working to review all the data collected.

If all goes to plan, Artemis II will take off around November 2024. The crew members, strapped inside the Orion spacecraft, will launch atop a NASA-developed Space Launch System rocket from NASA’s Kennedy Space Center in Florida.

The journey is expected to last about 10 days and will send the crew out beyond the moon, potentially further than any human has traveled in history, though the exact distance is yet to be determined.

The “exact distance beyond the Moon will depend on the day of liftoff and the relative distance of the Moon from the Earth at the time of the mission,” NASA spokesperson Kathryn Hambleton said via email.

After circling the moon, the spacecraft will return to Earth for a splashdown landing in the Pacific Ocean.

Artemis II is expected to pave the way for the Artemis III mission later this decade, which NASA has vowed will put the first woman and person of color on the lunar surface. It will also mark the first time humans have touched down on the moon since the Apollo program ended in 1972.

The Artemis III mission is expected to take off later this decade. But much of the technology the mission will require, including spacesuits for walking on the moon and a lunar lander to ferry the astronauts to the moon’s surface, is still in development.

NASA is targeting a 2025 launch date for Artemis III, though the space agency’s inspector general has already said delays will likely push the mission to 2026 or later.

The space agency has been seeking to return people to the moon for more than a decade. The Artemis program was designed to pave the way to establishing a permanent lunar outpost, allowing astronauts to live and work deeper into space long term as NASA and its partners map a path to sending the first humans to Mars.

Vanessa Wyche, the director of NASA’s Johnson Space Center, declined to provide details to CNN about the selection process. But she emphasized the diversity of the Artemis II crew, which includes men and women rather than only a staff of White male test pilots as has been the case for historic missions of the past.

“I can tell you, they still all have the right stuff,” Wyche said. “We have requirements different than we did (when we) just had test pilots” on inaugural missions.

Koch said in an interview with CNN’s Ed Lavandera that the group found out they were selected a few weeks ago.

“We were all sent to a meeting that was on our calendars under a different pretext that didn’t sound as lofty as the one it was going to be,” Koch said. “And accidentally two of us were very late to that meeting.”

She said the offer rendered her “speechless.”

“It truly is an honor,” she added. “It’s an honor — not to get myself in the space — but because it’s amazing to be a part of this team that’s going back to the moon and on to Mars.”

An interview with the four astronauts will air on “CNN This Morning” on Tuesday, which starts at 6 am ET.

CNN’s Kristin Fisher contributed to this story.

#83. From premature baby to social media star - Famed hippo Fiona dies

The Cincinnati Zoo in Ohio says its beloved hippopotamus Fiona has died just days after swallowing a knitted hat that slipped into her enclosure.

Fiona was born premature at the Cincinnati Zoo in 2017 and at first struggled to survive. At birth, she weighed 13 kg (29 lbs), which the zoo said was about half the previous lowest recorded birth weight for her species. She reached 1,000 lbs in 2018.

#82. As the Coalition stares down One Nation in NSW, a new threat is rising: the teals

The opposition’s struggle is creating an opportunity for progressive independents who are boosted by shrewd political operator Chris Minns

Get our daily political email, free app or daily news podcast

The New South Wales Coalition is facing a renewed threat to its heartland seats on Sydney’s north shore.

This time, the threat is from independent candidates – and they’re backed by the same community activists who put a swathe of teal independents into federal parliament.

The challenge, which has so far remained under the radar, comes as the NSW Coalition tries to shore up its right flank against a surging One Nation before the March state election. This has resulted in the Coalition lurching to the right on issues ranging from gun control to law and order, and, in the case of the NSW Nationals, to abandon climate targets altogether.

As former opposition leader Mark Speakman resigned from the Liberals on Thursday – joining an exodus of MPs announcing they wouldn’t contest their seats in March – he warned his party against becoming “One Nation lite”.

But the move to the right has also created an opportunity for progressive independents running in urban seats usually held by Liberal moderates, with several candidates about to launch their campaigns.

This weekend, Amelia Loye, a management consultant who specialises in stakeholder and community engagement, will announce she is running in the seat of Willoughby, on Sydney’s north shore. She will attempt to oust Liberal MP Tim James, who holds the seat on a 2.6% margin.

Loye is backed by community group Willoughby Votes, which came close to securing an independent in the 2023 election with 47% of the two-party preferred vote.

“Willoughby has already shown it is ready for an independent,” she said. “Nearly half of voters backed one last time. I want to earn their support, and the support of those still deciding.”

She joins high-profile candidate Elouise Massa, a small business owner, who became a household name after campaigning for Northern Beaches hospital to return to public hands after her toddler’s death in emergency.

Massa will run in the Sydney seat of Manly against Liberal Adelaide Cuneo, who is seeking to replace the retiring MP, James Griffin.

The independent deputy mayor of Mosman, Michael Randall, announced last weekend he will run in North Shore as a community independent. Several of the people behind former independent federal MP Kylea Tink’s campaign are supporting him.

Monica Wulff, another independent, has announced a run in Davidson.

Voters on the northern beaches have already sent two independents to Macquarie street – and both have a good chance of being returned.

At the federal level, teal independents Sophie Scamps, Zali Steggall and Nicolette Boele hold McKellar, Warringah and Bradfield respectively. Tink held North Sydney for one term before the seat was abolished.

The NSW Liberal party is not giving up on the northern beaches and the north shore, despite the trend towards independents.

“We’ll keep making our case directly to voters, including why we oppose Labor’s gun buyback that targets law-abiding owners rather than criminals and terrorists,” a Liberal spokesperson said.

This time, however, the independents have a boost in the form of NSW Labor premier, Chris Minns.

Sign up for Guardian Australia’s Politics, really newsletter here

During the final weeks of parliament before the NSW election, Minns joked that independent Jacqui Scruby, who won Pittwater at a byelection, should be known as “the member for Mona Vale road” for her tenacity in seeking an upgrade. It’s now being fast-tracked by the Minns government.

Minns has also appeared with Massa, and praised her for her advocacy.

But Minns, a shrewd political operator, has not fallen in love with independents – he’s just hedging his bets.

A year ago, with the Liberals languishing in the polls, Labor could have expected to increase its majority in 2027. But with One Nation storming to a primary vote as high as 30% in some polls, that prospect has dimmed.

In the current parliament, Labor has support of three independents – Greg Piper, Joe McGirr and Alex Greenwich – to ensure confidence and supply for its minority government. An expanded crossbench would give more options and provide a buffer for Labor losses to One Nation in western Sydney, the Hunter and possibly the Central Coast.

If all independents were successful – and that is a big if – almost the entire north shore region would go to state independents, except the seat of Wahroonga.

Most of the commentary about the 2027 election has focused on the likely Armageddon facing the Nationals, who could lose as many eight or nine of their 11 seats in the regions.

But a teal wave also remains a threat to the Liberal party, especially as the Coalition try to position themselves to fight off One Nation in the regions with policies that don’t sit well with urban voters.

One Nation could also prove helpful to independents if it makes good on its promise to run candidates in every seat. The party is more likely to shave votes from the Liberal primary vote, making it easier for the independents to overtake the Liberal on preferences from Labor and the Greens.

NSW’s optional preferential voting makes it hard to predict how strong flows will be – and it depends what parties advocate on how to votes. But in 2023 only 41% of One Nation voters nominated preferences, compared to 70% of Green voters and 49% of Labor voters.

The opposition leader, Kellie Sloane, has not ruled out either a preference deal or a post-election arrangement with One Nation, describing such options as “hypothetical”. But if they do end up preferencing One Nation, it may alienate moderate Liberals and ultimately work against them.

Disclosure: Anne Davies worked as a media and policy adviser to the independent federal MP Sophie Scamps from October 2023 to October 2024.

#78. NSW passes Mount Pleasant coalmine law as Greens claim change an attempt to head off One Nation

Labor says allowing mine to increase production reflects ‘gravity of situation facing hundreds of workers’ – but critics call it ‘unprecedented and dangerous’

Get our breaking news email, free app or daily news podcast

The New South Wales government has passed special legislation to allow a coalmine in the Hunter Valley to increase production by 32% in its final act of parliament before the March state election, in which the region will be a key battleground for One Nation.

After a marathon session in the legislative council on Friday, the Minns government and the Coalition voted at 1.36am on Saturday morning to allow the Mount Pleasant mine to increase its annual extraction rate from 12.5MT to 16.5MT until 2032.

At the start of debate, the resources minister, Courtney Houssos, told the upper house “this is a unique bill applying to unique circumstances”.

“Taking the decision to intervene in the way we have is not an easy one, but it is an important one,” she said. “And it does reflect the gravity of the situation facing hundreds of workers and their families in the Hunter.”

The legislation has drawn outcry from legal and anti-corruption experts, climate groups, crossbenchers and civil liberties organisations, after the high court ruled last month that an approval for an expansion that would increase the mine’s annual output to 21MT until 2048 was invalid.

In a 3-2 split ruling, the high court found the state’s Independent Planning Commission (IPC) failed to properly consider imposing conditions to minimise greenhouse gas emissions that would be generated by the mine when the coal is sold and burned overseas.

Despite saying he would not bypass the court’s decision, the NSW premier, Chris Minns, subsequently announced the legislation, claiming it would save 400 jobs ahead of Christmas. Minns has rejected the idea the legislation goes around the high court decision.

On Friday, the government and Coalition used their numbers to block almost 200 amendments to the bill sought by the Greens, who called the legislation “exceptional and unprecedented and dangerous”. Animal Justice party MLC Emma Hurst also opposed the bill.

“Labor describes the circumstances as exceptional, says this case is unique, and assures the public that other companies shouldn’t expect the same treatment,” the Greens climate spokesperson, Sue Higginson, told the parliament.

“But a political assurance offers no protection against the precedent being set.”

She accused the premier of being motivated by a desire to withstand a threat from One Nation to Labor seats in the Hunter.

“It’s very clear to us why Chris Minns has gone here, and that is because we’re really on the eve of an election, and One Nation is right now his threat,” she said at a press conference on Thursday.

The Hunter region, the centre of NSW’s coalmining industry, is emerging as a key battleground at the March 2027 state election. The One Nation NSW upper house candidate and former coalminer Stuart Bonds contested the Labor-held federal seat of Hunter in 2025, winning 41% of the two-candidate preferred vote. The party’s NSW leader, Mike Newman, is running in the Labor-held lower house seat of Cessnock.

Higginson told the upper house that in bringing the bill, the government had turned its back on the advice of the state’s Net Zero Commission, which has said further coalmine expansions are incompatible with meeting legislated emissions reduction targets.

“But who seems to care any more? What does the rule of law matter when [the premier] visits the Hunter and decides he needs to do something for someone so that he can remain somewhat popular in his own mind?” she said.

The Coalition, which supported the bill, said it was “about people”.

“It’s about the hundreds of workers at MACH Energy’s Mount Pleasant mine who, since the high court handed down its decision in October, have been left wondering whether they will still have a job in the new year,” Nationals MLC Sarah Mitchell said.

She said “losing hundreds of jobs in one go would completely devastate the Hunter region”.

The legislation ties consent for the mine expansion to a jobs guarantee protecting workers at the mine. Data provided to the government by the mine for the jobs guarantee, which covers more than 720 workers, is subject to an independent audit.

At a press conference on Wednesday, Minns said the legal proceedings would return to the land and environment court for consideration, but it would be “a tragedy if, in the meantime, that community effectively shut down as a result of this decision”.

“Most of the workers from Mount Pleasant live in Muswellbrook, and if the mine closed, the unemployment rate would jump by 5% just in that small town,” he said, speaking alongside workers at the mine and the Mining and Energy Union, which has welcomed the legislation.

In a report published on Tuesday, the parliament’s legislation review committee found the Mount Pleasant bill would “effectively undermine” proceedings that were still before the land and environment court and “would undermine procedural fairness” by removing avenues for legal challenge.

That report also highlighted concerns with a so-called Henry VIII clause that the barrister and anti-corruption campaigner Geoffrey Watson warned this week posed a “serious corruption risk” to the state.

The clause, allowing the resources minister of the day to override state laws as they apply to the Mount Pleasant development, was amended by the government in the lower house. Following the amendments, it cannot be used to prevent development from being subject to Icac investigations or to change the permitted coal production limit.

The Nature Conservation Council of NSW said the government had been “embarrassed into amending its own coalmining bill”, while the Greens said the amendments did not go far enough to constrain the power any resources minister might hold under the clause.

The Law Society of NSW said on Thursday it was concerned that without further amendments, the Henry VIII clause risked “undermining the rule of law”.

Houssos told the upper house on Friday the government had carefully considered concerns and moved amendments to the clause because it “recognises the importance of appropriate safeguards and accountability”.

#73. Victorian Greens seek urgent injunction over how-to-vote cards mimicking party’s branding but preferencing it last

Exclusive: Independent candidate Tosh-Jake Finnigan’s cards instruct people to vote in a way that would benefit Liberal party

Get our daily political email, free app or daily news podcast

The Victorian Greens are seeking an urgent injunction from the supreme court to stop an independent candidate distributing how-to-vote cards that allegedly mimic the party’s branding and urge people to preference it last.

As prepoll opened on Wednesday morning, Tosh-Jake Finnigan’s campaign team handed out how-to-vote cards that used the minor party’s colour scheme, font and triangle logo with the banner “Vote 1 Green Values”.

The card instructed people to vote in a way that would ultimately benefit the Liberal party. It asked voters to give their first preference to Finnigan followed by an Animal Justice party candidate, another independent candidate and then the Liberals in fourth place.

The Greens are determined to win back the ultra-marginal seat it lost to the Liberals at a February 2025 byelection with a 1.3% margin. Labor is running an open ticket in Prahran, which the Greens fear could deprive it of the preference votes it needs.

On Wednesday afternoon, the Greens state director, Martin Shield, requested Finnigan immediately stop distributing the material and threatened to take legal action if an undertaking was not given to do so in less than two hours.

Finnigan agreed to the request and produced an amended how-to-vote card within hours that included different colours with a revised banner that said “Vote 1 Trans Greens Values”.

“Due to legal threats from the Greens party, I confirm that I am not an endorsed Greens party candidate,” a disclaimer on Finnigan’s revised how-to-vote card said.

But the changes did not satisfy the Greens. On Friday afternoon, the party’s lawyers lodged papers in the supreme court.

At an initial hearing on Friday night, the Greens’ lawyer, Adam McBeth, alleged people handing out Finnigan’s material at prepoll wore shirts with the exact shade of green as worn by genuine Greens party volunteers.

McBeth alleged Finnigan’s team stood next to Greens party volunteers and Greens placards. The party argues voters at prepoll were deliberately misled.

“There is no other reason for an independent candidate who has an infinite range of colours and styles to choose from to so closely mimic the Greens, including the way people paid to hand out were dressed and where they were directed to stand,” McBeth said.

Finnigan, who was self-represented, said they had gone above and beyond to quickly make changes to their initial how-to-vote card and accused the Greens of using “legal manoeuvres” to stop them campaigning.

“It is my belief that they don’t want me to hand out on Saturday, which will be one of the busiest days of prepoll,” Finnigan said.

Finnigan told the court that they did not have enough money to print new cards. They said their campaign team had not been instructed to stand next to the Greens at prepoll.

“I am someone who holds Greens values, of course I am going to have some kind of green shirt,” Finnigan said.

“The honest reason I went for that colour of green is because the colour is called Irish green and I am of Irish background. That’s why I went for it. I just like the sound of Irish green.”

On Friday evening, after the supreme court action was lodged, the Victorian Election Commission (VEC) accepted Finnigan’s subsequent how-to-vote card and published it online.

Justice John Watson said the first how-to-vote card “looked like a pretty deliberate attempt to pass yourself off as a Greens candidate”, but he adjourned the matter until 3pm on Saturday to hear testimony from the VEC.

Finnigan became Victoria’s first openly transgender councillor in 2023 when they were elected to the Colac Otway shire council after a countback. They resigned from the council in October 2024.

The former Labor field campaigner founded the Restore Democracy Sack Dan Andrews party in 2022. They unsuccessfully contested an upper house seat for the party, securing 1% of first preference votes.

After working in the offices of Labor ministers during the 2014 state campaign, Finnigan became a whistleblower in the so-called Red Shirts scandal, which saw taxpayer funds used to pay electorate officers campaigning for Labor.

Finnigan’s campaign Facebook page carrying an authorisation message describes them as a “Green Independent” and contains attack ads against the minor party.

#69. Teenager allegedly organised arsons and kidnapping on Signal during Melbourne hospitality wars

Victoria police say boy, 17, communicated with offenders under username ‘Onepercent’

Get our breaking news email, free app or daily news podcast

Victorian police allege a 17-year-old boy tasked others to carry out a series of arsons, home invasions and a mistaken identity kidnapping that resulted in the partial amputation of a victim’s finger during Melbourne’s hospitality wars.

The boy, who was arrested on Friday afternoon, allegedly communicated with the offenders under the username “Onepercent” on the encrypted communications app Signal.

He was charged on Saturday with extortion, making threats to kill, three offences of criminal damage by fire, and home invasion. He was remanded to appear in court later on Saturday.

In a statement, police said it would be alleged that Onepercent was involved in tasking an arson at a York Street, South Melbourne licensed premises on 15 April; two home invasions and a kidnapping four days apart in Springvale later that month; and two arson attacks during March at a Williamstown ice-cream store and a car in Moonee Ponds.

The boy was arrested at a house in the Moonee Ponds region, in Melbourne’s north-west, police said.

It is alleged a trafficable quantity of cocaine, three phones and cash were seized during the arrest.

Police allege that when the mistaken identity kidnapping occurred at the Springvale property on 22 April, a 34-year-old victim received serious injuries to his lower body and the partial amputation of one of his fingers.

It is alleged the fire at Capriccio Gelateria in Williamstown on 22 March caused $600,000 worth of damage and endangered the lives of residents at the property upstairs.

On 16 November, police appealed for more information about the person behind the Onepercent user name.

“We need the public’s assistance to identify the user of this handle,” Det Insp Graham Banks said at the time.

“There will be people in the wider organised crime network and in our community who will have intimate knowledge of who this person is.

“We are hopeful that those people may now be in a position to come forward and speak to police.”

#67. Beijing promised to 'fight back' over Taiwan leader's US visit. But this time it has more to lose

An anticipated meeting between Taiwan’s President Tsai Ing-wen and US House Speaker Kevin McCarthy in California this week has sparked concerns of a repeat of the pressure campaign China launched last year when then-Speaker Nancy Pelosi visited Taipei.

At that time, Beijing encircled the island democracy with unprecedented military drills – firing multiple missiles into its surrounding waters and sending dozens of warplanes speeding across a sensitive median line dividing the Taiwan Strait.

It also cut off contact with the United States over a number of issues from military matters to combating climate change, in retaliation for what it viewed as a violation of its sovereignty.

This time, Beijing has already threatened to “resolutely fight back” if a Tsai-McCarthy meeting goes ahead.

It also slammed Washington for allowing Tsai to stopover in the US while en route to and from official visits in Central America, warning it could lead to “serious” confrontation between the two powers.

A defiant Tsai staked out her own ground, pledging as she took off on her 10-day trip not to let “external pressure” stop Taiwan from connecting with the world and like-minded democracies.

But the optics of the meeting, taking place in California and not Taiwan, and its timing – at a particularly thorny moment in China’s foreign relations and ahead of a presidential election in Taiwan that could reset the tone of its relationship with Beijing – may see Beijing tread more carefully this time, or at least not escalate further, analysts say.

“This puts the burden on China not to overreact, because any overreaction is only going to push China further away from the world,” said Yun Sun, director of the China Program at the Stimson Center think tank in Washington.

That doesn’t mean, however, that Beijing won’t be closely watching Tsai’s movements as it calibrates its response – and decides how much military might to flex over her meeting with an American lawmaker on American soil.

The opacity of China’s system – and the potential for competing interests within its vast bureaucracy – also make it difficult to accurately predict its response.

“Every time Taiwan does anything that China doesn’t like, the Chinese react with their own military coercion,” Sun said. But in the current situation, “they have to consider the consequences of overreaction,” she added.

The expected meeting, which McCarthy’s office announced earlier this week would take place on Wednesday, also comes at a precarious moment in US-China relations.

Washington and Beijing are struggling to stabilize their communication amid flaring tensions over issues from a downed suspected Chinese surveillance balloon to semiconductor supply chains – raising the stakes of potential damage to that relationship if Beijing lashes out as it did when Tsai met Pelosi.

Taiwan is still feeling the fallout of that response last August, with Chinese military forces now regularly making incursions over what had previously been an informal but largely respected border of control between Beijing and Taipei in the Taiwan Strait. Taiwan’s official Central News Agency also reported Monday that Tsai would meet with McCarthy, citing Tsai’s presidential office.

But a meeting between Tsai and the leader of the Republican majority in the US House of Representatives, who ranks second in line to the Presidency, would mark another symbolic moment for Taiwan and the US, which only maintain unofficial ties.

For Tsai, who is entering the final year of her two-term presidency, “it’s clearly a capstone event,” according to Wen-Ti Sung, a political scientist at the Australian National University’s Taiwan Studies Program. “She has this image as the Taiwanese president who has taken US-Taiwan relations to new heights, and who … has been able to give Taiwan almost unprecedented international visibility,” he said.

That increased visibility – and enhanced cooperation with the US – has followed China’s mounting pressure on the island, which sits fewer than 110 miles (177 kilometers) from the mainland coast.

China’s Communist Party claims the self-governing island democracy as its own despite never having controlled it, and has vowed to take the island, by force if necessary.

China thinks it's diplomatically isolating Taiwan. It isn't

The party has undertaken a sweeping expansion of its military capabilities over the past decade under leader Xi Jinping – and ramped up its pervasive economic, diplomatic and military pressure on Taiwan.

That’s driven concerns, among some in Washington, that Beijing is preparing for an invasion, though China’s official language still suggests that scenario is not its preferred option for achieving the claimed goal of “reunification.”

It is those pressures – and how to support Taiwan against unilateral actions by Beijing – that are likely to be on the table when Tsai, McCarthy and a bipartisan group of US lawmakers sit down on Wednesday.

Congress has been a pillar of increasing American support for Taiwan in recent years. Lawmakers regularly visit the island and drive bipartisan legislation enhancing support and cooperation.

While the US switched its diplomatic relations to Beijing decades ago, it maintains unofficial ties with Taiwan and is bound by law to provide the democratic island with the means to defend itself.

Under Washington’s longstanding “One China” policy, the US acknowledges China’s position that Taiwan is part of China, but has never officially recognized Beijing’s claim to the island of 23 million.

Though McCarthy does not have Pelosi’s decades-long record of advocacy regarding China, the California Republican is now a leading voice pushing for closer scrutiny of Beijing, and meeting Tsai could help him to burnish that image.

Last month, McCarthy told reporters that meeting Tsai in the US would not impact whether he travels to Taiwan in the future – something he had earlier said he wanted to do.

A meeting in California, on US soil, is widely seen as less likely to provoke Beijing than a McCarthy visit to Taiwan.

Pelosi’s trip – the first from a lawmaker of that rank to the island in 25 years – generated a fever pitch of nationalist and anti-US rhetoric in mainland China.

This time, so far, domestic conversation in China’s heavily controlled media sphere has been significantly muted.

But the stakes remain high – including for Beijing itself – over how it responds, analysts say.

As Taiwan prepares for a presidential election in January, a fierce response could push voters away from Taiwan’s main opposition party Kuomintang (KMT), widely seen as more friendly toward Beijing.

It could also jar with another high profile trip happening now: a tour of mainland China from former Taiwan president and senior KMT member Ma Ying-jeou, the first visit from a current or former Taiwan leader since the end of the Chinese Civil War in 1949.

Ma’s tour is a “once in a half a century opportunity to send a conciliatory message between the two sides, Beijing shouldn’t want to tank that,” said Sung, the political scientist.

What you need to know about China-Taiwan tensions

China is also acutely aware that its actions toward Taiwan are under a significantly brighter global spotlight following the invasion of Ukraine by Russian President Vladimir Putin, a close diplomatic partner of Xi. Putin’s rhetoric over Ukraine has echoes of how Xi speaks of Taiwan.

Beijing has recently sought to position itself as an agent of peace in that conflict – especially as it aims to repair frayed ties with Europe.

This week, as Tsai is expected to meet with McCarthy, French President Emmanuel Macron and European Commission President Ursula von der Leyen will head to China – an important opportunity that Xi may not want to overshadow with military posturing.

An aggressive response also risks stoking confrontation with the US, not yet six months after Xi and US President Joe Biden called to enhance communication during a face-to-face meeting in Bali.

“(A less overtly aggressive response) would imply that Beijing does not wish to escalate tension with the US to a level that can risk getting out of hand,” said Steve Tsang, director of the SOAS China Institute in London.

“A reset in US-China ties is not on the agenda, but an easing of tension is not beyond the realm of possibility.”

#62. Trump accused of falling asleep during Xi Jinping speech at Apec summit in China

US president appeared to doze off while Chinese leader made speech, but White House insisted he was awake

Donald Trump was, again, accused of falling asleep during a televised event, this time during the speech of the Chinese president, Xi Jinping, at the Asia-Pacific Economic Cooperation (Apec) summit in China.

The US president appeared to close his eyes for roughly 10 seconds on Thursday. As his eyes closed, Trump’s head tilted downward, and his hands remained clasped in front of him.

Several prominent Trump critics on social media alleged that the US president was asleep, including Republicans Against Trump, California governor Gavin Newsom’s press office and conservative lawyer George Conway.

White House officials denied the allegations, claiming the 80-year-old US president was looking down at papers in front of him.

“Fake News! President Trump was looking at papers in front of him while the speaker made remarks. You can clearly see his eyes move back and forth,” Steven Cheung, the White House director of communications, wrote on X in response to a Newsweek article.

“This is why the people despise media so much. They constantly lie,” Cheung added.

Trump often faces accusations of falling asleep during televised events such as Oval Office press conferences, cabinet meetings and sporting events.

Occasionally, while listening to other people speak, the US president will close his eyes for several seconds, appearing as though he is falling asleep. Despite footage of the instances going viral, the White House and Trump allies have insisted the US president does not fall asleep during meetings.

Administration officials have excused Trump’s apparent drowsiness as blinking or an example of the president listening intently. They consistently claim Trump “doesn’t sleep”.

Marco Rubio, the US secretary of state, testified to lawmakers earlier this year that he had “never seen” Trump fall asleep.

“On the contrary, the guy doesn’t sleep, which is a big problem because he calls me at two in the morning,” Rubio said in June.

Last year, JD Vance said that Trump hardly ever sleeps, even while traveling overnight.

“If we go on a 20-hour trip somewhere, he doesn’t sleep the entire time. And of course, if he’s not sleeping, if he’s working, he expects everybody else to be working too,” Vance told Fox News.

Trump became the oldest US president ever inaugurated when he was sworn in nearly two years ago. Despite his age, Trump often claims he is in near-perfect health, often bragging about his “perfect” cognitive test results and insisting he has a lot of energy.

#59. China, Russia and US agree peace declaration with no mention of Ukraine and Iran wars

Xi, Putin and Trump outline priorities that include UN reform, boosting oil supplies and ‘protection of diplomatic immunity’

China, Russia and the US say they have agreed a peace statement after an unprecedented trilateral summit, but the declaration made no mention of the wars in Ukraine and Iran caused by two of the participants.

The declaration on Thursday also outlined a set of priorities that included changing the UN, boosting oil supplies and what it said was the protection of diplomatic immunity.

The statement affirmed that “a nuclear war cannot be won and must not be fought”, but stopped short of suggesting the beginnings of a framework for denuclearisation.

The meeting between Xi Jinping, Vladimir Putin and Donald Trump has dominated the Asia-Pacific Economic Cooperation (Apec) summit this week, with China, the host, promising it would be “groundbreaking”.

“We are really making history,” Trump told Xi and Putin at the start of the meeting. “No peace was ever forged by declining opportunities for engagement.”

Putin told a press conference later that the three men had had a “free discussion” and now had a “mutual understanding”.

Some had hopes that the talks, which lasted about one hour and 20 minutes, could be used to discuss reductions in arms between the world’s three largest nuclear powers. Beijing has previously rebuffed Trump’s advances to join such talks, pointing to the larger US and Russian stockpiles. On Thursday, though, Xi called for nuclear weapons to “be completely prohibited and thoroughly destroyed over time to make the world free of [them]”.

The language of the declaration around nuclear war is the same as that previously issued by the five permanent members of the UN security council, which includes France and the UK.

“The real test will be whether the three countries follow through on their stated commitment to discuss nuclear risk reduction and preventing nuclear proliferation,” said Patricia Kim, an expert on Chinese foreign policy at the Brookings Institution, a US thinktank. Even the suggestion that China would engage was significant, said Yun Sun, a senior fellow at the Stimson Center.

Analysts had said that the meeting’s primary significance was in its optics. “Overall, very little of substance seems to have been accomplished … This summit appears to have been all about the symbolism and prestige value,” said Prof William Hurst, from the University of Cambridge’s Centre for Geopolitics. Putin pushed back at the suggestion the summit had just been a photo-op. “It is a serious collaborative work,” he said.

In the declaration, the men “committed themselves to maintaining strategic stability and international security”.

First among their concerns was “promoting UN reform so that it may respond more effectively to global challenges”. Sun said this was a favourite topic of China’s and showed that Beijing was “flexing its diplomatic muscles”. She said: “It is able to set the agenda and the deliverables according to its priorities … and make other great powers accept them.”

The declaration also agreed that “the international criminal court [ICC] and other similar institutions must not abuse their jurisdiction”. Putin is subject to an ICC arrest warrant and so the clause added to the perception that he has done well from the summit. Since the 2022 invasion of Ukraine, the Russian president has been treated as an international pariah by Kyiv’s allies and he has not been to an Apec summit in years.

Hurst said: “This was no Yalta moment, but it was certainly a solid public demonstration of Russia’s (and Putin’s) continuing relevance and power. It’s yet another example of Putin playing a poor hand to maximum effect.”

Xi, his status as a global power broker now assured, began the morning by addressing Apec leaders, reiterating the themes of trade openness and innovation he has emphasised throughout the summit. However, Apec affairs appeared to take a back seat for most of the day, with business dominating the discussions. For all the camaraderie on display, relations are complex. Russia is under US sanctions over the Ukraine war, while China and the US spent much of the last year engaged in an escalating tit-for-tat trade war before Trump and Xi called a truce.

Trump and Putin earlier held a separate meeting – their first since August 2025 – where the US president called his Russian counterpart his “friend”.

European allies meeting in Berlin to show their support for Ukraine expressed indignation at the cosy optics. “Mr Putin is not our friend and ⁠will never be,” the Polish prime minister, Donald Tusk, said in a rejoinder to Trump. The Danish prime minister, Mette Frederiksen, said she found it “very incomprehensible” that a US ​president would want to sit down with Putin.

The peace declaration in Shenzhen said the three countries planned “to increase the production and supply of refined petroleum products for the benefit of all nations”. That was important, said Shan Guo from Hutong Research, a Shanghai-based advisory firm, but “now let’s see if the US will lift sanctions”.

Trump and Xi then held their third bilateral of this year. Xi said the US-China relationship was “generally stable and positive”, according to state media. But he cautioned Washington over Taiwan, saying the US “must keep doing the right thing to avoid major setbacks in US-China relations”.

Another watched-for topic lacking detailed findings was that of AI. Xi said the two countries should “enhance communication and coordination to guard against security risks … and ensure that AI remains under human control at all times”.

Ultimately, the lack of breakthroughs might suit China, which has no appetite to move beyond the role of convener, said Kim. “Beijing has little interest in taking on the diplomatic risks of brokering an actual peace agreement in Ukraine or resolving the conflict with Iran. That would entangle China in conflicts it would rather avoid,” she said.

→ Baca artikel asli

#58. South Korea catches 111 students with new measures against exam cheating

How South Korea tackles cheating on its college entrance exam

South Korea's education ministry says 111 candidates were found with unauthorised smart devices during a high-stakes nationwide exam, after officials introduced urgent measures to tackle rising cases of AI-assisted cheating.

Five of the candidates were caught red-handed using smart devices during the exam, authorities said on Friday.

Half a million students sat for the college entry exam, also known as Suneung, which took place this week under the shadow of several recent high-profile cheating scandals.

Prior to the Suneung, authorities announced anti-cheating measures such as emergency alerts sent to every single mobile phone in the country to detect unauthorised devices in exam halls.

On Friday, President Lee Jae Myung called for an overhaul of the exam system to tackle AI-assisted cheating.

In South Korea, public examinations "significantly affect the distribution of opportunities and resources", Lee said, adding that using AI to cheat in such tests "rocks the basic order of our society by undermining fairness and trust".

Concerns over academic dishonesty had been brewing in recent weeks, after reports of candidates cheating with the help of AI devices.

Last month, two candidates were caught cheating with AI glasses during university entrance exams.

The education ministry said earlier this month that four students across middle and high schools were caught using AI glasses to cheat this year.

These fears came to a boil over the weekend, when the top scorer of South Korea's certified tax accountancy exam admitted to cheating by using a miniature camera, mobile phone and AI tools.

His confession came amid speculation over his unusually high score, with just one out of 160 questions wrong.

"I used a microcamera to photograph the test questions and sent them to an AI, then checked my answers on my smartphone to commit the cheating," said the candidate, 34-year-old Han Won-seok, in a video published on Sunday.

His confession, days ahead of the Suneung, prompted authorities to introduce last-minute measures to prevent cheating during the national exam.

The measures included nationwide emergency alerts on the exam day.

Three alerts were sent between 08:00 and 15:00 on Thursday, causing phones across the country to blare out in unison - even if they were on silent mode, and even if they belonged to tourists using data roaming.

The phones did not ring out if they were switched off or on airplane mode.

It was the first time that South Korea tapped on its national disaster alert system to catch exam cheaters.

Candidates also had to cover up the buttons on their shirts and jackets to block any hidden cameras, and authorities conducted inspections of eyewear.

All this made even honest test takers paranoid.

Ahead of the Suneung, one high school senior told the Korea Times that he was worried his horn-rimmed spectacles would be mistaken for smart glasses.

Another told news outlet Yonhap that he usually wore glasses but made an effort to put on contact lenses for the Suneung to avoid raising suspicions.

Held every November, the Suneung is an infamous eight-hour marathon of back-to-back exams.

Students attempt about 200 questions across various subjects, which include Korean, maths, English, social and natural sciences, among others.

The country itself comes to a standstill - construction work halts, planes are grounded and military training gets suspended to facilitate an optimal testing environment.

Many South Koreans see the Suneung as a culmination of years of formal education - and a turning point that determines their university placements, careers, and social statuses.

#57. Suspected Chinese spy balloon was able to transmit information back to Beijing

The Chinese spy balloon that transited the US earlier this year was able to capture imagery and collect some signals intelligence from US military sites, a source familiar with the matter tells CNN.

The balloon was able to transmit information back to Beijing in real time, the source said, and the US government still does not know for sure whether the Chinese government could wipe the balloon’s data as it received it. That raises questions about whether there is intelligence the balloon was able to gather that the US still doesn’t know about.

Still, the intelligence community has not been overly concerned about the information the balloon was able to gather, the person said, because it is not much more sophisticated than what Chinese satellites are able to glean as they orbit over similar locations.

A US intelligence official told CNN on Monday that “although analysis of the wreckage of the High Altitude Balloon remains ongoing, thus far, its flight over the United States does not appear to have provided critical new insights to the People’s Republic of China.”

The US also knew what the balloon’s path would be and was able to protect sensitive sites and censor some signals before the balloon was able to pick them up, officials have said.

As CNN has reported, the US intelligence community last year developed a method of tracking what it says is a fleet of these Chinese balloons operating across the globe, controlled by the Chinese military.

The FBI is still examining the balloon, but so far officials have been able to glean additional information about how the device worked, including the algorithms used for the balloon’s software and how it is powered and designed.

CNN has reached out to the National Security Council at the White House and the Pentagon for comment. NBC was first to report on the new intelligence.

The balloon first crossed into US airspace over Alaska in late January before passing through Canada and down into Montana, where it hovered for a few days, leading the US to believe it was trying to surveil sensitive military sites, like Malmstrom Air Force Base in Montana. It was eventually shot down by the US off the East Coast on February 4, and the incident further escalated tensions between Washington and Beijing, including the postponement of a diplomatic visit by US Secretary of State Antony Blinken to China.

A senior State Department official said in February that, as the balloon floated across the US, it “was capable of conducting signals intelligence collection operations.”

Gen. Glen VanHerck, the commander of US Northern Command and NORAD, said at the time that the US “did not assess” that the balloon “presented a significant collection hazard beyond what already exists in actionable technical means from the Chinese.”

The surveillance program, which includes a number of similar balloons, is in part run out of the small Chinese province of Hainan, officials told CNN. The US does not know the precise size of the fleet of Chinese surveillance balloons, but sources tell CNN that the program has conducted at least two dozen missions over at least five continents in recent years.

Roughly half a dozen of those flights have been within US airspace, although not necessarily over US territory, according to one official familiar with the intelligence.

China has maintained that the balloon was actually just a weather balloon thrown off course, and the US has been assessing the possibility that it was not deliberately maneuvered into the continental US by the Chinese government, CNN has reported.

Still, China did maintain some ability to maneuver the balloon, officials believe. And once the balloon was over Montana, China appeared to take advantage of its position to loiter over sensitive sites and try to collect intelligence.

This story has been updated with additional reporting.

CNN’s Alex Marquardt contributed to this report.

#53. California investigates Nvidia over whether chip exports ended up in China

State attorney says scandal ‘raises bright red flags’ and aims to find out if company misled investors

California has served an investigative subpoena on Nvidia after a scandal that prompted the departure of co-founder and longtime chief executive Jensen Huang.

Rob Bonta, the US state’s attorney general, issued the subpoena as part of an investigation into whether exports of the California-based company’s advanced semiconductor chips ended up in China, contravening US restrictions.

“The conduct of Nvidia and its former chief executive raises bright red flags, including about the ways in which the company may have misled investors,” said Bonta.

“Nvidia is in a messy, transnational situation but the underlying principle of our corporate securities law is clear: companies cannot mislead their investors.”

The subpoena, issued on Thursday, followed Huang’s resignation a week ago after it emerged he had multiple affairs, including one with Alice Huang, a suspected Chinese spy.

Alice Huang, who is not related, is associated with Singapore-based Megaspeed, which has bought nearly $5bn (£3.8bn) of Nvidia chips and hardware in recent years, and is being investigated for possibly diverting advanced restricted chips to China.

Megaspeed has said it is a “legitimate and lawful” business, and that Huang left the company late last year.

Bonta said the investigation would also look at whether the former Nvidia chief’s actions resulted in confidential information being passed to China.

“My office is investigating whether Nvidia or Jensen Huang’s actions resulted in China obtaining advanced chips or confidential information,” he said. “And if Nvidia misled investors and the public about this risk, in violation of the law.”

Nvidia’s shares are down by a fifth since Huang admitted the extramarital affair, hitting a one-year low at the close of trading on Thursday, and wiping almost $1tn off its market value.

Earlier this week, Harvey Jones, Nvidia’s chair, said the company had no intention of reinstating Huang after the former CEO said he would be “honoured” to return. Elon Musk and a host of tech bosses had campaigned for Nvidia to bring him back.

Huang is due to testify before several congressional committees over the potential breach of national security on 1 December.

Get set for the working day – we'll point you to all the business news and analysis you need every morning

Shares in rival chipmaker AMD closed down almost 8% on Thursday after the publication of reports connected to short seller Hunterbrook warning about its links to China and the “circular financing” of customers.

The company, which has been targeted amid the Nvidia scandal, is looking at a further fall of more than 5% in pre-market trading.

Hunterbrook Media published allegations on the use of AMD’s software by a Chinese server maker blacklisted by the US. Sister publication, The Bear Cave newsletter, alleged that AMD’s use of datacentre lease guarantees and backstops for unsold cloud computing capacity could leave it exposed to a pullback in AI spending.

AMD said the reports’ claims “misrepresent AMD’s business practices and relationships”.

“AMD complies with applicable US export control laws and regulations and maintains rigorous processes to ensure compliance across our global operations,” the company said. “We remain focused on serving our customers and upholding the highest standards of integrity.”

AMD’s share price has more than doubled so far this year, giving the company a market capitalisation of more than $800m.

#51. Catherine Zeta-Jones' daughter says parents warned her about 'nepo baby' label

Carys Douglas said her Oscar-winning parents taught her that "building my own identity depended on me and no one else"

Catherine Zeta-Jones's daughter has said her parents warned her people might have "preconceived notions" about her embarking on an acting career, as she shared her views on the "nepo baby" label.

Carys Douglas, 23, made her stage debut this year, playing the lead role of Nina in Anton Chekhov's The Seagull in Brooklyn, New York.

Zeta-Jones, who is married to Michael Douglas, son of Hollywood star Kirk, previously said the "nepo baby" term was "horrific". It refers to children of famous parents perceived to have achieved riches and success off their parents' backs.

But the young actor said her parents taught her to build her "own identity" and she has learned how to deal with the "pressure".

During an interview with Elle Spain, Carys Douglas said her parents "do not hide" from her that people would have preconceived ideas about her.

"They taught me that building my own identity depended on me and no one else. It was my responsibility, and it still is.

"As time has gone on, I've come to understand that there are different ways to deal with that kind of pressure," she said.

Her father, star of hit 1980s films such as Wall Street and Fatal Attraction, recently stated that he is not a fan of the "nepo baby" label, calling it an "unfortunate title".

"I guess you could say that about any business. The same thing with Hollywood royalty. My father's father was a junkman," Douglas, 82, said, referring to Kirk, who was considered a Hollywood Golden Age icon.

Zeta-Jones, 57, from Mumbles, Swansea, also criticised the phrase, saying it was "horrible because these kids didn't ask to be born into this, they're finding their own way in life".

"You have to go with your dreams, your passion," Zeta-Jones said.

She added: "But they love it, and they're good. Otherwise, Michael and I would be the first to go, 'you know what? Maybe law school looks really good'."

Catherine Zeta Jones has declared the phrase "nepo baby" is "horrific"

Zeta-Jones' big break came when she landed a West End role in 42nd Street in 1987, before she became a household name in the UK in 1991 after starring in the ITV hit The Darling Buds Of May.

She has since become a global superstar, with roles in The Mask Of Zorro, Entrapment, Traffic and 2002's adaptation of Chicago, for which she won an Oscar for Best Supporting Actress.

The couple's children, Dylan and Carys, have both followed in their footsteps into acting.

Michael Douglas said his children have been performing "since they were in kindergarten".

"Every play, every show, summer theater camps, graduating... I mean, they've done so much work. It's unbelievable," he said.

Carys Douglas described Three Cliffs Bay in Gower Peninsula, Swansea, as "stunning"

Douglas and Zeta-Jones' children predominantly grew up in the US, but the family regularly travels to Wales, a place they are fond of.

Speaking to British Vogue last November, Carys Douglas said her summers were spent reading on her grandmother's sofa and travelling around the Gower Peninsula in Swansea.

"I feel so lucky that this is where I'm from," she told the magazine, reflecting on the "weird but magical places" scattered across the peninsula.

"Those beaches are some of the most beautiful in the world," she said.

She also spoke about her "theatrical" family's traditions, recalling how "there'd be lots of little competitions for singing and pantomime shows all summer".

"It's true, the Welsh love to sing," she added.

#47. Nasdaq erases all 2026 gains as AI sell-off dampens Anthropic’s second day of trading

The Nasdaq lost trillions in value, with the AI startup’s IPO failing to buoy it as investors question huge sums being poured into AI buildout

Who are Anthropic’s seven co-founders, now worth $180bn?

As Anthropic’s stock dropped during its second day of trading, the wider stock market dipped with it. On Friday, the Nasdaq Composite erased all its gains for 2026 and closed down 5% from its value on 1 January. The Dow Jones Industrial Average and S&P 500 closed in correction territory.

Anthropic’s record-breaking IPO failed to stem a global sell-off in AI-linked companies, and the tech-heavy Nasdaq erased some $4tn in market value this week, according to the Wall Street Journal.

Shares in the frontier AI company behind the Claude chatbot, which floated in the US on Thursday, fell by 9% on Friday, ceding most of the gains from the biggest initial public offering in history. They dropped to $88.30, towards the $83 level at which Anthropic priced its stock market debut.

Anthropic’s first two days as a listed company have been volatile. Its shares jumped by 17% on Thursday, after briefly surging by almost 40% when trading in the firm began.

US technology stocks followed Anthropic down on Friday, with the memory chipmaker SanDisk falling 14% and rival Micron down 12%. Nvidia fell 14%, eliminating hundreds of billions of dollars in market cap.

Neil Wilson, a UK investor strategist at Saxo, said there was “just relentless selling in tech”. He warned: “Anthropic shares tumbled to take an $85 handle as the post-IPO euphoria gives way to the reality of a steep sell-off across the tech and AI space that shows no sign of easing just yet.”

Global markets slumped on Friday too; Asian stock markets recorded their worst week since 2020, as shares in big chip companies fell. In Taiwan, stocks dropped 6.7% to hit their lowest point since mid-April, with the chip maker TSMC down about 6%.

The scale of the decline in Taiwan prompted the chair of its stock exchange to pledge to ensure the stable operation ⁠of capital markets, and reassure investors they should remain confident in the fundamentals of Taiwanese companies.

In Japan, the Nikkei index fell 5%, while South Korea’s tech-heavy Kospi index lost 5.5%. Shares in the tech investor SoftBank had their worst day in more than two years, down 14.5% in Tokyo, amid worries around its aggressive bets on AI.

European stock markets posted their biggest weekly loss since March 2025, with the pan-European Stoxx 600 dropping 1.9% on Friday. London’s blue-chip stock index hit a five-month low.

The sell-off followed losses on Wall Street on Thursday, where the main indices all fell by more than 2% on a day dominated by Anthropic’s IPO.

Chris Beauchamp, the chief market analyst at the investing and trading platform IG, said Anthropic could be “draining market liquidity”, as investors sell shares in other companies to raise funds to pay for stock in the newly floated AI firm.

Get set for the working day – we'll point you to all the business news and analysis you need every morning

“The slide from yesterday’s highs sends a message that there might be more at work here than investors realise, and while brokers are scrambling to buy more, that liquidity has to come from somewhere, and the other tech names may be the first ones to feel the pressure,” Beauchamp said.

Anthropic’s flotation has failed to ease worries around the AI trade, as investors increasingly question whether companies can deliver the returns necessary to justify the billions of dollars being poured into the AI buildout.

Daniela Hathorn, a senior market analyst at Capital.com, said: “The IPO demonstrated that demand for AI exposure remains strong, but it has not been enough to overcome concerns about elevated valuations, profitability and the sustainability of investment spending.

“The broader market mood has shifted from buying the dip towards questioning whether the correction has further to run. The weakness in equal-weighted indices suggests that selling pressure is no longer confined to a handful of technology giants, while Europe’s recent outperformance has also faded.”

Nvidia shares fell byafter California served the chip company with a subpoena as part of an investigation into whether its advanced semiconductors ended up in China.

Britain’s FTSE 100 share index dropped by 5.1% this week, its biggest weekly fall since the start of the Iran war.

Fears that US central bankers may raise interest rates to fight inflation have lifted the dollar to a 19-month high, and pushed down the gold price – with gold on track for its worst week since March.

Bitcoin hit its lowest level in four months, dropping below the $60,000 point for the first time since early July.
