#46. Texas death-row inmate screams as execution takes more than one hour

Reinaldo Dennes was convicted over the murder of a diamond dealer in 1996

A US death row inmate has screamed in apparent pain during his Texas execution which lasted more than an hour.

This is "not working the way it should be," Reinaldo Dennes, 70, said as he was being injected with the drug pentobarbital, according to witnesses.

A spokeswoman for the Texas Department of Criminal Justice said there had been unsuccessful attempts to find veins for the injections, but added that Dennes had reported pain in parts of his body where there were no attempted injections.

Dennes's case comes weeks after the botched execution of Christa Pike, who survived two lethal doses and is believed to be the only individual to have survived an execution attempt by lethal injection.

The Texas Department of Criminal Justice on Friday denied any claim that Dennes's execution was "botched", adding that the agency followed its protocols in carrying it out successfully.

"The overall process took about an hour, which is not atypical," department spokeswoman Amanda Hernandez told the BBC.

"The agency took its time to ensure the IVs were inserted into viable veins, placing them in the left foot and left side of the neck," she said, adding that the allegations of pain made by Dennes "began prior to any drugs being administered".

Dennes was sentenced to death over the 1996 murder of Janos Szucs, a diamond dealer who was found shot multiple times.

His execution came on the same day another inmate was put to death in Florida.

Executions with a lethal drug usually last from a few minutes to over an hour.

Michael Graczyk, an Associated Press reporter who has witnessed many such executions, described how they had waited for an unusually long time before being taken into the chamber. According to him, such delays are usually due to an inmate resisting the procedure or difficulty in finding veins.

When the witnesses were finally allowed in, Dennes almost immediately exclaimed, "The pain! Go! Go!", Graczyk said. He then uttered an expletive and asked "the father" to "take this pain".

He then shouted: "My right leg is killing me!", describing it as a cramp. After another expletive, he grunted and said, "I don't think this is normal."

Another witness, the reverend Jeff Hood, told USA Today that Dennes's execution was "by far the worst one" he had seen.

"He [Dennes] screamed out, 'my leg, my leg, my leg'," Hood said. "He screamed out twice, 'it's not working like it's supposed to.'"

How widely used is the death penalty in the US?

The use of pentobarbital has been under renewed scrutiny since Pike, a 50-year-old convicted murderer, survived two attempts at execution via the lethal injection in September. She was taken to hospital in a critical condition and has since returned to prison.

Her botched execution captivated and shocked the world.

Pentobarbital is a potent sedative that slows down the body, including the nervous system. Doctors sometimes use it as a treatment for certain conditions, but in high doses it can also be used as a lethal injection to cause death.

Its use in lethal injections came under renewed scrutiny last year after a US federal government review during the Biden administration raised concerns about the drug's potential for "unnecessary pain and suffering".

The Biden government banned its use in federal executions, but that did not bind individual states, which can choose their own methods.

I've seen nearly 500 executions - but never one like Christa Pike's

Could Christa Pike face execution again and by what method?

What happened in Christa Pike's failed US execution, and how did she survive?

#40. Nearly 600 women killed in South Africa within three months

The killing of 13 women around Johannesburg earlier this year sparked a national outcry

Nearly 600 women were killed in South Africa between July and September this year, according to the country's latest crime statistics.

This figure includes 13 women whose partially naked and bruised bodies were found dumped in public areas in and around Johannesburg.

The murders triggered national outrage and police faced pressure to find the culprits, in a country with one of the highest rates of gender-based violence and murder rates in the world.

In the latest shocking killing, five women and a toddler were gunned down in Free State province earlier this week.

The latest crime statistics, released on Friday, revealed that 581 women were killed in the country between July and September, including 131 in the Gauteng province which includes Johannesburg.

The same statistics revealed a slight decline in the number of murders.

Police reported 5,590 murders, 204 fewer than in the same period last year. This still come to an average of about 60 murders a day.

It is not clear whether the number of women killed is an increase or decrease because the police had stopped giving these figures recently due to what they said were "significant anomalies" in the statistics.

Grim discoveries of murdered women spark fear in South Africa

Acting Police Minister Firoz Cachalia said there needed to be "greater efforts to prevent... violence" against women and children.

According to police in Free State province, south of Johannesburg, three masked men opened fire on a family in the township of Botshabelo on Wednesday night.

The trio were allegedly looking for the owner of the house and when they realised he was not there, "forced their way into the house and started shooting at the people inside", police said.

The owner's wife was also shot in the stomach and was taken to hospital. No arrests have been made.

On Thursday, the police chief for Gauteng province said that eight people had now been arrested in relations to the 13 murders which sparked a national outcry earlier this year.

General Tommy Mthombeni said three of the arrests related to the murder of Elizabeth Moselakgomo, an avid runner whose disappearance prompted a highly publicised nationwide search for her.

The 38-year-old went missing while out for her daily run near her home in Kempton Park.

Her body was found badly bruised and partially clothed on 12 September.

The police chief said two other people had been detained in connection with the death of Jabulile Ntimba, 33, who was discovered half-naked and wrapped in a sheet on the side of a road in Boksburg on 17 September. Ntimba's was the ninth body found in the Ekurhuleni region.

He said another person had been arrested for one of the Kempton Park cases while a suspect was arrested over Ntombifuthi Nxumalo's death in Tembisa, north-east of central Johannesburg.

Nxumalo was discovered at a cemetery on 26 September. She was found naked with serious head injuries as well as stones near her body.

Another arrest was made back in July, in relation to the death of Busisiwe Mahlangu, whose body was found in Kempton Park on 15 July. Hers was the first of the 13 bodies discovered in the area.

In September, President Cyril Ramaphosa vowed that "no stone will be left unturned" to find those behind the killings.

Last year, he declared that violence against women was a "national disaster".

#39. Trump threatens ‘tremendous penalties’ for US tech firms that build datacenters abroad

President gives wide-ranging White House press conference and says he has ‘fantastic relationships’ with Putin and Xi

Donald Trump threatened on Friday to impose “tremendous penalties” on American tech companies that build datacenters outside the US, though he gave no details and told reporters he had not yet informed his own officials about the plan.

In a discursive press conference from the White House briefing room, Trump also said he would “look into” the report that Iran had been planning to possibly detonate a “dirty bomb” in Washington DC, and backed JD Vance over comments the vice-president made blaming Republican midterm losses on the Iran war.

Trump also expounded on his friendship with the Russian president Vladimir Putin, whom he met alongside China’s Xi Jinping at the Apec summit on Thursday, adding he had “fantastic relationships” with both autocratic leaders.

At the White House, Trump told reporters he wanted datacenters built by US companies to stay on US soil.

“We’re not allowing datacenters to go into other countries, because China will take all of them, and we’re not allowing that to happen. And we’re going to put tremendous penalties on companies that try to do it,” he said.

He said Americans should welcome datacenters, and urged communities to extract concessions from developers, repeating a point he made before the midterms.

“You ought to take advantage of the datacenters. Make them pay you, make them pay your community, but don’t shut them off,” he said.

With many Americans turning against the datacenters being built near their homes, the US tech industry is making a show of conciliation by promising to take fewer government handouts, to be more transparent, and to give more money to towns and cities willing to allow server warehouses to be built.

The Trump administration on Monday issued new rules to restrict remote access to advanced US computing chips and servers, in a move it said was designed to limit China’s ability to use US chips to train its artificial intelligence models – though a House committee said on Wednesday the US had signed off on sales of nearly 900,000 AI chips to China in six months.

China’s ability to obtain advanced AI chips made by the US firm Nvidia, in which Trump is an investor, has been spotlighted in recent weeks after its CEO, Jensen Huang, was fired over his affair with a woman alleged to be spying for China.

In the same press conference, Trump was asked about reports Iran was planning to potentially detonate an explosive laced with nuclear material – a so-called “dirty bomb” – in Washington DC. The president called it a “rumour” and said: “If they do, we’re going to fight.”

He added: “We know a lot about what’s happening in Iran. We know everything they say. We know who they are, where they are. We’ll find out about it.”

Responding to a question about reports of a rift with Vance, Trump said the vice-president’s remark blaming the Republican collapse on the Iran war was “a very accurate statement”.

“I didn’t say I was unhappy with it,” the president said. “I would have added one other thing if it were asked … would you do it again? And absolutely, the answer is yes, because we cannot allow Iran to have a nuclear weapon.”

Trump was asked whether Vladimir Putin could really be considered a friend to America since Russia is a close ally of Iran.

“I can’t speak to America,” he said, adding: “I consider [that] I have fantastic relationships with Putin. That’s a very good thing. I have fantastic relationships with President Xi. That’s a good thing, and our country is safe because of that.”

He praised Putin as he repeated his grievances over the Department of Justice’s investigation into the Russian attempts to influence US voters ahead of the 2016 presidential election.

“I’ve been dealing with Putin … [since] the fake Russia, Russia, Russia hoax,” he said.

Trump also spoke about two prominent Democrats. He said Gina Hinojosa, who won the race for Texas governor over Trump’s Republican ally Greg Abbott, called him. “She was really nice,” Trump said. “It was a very productive call. And she wants to have a strong border, and I think we’ll be able to work something out.”

And he said Hakeem Jeffries – who Trump previously called a “thug” but is now in line to be the next speaker of the House, and the first Black speaker – is “a smart guy. He’s a streetwise guy.”

#37. Russian authorities detain suspect over St. Petersburg cafe blast

Our live coverage for the day has ended. Follow the latest Ukraine news here or read through the updates below.

The preventive detention hearing for Daria Trepova, who was detained in connection with the explosion at a cafe in St. Petersburg, Russia, will take place on Tuesday at Basmanny District Court in Moscow, according to Russian state news agency Vesti.

Russian authorities detained Trepova, 26, claiming she was involved in the blast that killed well-known military blogger Vladlen Tatarsky at a café in St. Petersburg on Sunday.

The US Ambassador to the United Nations Linda Thomas-Greenfield on Monday described Russia assuming the United Nations Security Council (UNSC) presidency this month as “an April Fool’s joke.”

“But the truth of the matter is, it’s a rotating seat. We expect that they will behave professionally. But we also expect that they will use their seat to spread disinformation and to promote their own agenda as it relates to Ukraine, and we will stand ready to call them out at every single moment that they attempt to do that,” Greenfield added.

Greenfield said she is not surprised if the Russians asked the foreign minister to come to UNSC headquarters in New York but also said council does work beyond Ukraine.

“We haven’t decided yet on what our attendance levels will be, but we intend to carry out the business of the Security Council during this month. The Security Council does more than Ukraine. We work on many issues, and we again expect that Russia will carry their presidency in a professional way, but when they don’t, we will stand ready to call them out,” Greenfield said.

Vasily Nebenzya, Russian ambassador to the United Nations, dismissed concerns that Russia could fairly be the president of the Security Council for April during the ongoing war in Ukraine.

He said there were no complaints in February 2022 when Russia was last president of the council, while Russia invaded and pointed out that the US was president of the council in 2003 — the year Iraq was invaded.

The ambassador said as long as world order is maintained, there will not be any change in UN procedures that might lead to a change in Russia’s status.

Russia took over presidency on Saturday of the UN’s top security body, which rotates every month.

Newly published video shows the moments running up to an explosion that killed Russian military journalist Vladen Tartovsky in a St Petersburg café on Sunday.

Tatarsky died when a blast tore through the cafe where he was appearing as a guest of a pro-war group called Cyber Front Z. Prior Russian media reports suggested that Tatarsky may have been killed by a device hidden in a figurine presented to him by a woman before the blast.

The 25-second video shows Tartovsky putting a statuette given to him at the event into a box after passing the microphone to another man.

He places the statuette back into the box and then covers it with what appears to be paper wrapping. As he presses down the paper, there is an explosion.

In the last two seconds of the video, Tartovsky is pixillated, but the rest of the scene is not.

More to know: The well-known Russian blogger was killed in what appeared to be an audacious attack on a high-profile pro-Kremlin figure, officials said.

Authorities said they were treating the case as suspected murder.

At least 32 people were injured in the blast, with 10 people in serious condition, state media Ria Novosti reported, citing the Russian Ministry of Health.

Russian ambassador to the United Nations Vasily Nebenzya on Monday scoffed at concerns that his country could fairly be the president of the Security Council for April during the war in Ukraine.

Nebenzya pointed out that the US was president of the council in 2003 — the year Iraq was invaded.

He said there were no complaints in February 2022 when Russia was last president of the council, while Russia invaded.

The ambassador said as long as world order is maintained, there will not be any change in UN procedures that might lead to a change in Russia’s status.

Russia took over presidency on Saturday of the UN’s top security body, which rotates every month.

Ukraine has received the first tranche of $2.7 billion from a new International Monetary Fund (IMF) program, Ukrainian Finance Minister Sergii Marchenko said in a tweet Monday.

The IMF, which regularly makes emergency loans to countries in crisis, on Friday approved a new four-year extended arrangement under the Extended Fund Facility (EFF) of around $15.6 billion as part of a $115 billion total support package for Ukraine.

The program aims to “anchor policies that sustain fiscal, external, price and financial stability and support economic recovery, while enhancing governance and strengthening institutions to promote long-term growth in the context of post-war reconstruction and Ukraine’s path to EU accession,” the IMF said in a statement.

The program also helps Ukraine to carry out “more ambitious structural reforms,” it said.

The Extended Fund Facility (EFF) loan is the first major conventional financing program approved by the IMF for a country involved in a large-scale war, Reuters reported.

The risks to the arrangement are “exceptionally high,” said Gita Gopinath, first deputy managing director of the global lender.

“The success of the program depends on the size, composition, and timing of external financing on concessional terms to help close fiscal and external financing gaps and restore debt sustainability on a forward-looking basis under the baseline and downside scenarios,” she added.

German Chancellor Olaf Scholz on Monday assured Moldova of Berlin’s support on its path to accession to the European Union as Moldovan and American officials allege Russia of trying to weaken the government in Chisinau.

“Moldova is part of our European family. In the summer, we granted it candidate status. And I very much welcome how resolutely Moldova has tackled the necessary reforms that are indispensable for EU accession,” Scholz told a news conference in Bucharest, Romania alongside Romanian President Klaus Iohannis and Moldovan President Maia Sandu.

“Moldova can be sure of our support on this path. I assured the President [Sandu] of this once again today. Moldova does not stand alone, but receives massive international support,” the German chancellor continued.

Scholz expressed “great concern” about reports of alleged Russian attempts to destabilize Moldova and said Germany would do its “utmost” to support Moldova in arming itself against “attempts of destabilization by Russia.”

In February, Moldova’s President Sandu accused Russia of planning to use “saboteurs who have undergone military training and are disguised as civilians” to destabilize the country — claims which were rejected by Russia’s foreign ministry as “unfounded.”

According to White House officials, the US believes that Russia is working to weaken the Moldovan government, as it continues to seek closer ties with the European Union.

“The sovereignty and territorial integrity of any state is inviolable. This requirement of the Helsinki Final Act and other agreements under international law was also signed by Russia. And it is still valid. Therefore, we do our utmost to support Moldova in arming itself against attempts of destabilization by Russia,” Scholz said.

Speaking at the same event, Sandu said that “it is very important, and I am happy that Moldova is a dialogue partner with Romania and Germany. The projects we are involved in together are very useful for our people and will strongly lead us to the accession (to the European Union).”

CNN’s Radina Gigova, Anna Chernova and Natasha Bertrand contributed reporting to this post.

NATO Secretary General Jens Stoltenberg said Monday that the alliance has not seen any changes in Russia’s nuclear posture since Russian President Vladimir Putin announced late last month Moscow’s plans to station tactical nuclear weapons in neighboring Belarus.

“We monitor very closely what Russia does. But so far, we haven’t seen any changes in their nuclear posture that requires any change in our nuclear posture,” the NATO chief said, while answering questions from reporters on the topic at a news conference in Brussels.

Stoltenberg described Putin’s announcement as a “pattern of dangerous, reckless nuclear rhetoric.”

“President Putin tries to use nuclear weapons as a way to prevent us from supporting Ukraine… intimidation, coercion to stop NATO Allies and partners for supporting Ukraine in their right to defend their own country,” he said.

“We will not be intimidated. We will continue to support Ukraine,” Stoltenberg continued.

NATO’s chief Jens Stoltenberg said on Monday that Western allies have delivered more than $70 billion in military aid to Ukraine over the last year since the Russian invasion started.

“There are no signs that President Putin is preparing for peace. He is preparing for more war,” Stoltenberg warned, speaking at a news conference in Brussels.

“We do not know when this war will end. But when it does, we will need to put in place arrangements so that Ukraine can deter future aggression. And history does not repeat itself. We cannot allow Russia to continue to chip away at European security,” he said.

Stoltenberg added that allies have delivered 65 billion euros (around $70.7 billion) in military aid, and said he welcomed the recent arrival of modern battle tanks and other armored vehicles in Ukraine.

“This can make a real difference on the front lines and allow the Ukrainian forces to liberate more territory,” he said.

The NATO head affirmed that the alliance’s support is for the “long haul” and said, “we will discuss how we can step up our support, including by continuing to strengthen Ukraine’s armed forces and supporting the transition from Soviet-era to NATO equipment and doctrine.”

Stoltenberg also said allies must address other threats and challenges, citing instability, terrorism, and the growing influence of Iran, Russia, and China. He urged allies to invest more in defense to tackle all these wide range of issues.

Norway and Denmark will cooperate to donate 8,000 artillery rounds to Ukraine, the Norwegian government announced in a statement on Monday.

“Norway donates the shells, while Denmark donates the associated fuzes, propellant bags and primer cartridges,” the statement said.

Ukrainian President Volodymyr Zelensky reacted to the announcement, saying in a tweet that he was grateful for the donation.

“Grateful to the Danish and Norwegian governments led by @Statsmin and @jonasgahrstore for the joint initiative to transfer an additional batch of artillery shells to Ukraine. Timely military assistance from partners is the key to bringing our joint victory closer!” Zelensky said.

The rounds will be “standard NATO 155 mm artillery ammunition and will supplement the Danish donation of 19 Caesar self-propelled howitzers,” Norway’s statement added.

Norway’s artillery shells will be delivered from the country’s armed forces’ own stock, according to the statement.

Norwegian defense minister Bjørn Arild Gram said “Ukraine has a significant need for artillery ammunition. Norway will contribute where we can. It is important for both Europe’s and Norway’s security that Ukraine succeeds in standing up to Russia’s attack.”

“The Ukrainians still have a great and urgent need for ammunition for their fight for freedom against Russia. Denmark, together with other EU and NATO countries, is in the process of accommodating with a number of different military contributions. Our cooperation with Norway is an important example of this,” the Danish acting defense minister, Troels Lund Poulsen, said in the statement.

The Ukrainian military has reported little change to the front lines but heavy fire from Russian forces at various parts of the front line in the Donetsk region.

The General Staff said that more than 45 enemy attacks had been repelled over the past day, with the focus on Lyman, Bakhmut, Avdiivka and Mariinka.

It said there were 17 air strikes and more than 20 strikes by multiple launch rocket systems.

“In the Bakhmut direction, the enemy is trying to take full control over the city of Bakhmut and continues to storm it,” the General Staff said.

West of the city, Ukrainian defense forces repelled about 20 enemy attacks, according to the General Staff.

Oleksandr Syrskyi, commander of Ukrainian Land Forces, posted on Telegram that in Bakhmut he had met with “soldiers and commanders who destroyed the myth of invincibility of the Wagner fighters and Russian paratroopers.”

“The enemy is weakening and trying to cover up its failures with new fakes about the capture of Bakhmut,” Syrskyi said.

Syrskyi referenced the possibility of a future counter-offensive, saying that “the forward movement of our entire army depends on what every soldier is doing right now on their section of the front.”

Some of the most intense fighting is to the northwest and west of the city of Donetsk – as the Russians continue months-long efforts to dislodge Ukrainian defenses in Avdiivka and Mariinka. The General Staff said that some 20 assaults had been repelled.

“There are also some operational successes of our military in the Donetsk area. Our military have taken some positions. They have taken a more strategic and favourable position,” said Oleksii Dmytrashkivskyi, spokesperson for the military in that zone.

He also noted that Russian special forces had been introduced in the area.

“The enemy is trying to attack our positions between Avdiivka and Marinka. These attempts do not stop. It should be noted that the enemy has suffered quite significant losses,” Dmytrashkivskyi said.

He also claimed that the Russians “don’t have the same amount of equipment and, as they themselves say, they are on a ‘diet’ of ammunition for assaults.”

Social media video showed both sides engaged in heavy fire in the Avdiivka area, which the Russians have surrounded on three sides.

Denis Pushilin, the leader of the self-declared Donetsk People’s Republic, claimed that Russian forces were still advancing in the Avdiivka direction.

“It is still premature to speak of encirclement, but the situation is being created, if not critical, then extremely difficult for the enemy… After clearing Kamianka, our units are moving further. There are difficulties both with the terrain, which must also be overcome, and there are also similarities with weather conditions. But these difficulties are mutual — both for the enemy and for us,” Pushilin said.

The war in Ukraine has cost an estimated $2.6 billion of damage to heritage and cultural sites in the country, the UN cultural body UNESCO said in a recently published report.

The report, which covers one year of war in Ukraine between February 24, 2022, and February 24, 2023, was a joint assessment conducted by the Ukrainian government, the World Bank, the European Commission, and the United Nations.

UNESCO was in charge of the part of the report dedicated to culture and tourism.

“As of February 24, 2023, the total damage cost from identified assets is estimated at $2.6 billion,” the report said in its assessment of damage related to culture and tourism.

Some $1.7 billion of that cost was distributed to “historic cities, buildings, and sites imbued with recognized cultural/social values,” $650 million to tourism facilities, $143 million to “movable cultural properties and collections, repositories of culture,” and $150 million to “buildings/workshops/ateliers dedicated to cultural and creative industries,” according to UNESCO.

The most impacted region in Ukraine was Kharkivska, which has suffered 30% of the total damage to its cultural assets, the report added.

UNESCO said that losses are estimated at around $15.2 billion, which includes revenue losses from tourism, art, sports, entertainment, recreation, cultural education, and creative and cultural industries.

“The war has significantly impacted the diversity and richness of culture and cultural heritage in Ukraine, causing damage to cultural infrastructure and assets, reducing livelihoods for cultural creators, bearers and practitioners, limiting access to culture, and impeding the exercise of cultural rights,” the report said.

UNESCO’s head Audrey Azoulay is currently in Ukraine, the organization told CNN on Monday.

NATO Secretary General Jens Stoltenberg said it will be historic when the military alliance raises the Finnish flag for the first time at its headquarters on Tuesday.

“This is a historic week. Tomorrow we will welcome Finland as the 31st member of NATO, making Finland safer and our alliance stronger. We will raise the Finnish flag for the first time here at NATO Headquarters,” Stoltenberg said, speaking in Brussels Monday.

“It will be a good day for Finland’s security, for Nordic security, and for NATO as a whole,” Stoltenberg added.

Finland is set to become a member of the alliance on Tuesday, with Finnish President Sauli Niinistö traveling to Brussels to attend an accession ceremony at NATO headquarters.

Stoltenberg also spoke about Sweden’s bid to join NATO, saying Finland’s accession is “in itself something we should celebrate” but is also good for Sweden.

“It makes Sweden more integrated into NATO and makes Sweden even more safer,” Stoltenberg said, adding “at the same time we celebrate and enjoy that Finland is now full-fledged member, we should continue work to finalize the Swedish accession process.”

CNN’s Laurent Kent contributed to this report.

At least 501 children have been killed in Ukraine since February 2022, when Russia launched its full-scale invasion, said Catherine Russell, executive director of the United Nations Children’s Fund (UNICEF).

“Another tragic milestone for Ukraine’s children and families,” Russell said in a tweet on Monday.

Russell warned the real figure is “likely far higher” than the numbers verified by the UN agency.

Additionally, almost 1,000 Ukrainian children have been injured, “leaving them with wounds and scars - both visible and invisible - that could last for life,” she added.

The head of the United Nations nuclear agency, International Atomic Energy Agency, will travel to the Russian enclave of Kaliningrad in the Baltics Wednesday to discuss the safety of Ukraine’s Zaporizhzhia nuclear power plant, a spokesperson for the agency told CNN.

“IAEA Director General Rafael Mariano Grossi will visit Kaliningrad on Wednesday as part of his ongoing consultations aimed at ensuring the protection of Ukraine’s Zaporizhzhia Nuclear Power Plant during the military conflict,” the spokesperson said Monday.

The plant has been occupied by Russian forces since March of last year and is now run by the Russian atomic agency, Rosatom.

The situation at the nuclear plant has not improved, according to the IAEA chief, who visited the facility last week and cited increased military activity in the area.

Grossi during the visit said he was trying to formulate “realistic, viable proposals” that would be acceptable to both sides.

Twelve Ukrainian prisoners have been returned home from captivity – 10 military and two civilians, Ukraine says.

Five of them were seriously wounded, according to the Coordination Headquarters for the Treatment of Prisoners of War.

The soldiers were captured in various parts of the front lines, it added.

On March 24, “Ukraine voluntarily and unconditionally returned to Russia all five severely wounded prisoners who were transportable,” the headquarters said, but added that Russia had released “only five seriously wounded Ukrainians, not all of them, as required by the international conventions signed by Russia.”

The Russian decision showed “a blatant lack of interest in establishing further exchanges for particularly vulnerable categories of prisoners and civilian hostages,” the headquarters said.

Since the beginning of the invasion in February last year, 2,005 Ukrainians have been released from captivity, according to the headquarters.

Wagner boss Yevgeny Prigozhin said Monday that he “gave” the St. Petersburg cafe, where an explosion killed Russian military blogger Vladlen Tatarsky on Sunday, to the Cyber Front Z group, which hosted an event at the cafe.

When asked to confirm whether he was the owner of the cafe, Prigozhin didn’t deny it, in comments published Monday by his holding company Concord on its VK social media page.

“Indeed, I gave the cafe to the patriotic movement Cyber Front Z, and they held various seminars there,” Prigozhin said.

Russia’s independent news outlet Fontanka is reporting that the cafe belongs to Prigozhin, on the basis of the Concord company’s ownership mentioned on the cafe’s receipt.

Prigozhin is the founder and sole owner of Concord, according to public records.

CNN has not been able to independently verify Prigozhin’s claims and whether he is the legal owner of the cafe.

In the same post, Prigozhin is also commenting on who may be behind the blast.

“I do not know the details of the incident now, I was only informed that, unfortunately, Vladlen Tatarsky died. I don’t know about the rest of the details,” Prigozhin said.

“Most likely, this tragedy happened at the seminar. As for the death of Darya Dugina, yes, everything is similar,” he said referencing the death of the daughter of ultra-nationalist philosopher Alexander Dugin, who was killed by a car bomb on the outskirts of Moscow last year.

“But I would not blame the Kyiv regime for these actions. I think that there is a group of radicals that is hardly related to the government. That’s what I would call it,” Prigozhin said.

Ukrainian President Volodymyr Zelensky has declined to make any comment about the explosion that killed Russian military journalist Vladlen Tatarsky in St. Petersburg on Sunday.

Zelensky said while on a tour of the northern Chernihiv region, “About St. Petersburg. I’m not thinking what is going on in St. Petersburg, or in Moscow. They have to think. Russia have to think about their cities. I’m thinking about our country. And our cities.”

Russia has arrested an anti-war activist named Daria Trepova in connection with the explosion that killed Tatarsky and injured at least 32 others at a cafe on Sunday.

Trepova’s husband Dmitry Rylov told the independent Russian publication The Insider that he is convinced his wife “was really just set up and used.”

Some more context: Kremlin spokesperson Dmitry Peskov has said Ukraine may be behind the explosion. “There is evidence that the Ukrainian special services may be involved in the planning of this terrorist attack,” Peskov said. However, no evidence has yet been presented about who carried out the bombing.

Ukraine has said little about the explosion, beyond blaming in-fighting in Russia.

Two independent organizations in Russia — the Libertarian party and Russian opposition leader Alexey Navalny’s Anti-Corruption Foundation — have denied any association with Trepova.

Wall Street Journal reporter Evan Gershkovich has filed an appeal against his arrest, according to the Russian state news agency TASS.

The agency cited the press service of the Lefortovo court in Moscow, where he was taken into custody last week.

“The court received a complaint from Gershkovich’s defense against the choice of a preventive measure in the form of detention,” TASS reported.

No date for hearing the appeal has been set.

Gershkovich is currently being held in a pre-trial detention center at the notorious Lefortovo prison until May 29. He faces up to 20 years in prison on espionage charges.

It is the first time an American journalist has been detained on accusations by Moscow of spying since the Cold War.

#35. Indian man sentenced to 19-plus years in plot to assassinate US-based Sikh separatist leader

Watch: Inside court as Nikhil Gupta sentenced to 19 years over assassination plot

A federal judge sentenced an Indian man to 235 months, more than 19 years, in an attempted plot to kill a leading Sikh political figure in the US.

Nikhil Gupta, an Indian national, pleaded guilty to murder-for-hire, conspiracy to commit murder-for-hire, and conspiracy to commit money laundering in February. On Friday, the judge said he likely will be deported or removed after his sentence.

US prosecutors alleged Gupta was directed by an Indian government official to kill Gurpatwant Singh Pannun, a leading figure of the Khalistan movement, which advocates for an independent homeland for Sikhs.

India has denied involvement. Its government has accused Pannun of being a terrorist, which he denies.

Pannun, an American citizen, is the spokesman for US-based Sikh separatist group Sikhs for Justice (SFJ), banned in India since 2019.

He previously told the BBC that "Nikhil Gupta's guilty plea was judicial confirmation that India's Modi government orchestrated a structured murder-for-hire assassination plot on American soil".

India has opposed the Khalistan movement, and its political parties also remain opposed to the matter. Sikhs are a religious minority that make up about 2% of India's population. Some groups have long called for a separate homeland for Sikhs.

Nikhil Gupta (seen in a 2024 courtroom sketch) pleaded guilty in a plot to kill Gurpatwant Singh Pannun, a U.S. resident who advocated for a sovereign Sikh state in northern India

Prosecutors alleged that in May 2023, Gupta was recruited by Vikash Yadav, an Indian intelligence officer, to carry out an assassination of Pannun.

Yadav allegedly provided Gupta with personal information about Pannun, including his address in New York and information about his daily activities, prosecutors said.

At his handler's direction, Gupta contacted someone to facilitate the assassination, but that person was actually a US government informant, prosecutors said. The informant introduced Gupta to a "hitman", who was actually an undercover officer with the Drug Enforcement Agency.

Gupta was arrested in June 2023 at an airport in Prague, Czech Republic and extradited to the US. He has been held at a jail in New York pending his sentencing Friday at a Manhattan court.

"He thought that from outside this country he could kill someone in it without consequence, simply for exercising their American right to free speech," then-US attorney Jay Clayton said in a statement, after announcing Gupta's guilty plea.

Following Gupta's arrest, a spokesperson for India's Ministry of External Affairs said that Yadav is no longer employed, external by the Indian government.

The country also set up an inquiry committee to address security concerns raised by the US, the spokesperson said.

Narinder Singh Randhawa, a Sikh separatist and successor of Hardeep Singh Nijjar as temple leader in Surrey, British Columbia, told the BBC the sentencing marks the "first time we see justice" against what he calls historic and systemic persecution of Sikhs by the Indian government in India and abroad.

Inderjeet Gosal, another Sikh separatist advocate based in Ontario, called the sentencing "a big community event".

"It's for all the community to finally witness someone getting caught red-handed."

Indian man admits plot to kill US-based Sikh separatist leader

Who was Canadian Sikh leader Hardeep Singh Nijjar?

Why are some Sikhs calling for a separate state?

Canada accuses India of role in Sikh leader's murder

India crushed a violent armed Sikh insurgency in the 1980s and 1990s aimed at demanding the creation of Khalistan. The movement has little support in India now although some in the Sikh diaspora continue to advocate for Khalistan.

Pannun is facing serious charges, including in connection with terrorism, in India, but he denies the allegations, saying he is an activist who just believes in Khalistan.

In a victim impact statement to the court, Pannun said the foiled assassination attempt has not deterred his activism.

"I am entirely willing to take India's bullets if that is the price required to achieve freedom," he wrote.

Gupta's case placed increased scrutiny on a separate incident, involving the 2023 murder of a Sikh separatist leader in British Columbia, which led to increased tensions between India and Canada.

In September 2023, then-Canadian Prime Minister Justin Trudeau said there were "credible allegations potentially linking" India to the murder of Hardeep Singh Nijjar, another advocate for the creation of Khalistan and an associate of Pannun's.

Nijjar was shot and killed by two assailants outside a Sikh temple in British Columbia. India also has denied involvement in his death.

The day after Niijar's murder, US prosecutors allege that Gupta told the undercover officer Nijjar "was also the target" and "we have so many targets."

#33. Caribbean nations fear economic impact of shuttering citizenship program after EU threats

St Kitts and Nevis to wind down citizenship-by-investment program after EU warns of end to visa-free access

The decision by St Kitts and Nevis to end a lucrative programme offering citizenship to wealthy foreigners in exchange for investment threatens its economy and ability to support people in need, the premier of the island of Nevis has warned.

On Friday, Terrance Drew, the prime minister of the twin-island Caribbean nation, announced he would wind down the citizenship-by-investment (CBI) programme, after the European Union threatened to end visa-free access for St Kitts and Nevis.

Mark Brantley, the premier of Nevis, which has autonomous self-governance in some aspects, said he understood that the “country was given very little choice”, but did not “support the winding down of the program”.

The 42-year-old CBI programme, which St Kitts and Nevis pioneered, allows wealthy foreigners to obtain citizenship and ultimately a passport by investing in infrastructure and other development projects. It has brought billions of dollars into the country and in some years accounted for the largest share of government revenue.

In June, the European Commission wrote to the five Caribbean nations that currently operate CBI programmes: St Kitts and Nevis, Dominica, Antigua and Barbuda, Grenada and St Lucia. It warned that countries may lose visa-free access to its 29-country Schengen area if they did not phase out CBI.

In Friday’s statement, Drew blamed concerns over the programme on mismanagement by previous administrations, including years of underselling to undercut other countries, a controversial prison project, weak escrow protections and investment sums not being properly paid into the citizenship unit.

He said his government had led major reforms to the Caribbean’s programmes with international partners, including the US treasury, but argued that continuing the programme risked visa-free travel to parts of Europe and access to international banking. That could disrupt services allowing visitors to use overseas cards and families abroad to send money home.

Brantley, however, argued it was unfair for the EU to force St Kitts and Nevis to make a move without offering assistance to navigate the transition to other industries.

“I have not yet seen from the EU any corresponding suggestions of assistance. And we have to realise that these citizenship-by-investment programs, not only in St Kitts and Nevis, but … smaller countries of the Caribbean, have contributed significantly to our gross domestic product and also to ensuring that our people … can get adequate access to water and to education, to healthcare and the like,” he said.

Brantley described the 1 June 2028 deadline the EU gave the country to phase out the lucrative programme as “frankly ludicrous”.

In his statement, Drew said the CBI programme “did a lot of good, but it also created a danger”.

“When one source pays for half or more of everything, you are at its mercy. If it stops, everything stops. That is putting all of your eggs in one basket,” he added.

The prime minister said the government aimed to diversify its economy by strengthening tourism, yachting, and digital and creative industries, among others. He added that the sale of clean, geothermal energy was “at the heart of it” with drilling and exploration already planned.

“I will not pretend that the road is easy. Less CBI money means we must spend wisely and plan carefully. We will. Our plan is to make this change by growing our economy. Raising taxes is not part of that plan,” Drew said.

Antigua and Barbuda has also expressed willingness to end the programme, though it is trying to negotiate a longer transition period. The five Caribbean governments have taken differing positions on the EU demand. According to local media, Grenada has indicated it wants its programme to continue until 2032, while Dominica and St Lucia have responded to Brussels without publicly committing to ending their programmes.

Jermine Abel in Basseterre contributed reporting

#32. Hegseth uses Venezuela visit to boast of Maduro’s capture by US military

Defense secretary asserts US dominance on trip that would have seemed ‘impossible, almost unbelievable’ a year ago

Pete Hegseth has used his first visit to Venezuela since US special forces abducted its former president, Nicolás Maduro, to boast of the dictator’s capture during what he called “the single most sophisticated military operation in the history of Latin America”.

Maduro was controversially seized by Delta Force operatives in the early hours of 3 January, setting in motion an improbable rapprochement between Washington and Venezuela’s anti-imperialist “Chavista” regime, which has ruled since 1999.

Speaking outside the recently reopened US embassy in Venezuela’s capital, Caracas, on Wednesday, Hegseth said: “It’s a great honor to be here as the first United States secretary of war this century to set foot in Venezuela.​ Just one year ago my presence here … would have been deemed impossible, almost unimaginable.

“In fact, exactly one year ago today … [Maduro] stood on a stage not too far from here and yelled mockingly, ‘Come and get me!’” Hegseth recalled, before adding sarcastically: “Well, we answered that call.

“The reality is that … January 3 is the day that will go down in history as a day of liberation for Venezuela,” Hegseth claimed, although Maduro’s allies continue to hold power with US support.

Earlier in the day, Hegseth was welcomed to the Miraflores Palace by Maduro’s former vice-president, Delcy Rodríguez, who became acting president with Donald Trump’s blessing after his abduction.

In the aftermath of the deadly US attack, Hegseth was one of the bluntest Trump administration voices when it came to celebrating the invasion. “Maduro had his chance … he effed around and he found out,” the defense secretary bragged.

Speaking at the palace, Hegseth struck a more conciliatory tone as he vowed to deepen cooperation with Venezuela’s new leaders in brief comments that were broadcast by the state broadcaster VTV.

“I stand here on behalf of President Trump, who sends his greeting and looks forward to the opportunities, not just militarily … but also economically: the incredible resource capabilities that your country has, that we look forward to unlocking together with you,” Hegseth told Rodríguez and senior cabinet members, including the defense and foreign ministers, Gustavo González López and Oliver Blanco.

Hegseth said his visit to Rodríguez’s “beautiful country” was designed to re-establish and increase “defense cooperation” between “two great militaries” that less than a year ago were briefly at war. Earlier this month, the New York Times reported that Trump officials were trying to negotiate a long-term US troop presence in Venezuela as part of their push for regional hegemony.

“I appreciate your leadership and commitment to your country,” Hegseth told Maduro’s successor, a once outspoken anti-imperialist leftist who has overseen an unlikely realignment with Washington since her ally was grabbed.

During her five-minute address, Rodríguez, whose oil-rich country is widely seen to have become a US protectorate after January’s invasion, made no mention of Maduro, who is behind bars in New York City.

But referring indirectly to his capture and the subsequent reconciliation with Washington, she told Hegseth: “Thanks to the political and geopolitical maturity of our governments, we have – in a very short period of time, just a few months – turned what were very difficult and very tough times for our people, into an extraordinary space for cooperation and mutual benefit.”

Rodríguez hailed the “historic” energy deal her government recently struck with Trump, who has called it “the biggest oil deal in world history” as well as “the spoils” of his “conquest” of Venezuela.

Hegseth claimed the agreement, which reportedly involves 65bn barrels of reserves, “makes the strait of Hormuz a non-factor for the western hemisphere”.

Critics of Rodríguez’s political movement painted Hegseth’s visit as a humiliating act of genuflection for a president with no choice but to roll out the red carpet for the man whose troops captured her boss.

Writing on X, Luz Mely Reyes, a prominent Venezuelan journalist, said Hegseth’s visit was designed to underscore Washington’s “direct administration” of her country.

“Ten months after Nicolás Maduro’s capture, Washington no longer needs to fire shots to remind everyone who is in charge: the mere presence on Venezuelan soil of the architect of the January incursion reaffirms that the country’s operational sovereignty is subject to the White House’s directives,” Reyes said.

Reyes suspected the visit also carried a message for members of Venezuela’s armed forces. “Domestically, the trip forces the military high command to legitimize the man who directed the extraction of their former commander in chief,” she said. “In this regard, I wonder whether it might be a message of discipline intended to prevent any pockets of resistance in the barracks.”

Trump’s attempts to expand his Shield of the Americas anti-cartel coalition of Latin American governments suffered a major blow last month with the defeat of his far-right ally, Flávio Bolsonaro, in Brazil’s presidential election. The leftwing winner, Luiz Inácio Lula da Silva, has indicated his country will not join the initiative.

But Venezuela’s interim government is in little position to resist. During his surprise visit to Venezuela, Hegseth said Venezuela had gone from being “our most aggressive regional threat to [being] a compliant security partner”. He praised Rodríguez for working “closely, pragmatically, and diligently with the United States to stabilize Venezuela”. Hegseth claimed US and Venezuelan armed forces would work together to attack “narco-terrorists” operating on Venezuelan soil, vowing: “We will hunt you down. We will find you. And we will kill you.”

Concluding her speech at the palace, Rodríguez told her foe-turned-friend: “Thank you, secretary, and I wish you a lifetime of happiness.”

#31. Trump threatens ban on US firms building AI data centres overseas

Trump says he won't allow American companies to build data centres "outside" the US

Donald Trump has said he will ban US companies from building AI data centres overseas, threatening a "tremendous tax" for tech firms who do so.

"We're going to keep it all in this country," the US president said, while providing little detail on the potential policy he said others in his cabinet were hearing about for the first time.

Data centres were a central issue in the midterm elections earlier in November, with many Democratic candidates promising tougher regulation on their construction.

Tech giants including Meta and Google have entered agreements to build data centres overseas. It remains unclear how such a policy would be enforced if it was brought in.

"We're not going to allow our companies to build data centres outside of our country," Trump told reporters in a press briefing following his return from his trip to Asia.

"Because China will take all of them, and we're not allowing that to happen."

But whether that idea will become policy remains unclear.

"I don't know if that's a breaking story, but I haven't even said that to my people. But I'm telling them now, they're here," he said.

Trump floated the policy after saying the US was leading on data centre construction, and he would not allow China to beat the US when it came to AI - which he calls super intelligence, or SI.

It also follows an announcement earlier this week that would restrict the export of advanced computer chips and who can access the computing power provided by them.

Washington on Monday announced firms would require a licence to buy advanced chips, so US officials can monitor whether they are being used to serve Chinese companies or they are being smuggled into China.

The move will impact South East Asian countries that have invested heavily in data centres, as Chinese companies seek to rent computing power from overseas.

Why Canada is embracing AI data centres while the US pushes back

Data centres are crucial to the AI boom. The vast warehouse-style buildings house the advanced computer chips and servers that are needed to run AI programmes such as Claude and ChatGPT.

As of April there were about 3,000 data centres across the US, with another 1,500 in various stages of approval and construction, according to the Pew Research Centre.

Many of them are built in rural areas, and concerns about issues including noise pollution and energy consumption helped drive Democratic candidates promising tougher rules on data centre construction to wins on 3 November.

"You ought to take advantage of the data centres. Make them pay you, make them pay your community, but take advantage don't shut them off," Trump said.

"We have a lot of places that want them badly, and they're great places. They have energy and they have everything. They're tremendous assets."

Large tech firms - often called AI hyperscalers due to the size of their investments in AI - have also been branching out, and Canada has made moves to step in to offer itself as an alternative location for these sites, with less local pushback against projects so far.

Meta has already broken ground on a data centre, external in the province of Alberta, and Anthropic has signed a long-term agreement to lease a proposed data centre in Nova Scotia.

The BBC has reached out to Meta for comment on Trump's remarks.

In September Google announced a â¬13bn ($15bn: Â£11bn) investment in AI infrastructure in Finland, which included funding for three data centres.

Last month, building work for two of those centres was halted due to concerns over the clearance of more than 300 hectares of forest.

Trump called out the build in Finland specifically, saying his administration was "not allowing that to happen".

#28. Florida pastor found guilty over 2021 assassination of Haitian president

Christian Emmanuel Sanon faces life in prison after conviction on charges arising from killing of Jovenel Moïse

A Florida pastor with dual Haitian-American citizenship was convicted on Wednesday of US charges arising from the 2021 assassination of the Haitian president Jovenel Moïse.

Christian Emmanuel Sanon was found guilty on charges including conspiring to kill Moïse after a nearly eight-week trial in federal court in Miami. He had faced a total of eight charges including conspiracy to kidnap or kill a person outside the United States.

He faces up to life in prison at sentencing.

Federal prosecutors said south Florida was a hub for planning and financing the plot to oust Moïse and install a president of the conspirators’ choosing. The government said Sanon was the conspirators’ initial choice for Moïse’s replacement. Prosecutors also said Sanon helped raise money for the conspiracy and arranged travel to Haiti and lodging for himself and others in that country.

Sanon acknowledged in trial testimony that he was in communication at one point with people trying to replace Moïse, but pointed out that the group had moved on to another candidate long before the assassination. He denied any knowledge of plans to kill Moïse.

Sanon was born in Haiti and moved to the US as a teenager. He testified that he worked with churches in Haitian immigrant communities in Florida and the midwest.

Haiti has not had a president since authorities said that a squad of mostly retired Colombian soldiers killed Moïse and wounded his wife in a night-time raid at their home on 7 July 2021. The poorest country in the Americas, Haiti has a history of dictatorship, political upheaval and foreign intervention that has long hindered democratic rule.

The election of a new president is scheduled for next year.

Prosecutors said the scheme to kill Moïse included paying about two dozen foreign mercenaries with money obtained from US government programs that were intended to help businesses during the Covid pandemic, according to court records. US prosecutors have charged more than 40 people with crimes related to the assassination and a dozen have been convicted, including five who are serving life sentences.

The plot to oust Moïse began taking final shape in April 2021, when Sanon met in south Florida with a former Haitian senator and several US businesspeople, according to an indictment. The group agreed that Sanon, once in power, would award contracts to two Florida-based companies – Counter Terrorist Unit Federal Academy and Counter Terrorist Unit Security, collectively known as CTU – for infrastructure projects, security forces and military equipment, according to investigators.

CTU initially hired about 18 Colombian nationals with military training to provide security for Sanon, according to prosecutors. Conspirators also spent months obtaining weapons and body armor and attempting to build ties with Haitian gangs, officials said.

By June 2021, the conspirators realized Sanon had neither the constitutional qualifications nor sufficient popular support to become president. They shifted their support to Wendelle Coq Thélot, a former Haitian supreme court judge who died in January 2025 while a fugitive.

Sanon was previously scheduled to be tried with four other men who were convicted earlier this year, but his case was separated from theirs and delayed for health reasons.

#27. Denmark accuses Russia of plotting to assassinate US-Ukrainian rapper

Denmark has accused Russian intelligence services of plotting to assassinate a Ukrainian-American near Copenhagen, saying they had "planned" and "initiated" the attempt.

Denmark's security and intelligence agency said it had stopped individuals who were intending to kill the person, named by Danish media as rapper Kyivstoner, at a concert in September. Two people were charged with attempted murder and assisting a foreign intelligence service, and pleaded not guilty.

Denmark's foreign minister said Russia's defence attachÃ© will now be expelled.

Russia's Embassy in Denmark called the allegations "unsubstantiated" and said Copenhagen failed to provide evidence of Moscow's involvement.

In a statement posted on social media, the embassy claimed a "Danish model" had emerged, which it described as a "Russian trace" allegedly being discovered and "exploited for a propaganda spectacle". It accused Denmark of "provocations" intending to "frighten the Danish population in order to justify... a prolonged confrontation with Russia".

Russian state media cites the foreign ministry in Moscow as saying it will respond to the expulsion of its official "accordingly".

Danish media named the target of the assassination attempt as the rapper known as Kyivstoner, whose real name is Albert Vasiliev.

Denmark's security and intelligence agency (PET) said the individuals who allegedly tried to assassinate him were associated with a banned Danish gang named Loyal to Familia. The group is "known for extensive, serious and violent criminal activity", according to the agency.

PET, which worked with police on the case, said three individuals were stopped while travelling by car near the music venue where Kyivstoner was set to perform on 25 September.

Firearms and knives were found in the vehicle, PET said.

Since then, two people linked to the case have been charged with attempted murder and assisting a foreign intelligence service.

Both individuals pleaded not guilty to the charges, local media reported.

In July, Russia's FSB security service accused Vasiliev and the Ukrainian authorities of organising a "terrorist attack" on military facilities and personnel in Russia. The rapper denied this allegation on social media.

In a statement on Friday, Danish Foreign Minister Lars LÃ¸kke Rasmussen said the alleged assassination attempt was Russian "intimidation" and an attempt to make Denmark "waver" in its support for Ukraine.

Russia's defence attachÃ© must leave the country within seven days, Rasmussen said.

He also said Denmark wants the EU to restrict the ability of Russians to travel throughout Europe, saying even though Denmark is already very reluctant to issue visas, Russians can still get a Schenegen visa in another country and travel to Denmark. Denmark wants these visas to apply only in the country that has issued them, he added.

#25. Race to be next UN secretary general exposes divisions among world powers

Debate about dominance of permanent five members could reignite if major powers block Costa Rican frontrunner

The race to become the next UN secretary general could reach a decisive moment on Friday when a UN security council vote is likely to reveal whether the Costa Rican frontrunner for the post would be blocked by the big powers. If so, the setback will reignite a debate about the dominance of the permanent five (P5) members at the United Nations.

Rebeca Grynspan, an economist and UN insider who is from a family of Polish-Jewish wartime refugees to Costa Rica, has been in the lead in all but one of the eight straw polls of the 15 UN security council members since the first secret ballot was held on 30 July. Each member goes down the list of candidates, casting an “encourage”, “discourage” or “no opinion” vote for each candidate.

Despite countries not being required to reveal how they voted, it is thought Grynspan’s candidature has been resisted by Russia and the US, two of the five permanent security council members that can ultimately wield a veto against a candidate. Knowledge among the security council delegations of how countries are voting is down to private intelligence, leaks and a process of elimination.

The intensive lobbying over the job has spread across the globe, but Friday is especially important since the poll is slated to differentiate the voting of the P5 from the 10 elected members (E10) of the security council. To be recommended as secretary general to the UN general assembly, a candidate needs nine votes and no vetoes. In Friday’s colour-coded secret ballot, the P5 will cast red ballots and the E10 white.

One diplomat said: “The electoral system means it is important how many votes you win, but equally how many of certain votes you don’t lose.”

The protracted 140-day deadlock confirms the polarisation that has hampered the incumbent secretary general, António Guterres, but also belies claims the role is now an irrelevance.

Friday’s secret ballot may also reveal whether the United Arab Emirates’ campaign for its diplomat, Lana Nusseibeh, is about to blow up in its face due to the hostility to the UAE’s brand of active, commercially driven diplomacy, not just in Sudan, but also Somalia, Western Sahara, Libya and Yemen.

A late entrant, in the two straw polls in which she has contested so far, Nusseibeh secured only seven “encourage” votes, six “discourage” and two “no opinions”. This is despite prestigious big-power backing and her acknowledged personal diplomatic skills.

Nusseibeh has US and Russian backing. The French foreign ministry may have misgivings about the UAE, but Emmanuel Macron, the French president, travelled to the UAE in the midst of French riots to see the UAE president, Sheikh Mohamed bin Zayed Al Nahyan, and secure 100,000 metric tons of diesel a month.

In the UK, the foreign secretary, Ed Miliband, has kept his counsel, but he shares a Polish-Jewish refugee heritage with Grynspan and cannot be an admirer of the UAE’s human rights record or its evasion of EU sanctions on Russia. The prime minister, Andy Burnham, has UAE connections from his time as mayor of Manchester, and Nusseibeh is a fellow alumnus of the University of Cambridge. At the UN she worked closely with the UK in an informal group called “Friends of the Future of the UN”.

Yet 68 British MPs, including the Labour chair of the international development select committee, Sarah Champion, last week signed a letter calling on Burnham to veto Nusseibeh, describing her as “a tainted and compromised figure”.

Some of the opposition to her from elected security council members is not wholly political. Latin American countries believe an informal rotation system means it is their turn to lead the UN. As a result Panama and Colombia, currently on the security council, will oppose her. China has also been supportive of this rotation principle.

But others deeply object to what they consider to be the UAE’s dollar diplomacy and preparedness to use its extraordinary wealth to exacerbate and exploit pre-existing divisions in a country to gain a commercial foothold. Somalia, for instance, has a seat on the Security Council, and its president, Hassan Sheikh Mohamud, this week went on TV to accuse the UAE of buying ports, establishing military bases and supporting armed forces outside Somalia’s national security framework without federal approval.

It is also possible that Finland and Latvia, the two elected members with the greatest stake in Ukraine, are unimpressed that the UAE gave Vladimir Putin red carpet treatment when he visited Abu Dhabi in December 2023, nine months after the international criminal court had issued an arrest warrant for child trafficking. Greece, less engaged in Ukraine, may be more flexible.

Since the last poll, Nusseibeh has been to Bahrain, Britain and Pakistan, a clue as to where extra votes may be harvested. She also had a “thoughtful exchange” with Zeng Jixin, the Chinese ambassador to the UAE.

The two hurdles are whether someone so deep inside the UAE inner circle would have the courage in office to show true independence, and whether her active denials of UAE involvement in arming the RSF in Sudan leave her irretrievably tainted. From a Palestinian intellectual family, she insists she would make peace in Sudan her number one priority.

In the last ballot on 5 November, Grynspan won 12 votes in favour and three negative. But on 16 October – the only colour-coded ballot so far – which distinguishes between permanent and elected members – she received two discourage votes from permanent members, the equivalent of a veto. Nusseibeh had not joined the race at that point.

In an administration as personalised as the US, it has been hard for Grynspan to gain influence, with many sensing that Mike Waltz, the US ambassador to the UN, is the key influencer.

Grynspan has been reluctant to make the fact she is Jewish a feature of her candidacy, but the election of a Jewish person to run the UN might be seen as something the US could embrace as transformative. Her age – she is 70 – could be considered a drawback among those calling for a younger generation to renew the institution.

Although there are other Latin American candidates, Grynspan is seen as a more viable candidate than her rival, the Argentine Rafael Grossi, the head of the UN weapons inspectorate. Russia has opposed Grossi, objecting to his interventions over the Zaporizhzhia nuclear plant in Ukraine.

One way or the other, the logjam has to be broken soon.

#24. Mamdani announces plan to close New York's Rikers Island jail by 2032

Perched on a 413 acre (167 hectare) island between Queens and the Bronx, the notorious Rikers Island jail complex earned a variety of nicknames over the years

New York City's official overseeing the closure of the city's most notorious jail hopes a new plan from Mayor Zohran Mamdani will lead to "a moment of transformation" for how people are detained in the largest US city.

Mamdani on Friday unveiled a plan to close the Rikers Island complex by 2032, to build four new jails and drastically reduce the city's detainee population.

Plans to shutter the troubled facility date back to 2019, when the city council first voted to close it by this year. But plans have been delayed over the years in part due to the logistical difficulties over where to place inmates.

Dana Kaplan - who Mamdani appointed as the city's "Close Rikers Czar" told the BBC News channel on Friday that his latest proposal is not just about getting rid of a decrepit facility.

It is also a "generational opportunity" to "build a different type of detention system" and figure out how to detain people more appropriately, she said.

The Rikers Island jail complex is perched on a 413 acre (167 hectare) island between Queens and the Bronx, and has earned a variety of nicknames over the years because of its dilapidated conditions, including Gladiator School and Torture Island. The jail has housed a number of high-profile inmates awaiting trial, including Tupac Shakur, DMX and Harvey Weinstein.

It houses more than 6,500 inmates at a given time. Mamdani hopes to reduce that number to 4,100 - a goal that has been met with skepticism by the New York City correction officers' union.

"[B]uilding a new system that puts an arbitrary cap on the City's available jail beds is ill-conceived, irresponsible and jeopardizes the safety of all New Yorkers, including our Officers," NYC Correction Officers' Benevolent Association said in a statement. "The City cannot keep the jail population at around 4,100 without irresponsibly releasing individuals who committed violent felony charges."

"It's actually possible to have fewer people in jail in New York City - and that is what we should be planning for," she said.

The jail complex has long faced backlash over staffing shortages, crumbling conditions and inmate deaths in the facility, including nine this year. Legislation required the jail to be shuttered by 2027, but Mamdani blamed lags under former Mayor Eric Adams' administration as well as the Covid pandemic for the delays.

Last year, under Adams, a federal judge removed Rikers from the city's control, appointing an independent outside official to make decisions concerning the facility.

In an 88-page report released on Friday, Mamdani said Rikers was a product of "deteriorating infrastructure" and that officials had a "moral imperative" to close it.

New York City Council must approve the updated timeline, with members, including the city's speaker, indicating they support the plan.

Mamdani's office said the city would open up four new jails - one in Brooklyn by 2029, two in the Bronx and Queens by 2031 and one in Manhattan by 2032.

The mayor's office argues the new, smaller facilities will provide inmates "access to daylight, fresh air and clean space" leading to better outcomes for detainees. The facilities will be easier for families to access as well, as travel to the remote Rikers can take hours.

Mamdani said his administration would work to reduce the number of people being held in jail across New York City, including by offering alternative programmes to detention for some awaiting trial, by making it easier for families to post bail online and by trying to speed up the pretrial legal process.

He also wants to open up hundreds of beds in therapeutic housing units for prisoners with serious mental health issues.

Kaplan said Mamdani's administration had already opened 100 therapeutic beds at Bellevue Hospital in Manhattan, moving 100 people off of Rikers Island.

The proposal involves a large investments: $146m (Â£111m) for mental health and substance use treatment and vocational and education programming inside and outside the jails.

#22. Gérard Depardieu and French author investigated over sexual allegations

Michel Houellebecq (left) and (right) GÃ©rard Depardieu have been accused of an on-set incident

Warning: this story contains graphic content

French authorities are investigating disgraced actor GÃ©rard Depardieu and the author Michel Houellebecq over "allegations of a sexual nature", a source with knowledge of the investigation says.

The confirmation comes after reports in French media that the two were accused of being involved in a "gang rape" on the set of the film Thalasso in 2018 by an actress.

The source told the BBC that a judicial investigation was launched after a complaint was filed in May 2025.

Depardieu has already separately been convicted of unrelated sexual assaults. The BBC has contacted Depardieu's lawyers and Houellebecq, an acclaimed French author and occasional actor, for comment.

The actress, who has not been named publicly, accused Depardieu of penetration without consent and Houellebecq of forcing her to perform oral sex, in an interview with Mediapart, a French investigative site which first reported the allegations.

She told the outlet that none of this had been written into the script, and that she experienced "profound humiliation" and "unspeakable shame".

French law stipulates as of 2025 that all sexual acts performed without consent constitute rape.

The film's director, Guillaume Nicloux, has been accused of being complicit because he was said to have filmed the incident, Mediapart and AFP report. The BBC has contacted his agent and the Paris prosecutor's office for comment.

Nicloux told Mediapart that the scene had been improvised and he had not intervened as he had not observed "any constraints, verbal or physical opposition, or even discomfort".

The site published production documents showing footage of the scene had been destroyed.

The actress told the outlet that she had told friends, her agent and others involved in the film's production in Normandy about the incident at the time, but only filed the complaint after other women accused Depardieu of sexual misconduct.

The Asterix and Obelix star will next week find out whether he will stand trial for allegedly raping another actress, Charlotte Arnould.

On Monday, Depardieu dropped his appeal against a separate conviction for two counts of sexual assault during another film shoot, for which he was handed an 18-month suspended sentence.

Several other women have made allegations against the 77-year-old in the media.

#20. In a year of AI solving maths problems, Anthropic may have just solved the biggest

Anthropic just went public with a major IPO this week

An Anthropic employee and mathematician claims to have used the company's artificial intelligence (AI) chatbot to prove the famed Riemann hypothesis.

Levent AlpÃ¶ge said, external on Friday, a day after Anthropic made its US stock market debut, that over the course of about six weeks, he used the Claude chatbot to create a detailed proof of the maths problem.

If found to be correct, the proof would be among the most significant in history. AlpÃ¶ge described it as "one dawn across science of something far greater".

The claim comes as AI firms like OpenAI have in recent months announced scores of solutions to major higher maths problems that have eluded scholars for decades.

As for the Riemann hypothesis proof put forward on Friday, it was found using a non-public version of Anthropic's Claude.

It is a nearly 170-year-old maths problem that deals with prime numbers and is widely considered to be the single most famous problem in maths, in large part due to how complex it is.

Named for the mathematician Bernhard Riemann, the hypothesis puts forward that prime numbers have a determined order along the number line.

Prime numbers are the bedrock of higher maths, leaving breakthroughs related to them to have broad impact.

AlpÃ¶ge said that he messaged Anthropic CEO Dario Amodei on Sunday, asking him directly about how he could announce the discovery.

"The precise timing is horrible," AlpÃ¶ge said, referring to Anthropic's public stock listing. "If I could have, I'd have finished this all much earlier."

"I am clearly unilaterally responsible for feeling I just could not live if I waited even longer. There is an obvious cynical view, to do with the timing, but it is false," he added.

The complete textual proof that AlpÃ¶ge put forward in Lean, a computer language used for confirming maths proofs, is over 250,000 pages long.

Terrance Tao, a prominent mathematician, said on Friday, external that verification of the proof is still some way off.

"I imagine the digestion process will take some time, though in this case at least we have a human author to communicate with to assist with this task," Tao said.

No such human was obviously a part of major maths proofs made earlier this year using AI.

OpenAI in September claimed that employees using a non-public version of its AI tools were able to solve part of a notoriously difficult problem in just 88 hours. The problem was part of the Navier-Stokes equations, which concern how fluids move.

More than 10,000 AI agents, or computer programmed bots that operate somewhat autonomously, were put to the task as an AI training exercise.

The proof that OpenAI released on Navier-Stokes has yet to be confirmed as accurate, and it set off criticism within academic and higher maths communities and organisations about the use of AI to solve maths problems.

Anthropic has also claimed to have made other progress and solutions in maths, external and physics, external.

A group of maths scholars said, external AI companies boasting of major solutions to show how sophisticated their commercial products had become was "detrimental to the science of mathematics".

"We are witnessing a general threat to intellectual work, with misalignment between the outcome of the use of AI and its initial purpose," the scholars said in a group letter.

A month later, OpenAI released, external what it claimed were solutions, proofs, or major progress on 372 more outstanding higher maths problems.

It will take months, if not longer, for those claims to be reviewed by experts to prove their validity.
